# PREFACE

<margin>IX</margin>

The <term>Monadology</term> <note>Derived from the Greek word "monas," meaning unit; Leibniz’s theory that the universe is made of infinite, simple, soul-like substances called "monads."</note> of Leibniz in an English form has long been available and familiar to students of philosophy in the excellent translations of Professor R. Latta and Professor G. M. Duncan. There is no difficulty in understanding it so far as the diction is concerned; it is the thought which is difficult. It requires interpreting, not only in relation to the scientific concepts of an age when mathematics and mechanics were highly advanced, and physics, chemistry and biology still in their infancy, if indeed they could be said to have come to birth, but also in relation to contemporary science and philosophy.

The *Monadology* is pure <term>metaphysics</term> <note>The branch of philosophy that deals with the first principles of things, including abstract concepts such as being, knowing, substance, and cause.</note>. It belongs not to any particular time but to all time. Yet it enjoys a peculiar privilege in the history and development of modern philosophy by reason of the profound influence it has had in determining the direction of later thought, notably that of Kant and Hegel, and it cannot be neglected by anyone who is seeking to participate in the philosophical thinking of the present time.

The theory of Monads is only a small part of the intellectual work of Leibniz. For an account of his whole life and philosophy I may refer the reader to my *Leibniz* (Ernest Benn, London ; Little Brown, Boston ; 1929).

The *Monadology* was not written for publication. Leibniz, when he wrote it, had published only one work of philosophy in book form, *The Theodicy* <note>original: "La Théodicée." A title derived from the Greek words for "God" and "justice," referring to Leibniz's attempt to justify the existence of God in a world containing evil.</note>, and the references in the *Monadology* are almost exclusively to this work.

<summary>This preface introduces Leibniz’s "Monadology," framing it as a timeless work of metaphysics that influenced major thinkers like Kant and Hegel despite being rooted in the early scientific context of the 18th century.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, Monadology, metaphysics, Theodicy, philosophy history, Kant, Hegel, scientific history</keywords>

# THE STORY OF THE MONADOLOGY

The work of <term>Leibniz</term> <note>Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716) was a brilliant German philosopher and mathematician who co-invented calculus.</note> which we name the *Monadology* is a short treatise in French found among his papers without any title. It was written in 1714 about two years before his death. It purports to set forth in ninety short and carefully connected paragraphs a complete and succinct account of the philosophy which he was accustomed to describe as his "system." Another similar work, also found among his papers, bearing the title <term>*Principles of Nature and Grace founded on reason*</term> <note>original: "Principes de la Nature et de la Grace fondés en raison"</note>, consisting of eighteen paragraphs, is written with the same purpose of summarizing the philosophy. It seems probable that this was an earlier attempt, set aside as unsatisfactory, and afterwards worked up into <term>*The Monadology*</term> <note>original: "La Monadologie"</note>. We know from the correspondence that Prince <term>Eugene of Savoy</term> <note>A famous military commander and patron of the arts who lived in Vienna.</note>, a student and admirer of the philosopher, having read Leibniz's <term>*The Theodicy*</term> <note>original: "La Théodicée"; Leibniz's major work exploring how a good God allows evil to exist in the world.</note>, published in 1710, requested him to write a condensed statement of his philosophical principles, and Leibniz did so and sent it to the Prince. There is some doubt which of the two, the *Monadology* or the *Principles of Nature and Grace*

was forwarded to Prince Eugene, but the probability is greatly in favour of the former, if only because it is the more complete.

A German translation of the *Monadology* was published in 1720, and a Latin translation of this German translation was included by <term>Dutens</term> <note>Louis Dutens (1730–1812) was a French writer and clergyman who edited the first comprehensive collection of Leibniz's works.</note> in his collected edition of <term>*The Works of Leibniz*</term> <note>original: "Leibnitii Opera"</note> in 1768, under the title <term>*Principles of philosophy, or Theses in favor of Prince Eugene*</term> <note>original: "Principia philosophiæ, seu Theses in gratiam Principis Eugenii"</note>. The original French work was first published in <term>Erdmann’s</term> <note>Johann Eduard Erdmann (1805–1892) was a German historian of philosophy whose 1840 edition finally brought the original French text to light.</note> edition of Leibniz’s Philosophical works, in 1840.

# THE STORY OF THE MONADOLOGY

The work of <term>Leibniz</term> <note>Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716) was a polymath and philosopher who developed the theory of monads—indivisible units of existence.</note> which we name the *Monadology* is a short treatise in French found among his papers without any title. It was written in 1714, about two years before his death. It purports to set forth in ninety short and carefully connected paragraphs a complete and succinct account of the philosophy which he was accustomed to describe as his "system." Another similar work, also found among his papers, bearing the title <term>*Principles of Nature and Grace founded on reason*</term> <note>original: "Principes de la Nature et de la Grace fondés en raison"</note>, consisting of eighteen paragraphs, is written with the same purpose of summarizing the philosophy. It seems probable that this was an earlier attempt, set aside as unsatisfactory, and afterwards worked up into <term>*The Monadology*</term> <note>original: "La Monadologie"</note>. We know from the correspondence that Prince <term>Eugene of Savoy</term> <note>A prominent military commander and patron of science and art in the Habsburg Empire.</note>, a student and admirer of the philosopher, having read Leibniz's <term>*The Theodicy*</term> <note>original: "La Théodicée"; Leibniz's work defending God's justice in the face of suffering.</note>, published in 1710, requested him to write a condensed statement of his philosophical principles, and Leibniz did so and sent it to the Prince. There is some doubt which of the two, the *Monadology* or the *Principles of Nature and Grace*

<summary>This page recounts the origin of Leibniz's "Monadology," written late in his life as a concise summary of his philosophy for Prince Eugene of Savoy, and traces its publication history from early translations to its 1840 rediscovery.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, Monadology, Prince Eugene of Savoy, Theodicy, philosophy, publication history, Louis Dutens, Johann Erdmann</keywords>

II

## THE RELATION OF THE MONADOLOGY TO THE THEODICY

The <term>Monadology</term> <note>A work composed of 90 aphorisms that summarizes Leibniz's philosophy of "monads"—the fundamental, indivisible units of reality.</note> is a purely metaphysical treatise in the special meaning which Leibniz attaches to the term metaphysics, namely, a science of pure reason. In Leibniz’s view there is a faculty of **pure and naked reason** <note>original: "raison pure et nue"</note> and by it we are enabled to know reality as it is in itself, reality independent of sensible experience and independent therefore of the religious belief or faith which depends on the evidence of sensible experience, such as miracles, the fulfilment of prophecy, and, generally, what goes by the name of Christian evidence. The principle which guides and supports this pure reason is the logical **law of non-contradiction** <note>The logical rule stating that something cannot be both true and false at the same time.</note>, and the truth it attains is necessary truth, the denial of which involves absurdity. In sensible experience on the other hand we are always dealing with **contingent truth** <note>Facts that are true but could have been otherwise; for example, the fact that it is raining is a contingent truth, whereas 1+1=2 is a necessary truth.</note>. Any fact of experience may be affirmed or denied without involving self-contradiction. The principle which guides experience in the search for truth is the **law of sufficient reason** <note>The principle that nothing happens without a reason why it should be so and not otherwise.</note>. The *Monadology* is designed to give form and content to a science of pure reason.

Leibniz was a great controversialist. Controversy was a necessity of the age in which he lived, for the

<page-num>5</page-num>

<summary>This page defines the Monadology as a work of pure reason, distinguishing between necessary truths governed by non-contradiction and contingent truths governed by the law of sufficient reason. It positions Leibniz's metaphysics as a science independent of sensory experience or religious faith.</summary>
<keywords>Monadology, Theodicy, Leibniz, metaphysics, pure reason, law of non-contradiction, contingent truth, law of sufficient reason</keywords>

--- Page 20 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Leibniz’s philosophical system, specifically how his work moved from theological debate toward a rigorous, universal science. It follows the discussion of the "Principles of Nature and Grace" and the logical foundations of his thought.</meta>

->THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ<-

dominant intellectual interest was theology, and theologians were grouped into opposite camps. Of his two great philosophical works, the one, published in his lifetime, *The Theodicy* <note>original: "La Théodicée"</note>, was mainly taken up with controverting Pierre Bayle <note>A famous French philosopher and skeptic whose "Dictionary" pointed out contradictions between reason and religious faith.</note>, the famous author of the *Historical and Critical Dictionary*, and the other, *New Essays* <note>original: "Nouveaux Essais"</note>, not published till 1765, fifty years after his death, was a criticism of Locke’s <note>John Locke, the English philosopher who argued that the mind begins as a "blank slate" and gathers knowledge through experience.</note> *Essay on the Understanding*. Yet in these, as in his early correspondence with Arnauld and his later correspondence with Clarke <note>Antoine Arnauld and Samuel Clarke were major intellectual figures of the day with whom Leibniz engaged in deep debates about logic, grace, and the nature of space and time.</note>, he has always the ideal of a science beyond controversy, of a possibility of stating eternal truth in unambiguous words, the meanings of which once understood must command universal assent. The Monadology is the attempt to realize such a science.

The full title of *The Theodicy* <note>original: "La Théodicée"</note> is *Essays on Theodicy, on the Goodness of God, the Freedom of Man, and the Origin of Evil* <note>original: "Essais de Théodicée, sur la Bonté de Dieu, la Liberté de l’Homme, et l’Origine du Mal"</note>. To the uninitiated reader it might appear to be an ordinary attempt to reconcile the familiar difficulties involved in the Christian conception of God and the world, and the doctrine of man’s fall and redemption. Had we not possessed the Monadology it is probable that, with the passing of the old form of theological controversy and the advent of the scientific interest, the Theodicy would have fallen into complete oblivion. The Monad-

<summary>This page explains that Leibniz’s primary goal was to create a universal science of truth that could resolve the theological and philosophical conflicts of his time. It highlights his major works, the Theodicy and the New Essays, as steps toward the definitive system presented in the Monadology.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, Monadology, Theodicy, Pierre Bayle, John Locke, theology, universal science, religious controversy</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the introduction to Leibniz’s philosophical system, specifically how his work bridge the gap between religious faith and scientific reason. It completes the word "theology," which was split across the page break from page 6.</meta>

->THE MONADOLOGY AND THE THEODICY<-

7
B

theology enables us to see that it is only in outward form that the argument is theological and that its real strength lies in its appeal to a purely rational conception, which is as pertinent to the modern scientific aspect of the universe as it was to the pre-scientific animistic <note>Animism is the belief that all things in the universe—including plants and objects—possess a soul or spiritual essence.</note> aspect.

Leibniz argues with conviction and confidence that every apparent discrepancy between reason and revelation <note>Truths revealed by God, such as those found in the Bible, rather than through human logic.</note>, or, as it presented itself to philosophers in the seventeenth century, between the principles of Nature and the principles of Grace <note>In Leibniz’s work, "Nature" refers to the physical laws of the universe, while "Grace" refers to the moral world of the soul and God's justice.</note>, is capable of a rational interpretation. In his view there is a perfect conformity of reason with the things which concern the faith. In this Leibniz stands alone. The way of coexistence <note>original: "modus vivendi"</note> which philosophers in his time almost universally proposed and followed was a separation of the two realms, and theologians also met the difficulties of logical and experimental science by appealing from reason to faith. 

> “Nay but, O Man, who art thou that repliest against God? Shall the thing formed say unto him that formed it, ‘Why hast thou made me thus?’” <note>This is a quotation from the Bible (Romans 9:20), which was often used to argue that human reason has no right to question divine creation.</note>

The Christian religion was the revelation of a mystery. To rationalize it was impious intrusion. Bayle <note>Pierre Bayle (1647–1706) was a prominent French philosopher and skeptic who argued that faith and reason were fundamentally at odds.</note>, for example, who is the chief controversialist quoted at length in *The Theodicy* <note>original: "la Théodicée"</note>, found it impossible to reconcile the omnipotence <note>The quality of being all-powerful.</note> of God with the liberty of man, and the omniscience <note>The quality of being all-knowing.</note> and goodness

<summary>This page discusses Leibniz's unique position that religious faith and scientific reason are perfectly compatible, contrary to the prevailing 17th-century view that they must be kept separate. He argues that the apparent conflicts between the laws of nature and the laws of grace can be resolved through rational thought.</summary>

of God <note>This likely completes a sentence from the previous page such as "reconciling the justice of God."</note> with the existence of evil (imperfection, pain, and sin) yet he was not on that account disposed to lower the claims of reason on the one hand, or exalt the virtues of faith on the other. He found refuge as did most others in the reflexion on the disparity between the infinite and the finite. God's ways are not our ways. It is impossible we should see things from God's standpoint. Were it possible so to view reality it is certain all difficulties would disappear. This insidious form of scepticism <note>Leibniz is critiquing the idea that because we are small, we cannot trust our own logic to understand the universe.</note> was the one which Leibniz strove perseveringly to expose and to meet. 

Man, however handicapped by his finitude and by his vicissitude <note>The tendency for human circumstances to change or decline over time.</note>, is a rational creature. In possessing reason he possesses the absolute, for reason is one and the same, whether it is exercised by God or by the meanest of created things. Reason in the creature may be clouded or obscured, but in itself, *pure and naked* <note>original: "pure et nue"</note>, it is eternal and absolute.

<summary>Leibniz argues that human reason is not a lesser version of divine logic but is identical to it in essence, allowing humans to potentially understand the harmony of the universe despite the presence of evil.</summary>

### III

## BAYLE’S DICTIONARY AND THE ARTICLE RORARIUS

Pierre Bayle (1647-1706) is known chiefly as the author of the **Dictionary** <note>The *Historical and Critical Dictionary*, first published in 1697, was a massive work of scholarship that used biographical entries as a springboard for skeptical philosophical arguments.</note> which bears his name. In many striking respects he resembles Leibniz. Both writers were adherents of the **reformed religion** <note>This refers to the Protestant tradition, specifically those following the teachings of John Calvin.</note>, and both were especially interested in and deeply concerned with the particular points at issue between the **Catholic Church** and the **Reformers**, and keenly anxious to discover a *middle path* <note>original: "via media"; a theological or political middle ground aimed at finding a compromise between opposing parties.</note> which might effect a reconciliation. 

Bayle was the son of a minister of the **Reformed Church** and to the distress of his family had been converted in his student days to **Catholicism**. The conversion had endured however only seventeen months and he had then returned to the communion of the **Reformed Church**. He was particularly obnoxious to the **Catholics** on this account <note>In the 17th century, "relapsed" converts were often subject to severe legal penalties and social ostracization.</note>. In 1675 he was appointed Professor of Philosophy in the **Protestant Academy of Sedan**, but in 1681 the Academy was suppressed by **Louis XIV**, and he avoided prosecution by removing to Holland, where later he accepted an appointment as Professor in the **University of Rotterdam**. His *Historical and Critical Dictionary* is not a dictionary in the

<summary>This page introduces Pierre Bayle, a significant Protestant thinker and contemporary of Leibniz, highlighting his controversial religious conversions and his eventual exile to Holland.</summary>

It consists of articles on celebrated men of all ages in alphabetical order, but to all intents and purposes the articles are merely a convenience of the author, enabling him to express his own opinions under the guise of criticizing those of his contemporaries. According to one of his eulogists <note>Likely referring to Pierre Des Maizeaux, whose biography of Bayle helped cement his posthumous reputation.</note> he knew quite well that he was not a philosopher of the first rank, who could impress his readers by the novelty and originality of his doctrine, and therefore he devised this unusual mode of expression to compel attention and secure an audience. Voltaire has described him in his caustic manner: "An admirable dialectician rather than a profound philosopher, he knew almost nothing of physics. He was ignorant of the discoveries of the great Newton, and almost all of his philosophical articles assume or combat a Cartesianism that no longer exists." <note>original: "Dialecticien admirable plus que profond philosophe il ne savait presque rien en physique. Il ignorait les découvertes du grand Newton, et presque tous ses articles philosophiques supposent ou combattent un Cartésianisme qui ne subsiste plus."</note> Voltaire in this shows his own prejudice for at the time Bayle was writing **Cartesianism** <term>Cartesianism: The philosophy of René Descartes, which dominated 17th-century thought by prioritizing rationalism and a mechanistic view of nature.</term> was the prevalent philosophy, the only philosophy then generally recognized as modern in contrast with **scholasticism** <term>Scholasticism: The traditional school of philosophy taught in medieval universities, based on a synthesis of Aristotelian logic and Christian theology.</term>. Bayle wittily compared himself to Homer’s **cloud-compelling Zeus** <note>Referring to the Homeric epithet *Nephelēgereta*; here it implies a thinker who stirs up the "clouds" of intellectual doubt rather than clearing them away.</note>. "My talent" he said "lies in formulating doubts, but they are only doubts." His works are voluminous and are all controversial. The **Dictionary**, which was successful and celebrated, was first published in 1696 and an enlarged

Bayle’s article “Rorarius”, which is referred to and replied to in the *Monadology* <note>The *Monadology* (1714) is one of Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz's final and most important works, summarizing his philosophy of substance.</note>, derives its whole interest from its application to the doctrine of Leibniz. **Jerome Rorarius** <note>Girolamo Rorario (1485–1556) was an Italian diplomat whose work "That Animals Use Reason Better than Men" became a focal point for debates on animal consciousness.</note>, a native of Pordenone, in Italy, was a papal nuncio <term>Papal nuncio: A high-ranking diplomatic representative of the Holy See (the Pope) to a foreign court or government.</term> at the Hungarian Court in the sixteenth century. He was the author of a book which propounded the opinion that the **brutes** <note>In 17th and 18th-century philosophy, "brutes" was the standard term for non-human animals.</note> have a rational soul and even make better use of reason than does man. In support of this the book describes in detail a number of curious facts which illustrate the industry of brutes in comparison with the malice of man. 

These facts are embarrassing, Bayle tells us, alike to the **Cartesians** and to the **Aristotelians**, the two opposing factions. The Cartesians <term>Cartesians: Followers of René Descartes who famously argued that animals were "automata" or biological machines without souls, feelings, or consciousness.</term> deny that brutes have a soul, while the Aristotelians <term>Aristotelians: Those adhering to the traditional school of philosophy based on Aristotle, which dominated universities for centuries.</term> hold that although the brutes have feelings, memory and passions, yet they are not endowed with reason. The difficulty in the view that brutes have only a **sensitive soul** <term>Sensitive soul: A concept in Aristotelian biology where animals possess the capacity for sensation and movement but lack the higher "rational soul" or intellect found in humans.</term> is the difficulty of setting limits to sensibility and furnishing a specific difference between it and rationality in the soul of man. <header>IV</header>

## THE METAPHYSICAL PROBLEM OF THE MONADOLOGY

It was possible in the seventeenth century <note>The 1600s, an era of massive change in science and philosophy, often called the "Age of Reason."</note> to be indifferent to religion and open-minded in theology. It was possible to be a rationalist <note>A philosopher who believes that reason and logic, rather than religious revelation or sensory experience, are the primary sources of knowledge.</note> in regard to the mysteries of life and death, to be unresponsive to hopes and fears concerning the unseen world, and to be impassive and critical towards the popular realistic representations of heaven and hell. It was impossible, however, for any philosopher to take an untheological, or as we should describe it today in positive terms, a naturalistic or scientific view of man’s nature and origin. Our modern scientific aspect of nature is the outcome of a long historical and intellectual development. It was as clear to Leibniz and his contemporaries as it is to us today that the matter of our body is continuous with the inert matter of the universe, and that the form which the vital functioning of our organism assumes, repeats a type of general functioning common to all species of living creatures. Yet it was impossible for them to conceive that the human mind, with its rational individualized activities, could have come into

<summary>This page situates Leibniz’s philosophy within the 17th-century intellectual climate, noting that while thinkers could be skeptical of religious dogma, they lacked the "naturalistic" framework to explain the human mind through purely physical or biological means.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, Monadology, 17th Century, Rationalism, Naturalism, Theology, Philosophy of Mind, Human Origin</keywords>

existence otherwise than by a special creation <note>The theological belief that the universe and life originated from specific acts of divine creation, rather than through natural processes like evolution.</note>, or at least by an emanation <note>In philosophy, the idea that all things flow from a perfect high principle or "Source" (God), similar to light radiating from a sun.</note> which is a form of creation. Creation implied a creator endowed with sufficient knowledge and power. We have to take into account therefore, that in interpreting man and nature, the presence of reason and the faculty of discourse <note>The ability to communicate ideas through speech and logical argument.</note> appeared to raise man above mere animality and admit him to communion with the universal spirit; and man's knowledge of good and evil was direct evidence of a state of innocence which man had enjoyed and from which he had fallen <note>A reference to the "Fall of Man," the transition from a state of innocent obedience to God to a state of guilty disobedience.</note>. If to the vulgar <note>The general population or non-scholars.</note> the theory of man's special creation, his original innocence, and his fall, were expressed in myth and legend, to the philosophers they were rational doctrines. The Christian belief—the incarnation <note>The doctrine that God took on human flesh in the person of Jesus Christ.</note>, the atonement <note>The process by which a person removes the effects of sin and is reconciled with God.</note>, the means of grace and hope of glory—came under a different category. This was faith and not reason. It was based on historical evidence, guaranteed by authority and continuous tradition.

The imagery of the <term>Monadology</term> <note>Leibniz’s 1714 work which argues that the universe is composed of infinite "monads"—simple, indivisible substances that have no physical parts but contain a "soul-like" internal activity.</note> is theological, but its problem is metaphysical <note>Dealing with the fundamental nature of reality and existence.</note> in the precise meaning of the term. It is the problem of the conception of the reality which is the subject matter of the science of physics. At every moment of active conscious perception there is present to the mind and ideally represented in it an external and independent reality with a

What is this external reality, and what is the <term>sufficient reason</term> <note>Leibniz’s "Principle of Sufficient Reason" argues that nothing exists or happens without a logical reason or cause, even if that cause is known only to God.</note> of its representation? It is not a new problem, though it has received in modern times a new orientation. It is the same problem which presented itself to the ancient world when its philosophers inquired: How are the many one? 

It is the problem which <note>Thales (c. 624–548 BCE) is often called the first philosopher; he sought a single material origin for all things.</note> Thales of Miletus, and the other speculating Nature-philosophers of <note>A region on the coast of modern-day Turkey that was a hub of early Greek scientific thought.</note> Ionia, sought to solve by searching for a <term>primordial substance-form</term> to which all existence could be reduced. It is the problem which <note>These three thinkers were the fathers of "Atomism," the idea that the world is made of tiny, indivisible particles (atoms) moving through empty space (the void).</note> Leucippus, Democritus, and Epicurus thought they had solved in the theory of atoms and void. 

It has assumed a modern form by reason of the discovery of <note>The astronomer who proved the Earth revolves around the Sun, shifting the center of the universe.</note> Copernicus, the experimental method of <note>The physicist who championed the use of observation and mathematics to understand the laws of motion.</note> Galileo, and the philosophical principles of <note>Often called the father of modern philosophy, he emphasized the role of doubt and the distinction between mind and body.</note> Descartes. In Leibniz, the key to the solution is sought in the **principle of individuality**. In the <term>Monadology</term> <note>Leibniz’s masterwork, which proposes that the universe is made of "monads"—fundamental, soul-like units that represent the entire universe from their own unique perspective.</note>, this principle is made the basis of a complete metaphysics of the physical world.

### V

# A GENERAL SUMMARY OF THE THEORY OF MONADS

There are two logical principles which underlie all reasoning. These are the <term>principle of non-contradiction</term> <note>The logical law stating that a proposition cannot be both true and false at the same time; for example, a circle cannot be a square.</note> which we employ in the search for mathematical and metaphysical truths and the <term>principle of sufficient reason</term> <note>Leibniz’s influential idea that nothing happens without a reason why it should be so rather than otherwise, implying a rational order to the universe.</note> which we employ in all truths concerning matters of fact.

We perceive the universe of objects but we do not perceive the units of which the universe is composed. Yet we know that units must exist because the universe we perceive, and the objects we perceive as belonging to it, are <term>composites</term> <note>Things made up of multiple parts.</note>, and if there are composites there must be units. These units are the **monads**; they may be defined as <term>simple substances</term> <note>Elements that are "simple" because they have no parts and cannot be divided further.</note>.

The monads are not material **atoms**, for atoms are outside one another and so must have parts by which they adjoin one another. The monads are absolutely without parts. A monad has no extension <note>In philosophy, "extension" refers to the quality of taking up physical space.</note>, no shape, no position, and no movement. The monads are distinguished from one another by the quality or character which differentiates each from every other.

<column-break/>

**Key Vocabulary:**
*   **monads** <note>From the Greek *monas*, meaning "unit" or "one."</note>
*   **principle of non-contradiction**
*   **principle of sufficient reason**
*   **simple substances**
*   **composites**
*   **atoms**

<summary>This page outlines the two fundamental pillars of logic and introduces the "monad" as the indivisible, non-physical building block of the universe. It distinguishes these metaphysical units from physical atoms by their lack of size, shape, or parts.</summary>

<keywords>Leibniz, Monads, Principle of Sufficient Reason, Principle of Non-Contradiction, Metaphysics, Simple Substances, Atomism</keywords>

--- Page 33 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of Leibniz's Monadology, shifting from the definition of monads as simple substances to how they relate to our perception of the physical world. It addresses the nature of space, time, and matter as "appearances" rather than fundamental realities, and suggests that we can understand these invisible monads by looking at our own inner consciousness.</meta>

<page-num>19</page-num>
<header>THE THEORY OF MONADS</header>

The assemblage or aggregate of the monads is the real universe. This universe has neither extension, nor shape, nor position, nor movement, and it is not what we perceive. The universe which we perceive and the bodies which we distinguish in it have extension, shape, position and movement. They are not realities at all but only <term>phenomena</term> <note>In philosophy, a "phenomenon" is an object as it is perceived by the senses, rather than as it is in itself.</note> or appearances, like colours and sounds. The material world is a <term>well-founded phenomenon</term> <note>original: "phenomenon bene fundatum"; Leibniz’s term for appearances that are not mere illusions (like a dream) but are consistent and based on the underlying reality of the monads.</note>, such as the rainbow or the thunderstorm.

<margin>||</margin>
We do not perceive things as they are but only as they appear. Their appearances are due to the imperfect way in which we perceive them. <term>Extension</term> <note>The quality of taking up space.</note> is only a phenomenon; it is the representation of things as outside one another. Space and time are not real things nor even perceived phenomena. Space is the order of co-existences in our perceptions, time the order of successions.

Though we cannot perceive the monads as they really are but only in their appearances, yet we can know what a monad is, for we have the notion of it in our knowledge of our own soul. We have only therefore to imagine that in every monad there exists something analogous to feeling and to what in general we give the name of <term>perception</term> <note>The internal state of a monad representing external things.</note>. This gives us a positive knowledge of the monad. The monad, we can say, will experience change when it has different perceptions.

<vocab>monads, real universe, phenomena, well-founded phenomenon, extension, space, time, perception, soul</vocab>

<summary>This page explains Leibniz’s distinction between the true universe of monads and the world of appearances, arguing that space, time, and matter are "well-founded phenomena" rather than ultimate realities. It suggests that the human soul serves as the primary model for understanding the internal life and perceptions of a monad.</summary>
<keywords>Monadology, well-founded phenomenon, space and time, perception, soul as monad, metaphysics of extension</keywords>

--- Page 34 ---
<meta>This page continues the transition from the external appearance of the material world to the internal mechanics of the monad. Having established that space and time are perceptions, Leibniz now explores the source of change within these simple substances, introducing the concepts of internal force and appetition.</meta>

<page-num>20</page-num>
<header>THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ</header>

We are able to conceive the principle of a monad's perceptions. It must depend on a change or alteration which takes place within the monad. As the monad is simple, that is without parts, nothing can go out of it to act outside and nothing can enter into it to make it change internally. <note>This is the foundation of Leibniz's famous claim that "monads have no windows." Since they are not made of parts, they cannot be physically broken, altered, or entered by anything else.</note> Consequently the changes which happen to monads cannot have as their principle something which is outside them. Monads do not interact as the atoms were supposed to do.

This means that the substance of a monad must be conceived as *force*. There is more in each monad than its actuality for there is the possibility of change. There is in each monad a force which is the principle of all the changes which happen to it, or of all the perceptions it experiences, and so we can define <term>substance</term> <note>In this context, a "substance" is a fundamental, self-sufficient reality that does not depend on anything else for its existence.</note> as that which has in itself the principles of its changes.

The notion of force is metaphysical in its origin but perfectly intelligible. It is what everyone experiences and can observe in himself as effort when he wills to act.

The force of the monads, their effort, the continual tendency in them to produce a new perception is <term>appetition</term> <note>original: "appetitio"; from the Latin for "striving" or "desire." In Leibniz's view, this is the internal drive that pushes a soul or monad to move from one thought to the next.</note>. The state of every monad is continual change, the experiencing of new perceptions without cessation.

The bond existing between the monads which unites them into a universe is not a real but an ideal <note>The text breaks off here; Leibniz is likely referring to the "ideal bond" or "pre-established harmony" where monads do not physically touch, but are synchronized by God like perfectly timed clocks.</note>

<summary>This page explains that because monads are "windowless" and simple, they cannot be influenced by external objects; instead, all change comes from an internal "force" or "appetition." This internal drive constantly moves the monad from one perception to another, forming the basis of its existence.</summary>
<keywords>monad, perception, substance, force, appetition, Leibniz, metaphysics, internal change, windowless</keywords>

--- Page 35 ---
<meta>This page transitions from the internal principle of the monad (force and appetition) discussed on page 20 to the external relationship between monads. It introduces the "Pre-established Harmony," which is Leibniz's solution to the problem of how the soul and the body appear to interact without actually influencing one another.</meta>

<page-num>21</page-num>
<header>THE THEORY OF MONADS</header>

The bond <note>Leibniz is addressing the "problem of communication": if monads have "no windows" and cannot affect each other, how can they form a unified universe?</note> consists in the fact that they all combine together in striving for a common end as though there were real interaction between them. Out of these ideal relations arises a <term>harmony</term> <note>original: "harmonie"; Leibniz famously uses the analogy of two perfectly synchronized clocks to explain how things can work together without touching.</note>. The bond is of the nature of a harmony.

We have an example of such a harmony in experience. The soul, which is the <term>dominant monad</term> <note>Leibniz distinguishes the human soul as a higher-order monad that possesses memory and consciousness, allowing it to lead the "colony" of monads that make up the body.</note> of our body, has a succession of different perceptions independently of the body, which is constituted of other monads. Yet this body changes its state continually without receiving any influence from the soul and purely as the effect of its mechanism. Between the two there is a harmony such that changes of the body correspond with the changes of the soul.

The cause of this harmony is God who has <term>pre-established</term> <note>This "Pre-established Harmony" suggests that at the moment of creation, God coordinated every internal change in every monad so they would always be in sync.</note> it, not by determining the changes of the others, but by considering what must happen to each possible monad by reason of its own force and by actualizing those in which agreement was found.

What we call whole and part and composite are not such in reality. They are <term>phenomena</term> <note>To Leibniz, the physical world we see (the "composite") is a "well-founded phenomenon"—it is an appearance that accurately represents the underlying metaphysical reality of the monads.</note> which result from the pre-established harmony, precisely in the way in which in a musical performance the sounds propagated exist independently with no bond uniting them although in relation to our sense-organ they are united in a harmony.

<vocab>monad, soul, body, harmony, mechanism, pre-established harmony, God, phenomena</vocab>

<summary>Leibniz explains that monads do not interact directly; instead, God has synchronized their internal changes into a "pre-established harmony." This coordination explains the apparent connection between the soul and the mechanical body, as well as the nature of composite objects as mere phenomena.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of the nature of monads, moving from the concept of "Pre-established Harmony" on page 21 to how these spiritual units aggregate to form the physical world and the nature of conscious experience.</meta>

<page-num>22</page-num>
<header>THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ</header>

A body is an aggregate of <term>monads</term> <note>Leibniz’s fundamental building blocks of reality. Unlike physical atoms, monads are "spiritual points" or centers of force that have no parts and cannot be divided.</note>, which by the order they preserve among themselves produce the phenomena of <term>extension</term> <note>The physical quality of taking up space.</note>, mobility, impenetrability, motive force and inertial force.

Each state of a monad expresses and represents the relations between it and the rest of the monads. These states are the monad’s <term>perceptions</term> <note>For Leibniz, "perception" is not just conscious awareness, but any internal state that represents external things. Even a simple monad "perceives" the whole universe in this way.</note> and each perception is representative. As there is no limit to the representation, every perception represents in its way the actual state of the universe, and as every state has its position in a succession, the one perception represents past, present and future. Each monad, however, only represents the universe from its own view-point and in its own manner and not by leaps and bounds. This is why the monad perceives the universe only through the body of which it is the <term>dominant monad</term> <note>The soul or central monad that organizes a collection of other monads into a single living organism.</note>. We must think of the universe as an infinity of bodies.

A monad may have no consciousness of its perceptions and what they represent, because its perceptions may be totally obscure. Only when they become clear is there consciousness of them, and consciousness grows with the greater number of clear perceptions. Just as the roar of the surf <note>This refers to Leibniz's theory of "minute perceptions" (petites perceptions). He argues that we have many tiny, subconscious perceptions that only become a conscious experience when they combine—just as we hear the "roar" of the sea without being able to distinguish the sound of every individual water droplet.</note> is composed of individual waves but the sound of the waves may be obscured in the confused roar of the surf.

<meta>This page continues the discussion on Leibniz’s Monadology, specifically classifying different types of monads based on their clarity of perception and explaining the relationship between soul and body as a transformation rather than a beginning or end.</meta>

# THE THEORY OF MONADS
<page-num>23</page-num>

We never reach <term>perceptions</term> <note>Internal states that reflect the external world.</note> that are entirely simple. <term>Monads</term> <note>The fundamental, indivisible "spiritual atoms" that make up reality.</note> are ranked according to how clearly they perceive. When their perceptions are completely dim and confused, monads are called <term>entelechies</term> <note>From the Greek *entelecheia*; these are the most basic "soul-like" forces that drive matter but lack conscious awareness.</note>. When they possess some degree of clarity along with consciousness, they are called **souls**. When they possess the ability to know necessary truths, they are known as **rational souls** <note>The human mind or spirit, capable of reason and understanding universal laws.</note>.

An organized body is one whose individual monads exist in a state of **harmony** <note>Leibniz’s "Pre-established Harmony": the idea that God coordinated all substances so they work together without actually "touching" or influencing each other directly.</note>. This allows them to work toward a common goal in an order where they seem to depend on one another. Each monad is linked to a physical body through which it reflects the entire universe; in relation to this body, the monad acts as the **dominant entelechy** <note>The "commander" or central soul of a living organism.</note>.

There is nothing dead in nature. Everything in it is sentient and alive. Every tiny particle of matter contains a whole world of creatures, souls, and entelechies of an infinite variety of kinds.

In the absolute sense, there is no such thing as birth or death. There are only <term>metamorphoses</term> <note>Changes in form or state rather than a beginning or ending of existence.</note> and transformations. Our souls are not created at the moment we are conceived, nor are they destroyed when we die. They were created alongside the world itself and only become "rational" when their physical bodies develop enough to convey perceptions with a specific degree of clarity.

### II

## THE MONADOLOGY

<term>Monadology<note>original: "La Monadologie". This title refers to the philosophical system published by Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz in 1714. The term is derived from the Greek "monas," meaning unity or "one." Leibniz uses it to describe the fundamental, "simple" substances that compose the entire universe.</note></term>

<summary>This title page introduces Section II, dedicated to the study of Leibniz's Monadology and his theory of simple substances.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, Monadology, metaphysics, philosophy, simple substance, Section II</keywords>

--- Page 41 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of the discussion on Leibniz's Monadology. It defines the 'Monad' and establishes the historical context of 'substance' as it was understood in 17th-century philosophy and ancient Greek thought.</meta>

# THE MONADOLOGY

**1.** The <term>Monad</term> <note>Derived from the Greek word *monas*, meaning 'unit' or 'one'. In this context, it refers to the fundamental, indivisible building blocks of the universe.</note>, the subject of this discourse, is nothing else than a simple substance which enters into <term>composites</term> <note>Things made up of multiple parts; complex structures.</note>. Simple means that it is without parts.

The concept of substance dominates the philosophy of the seventeenth century. The notion of substance arises in the common sense idea that there must be some kind of existence occupying space to which all the variety of the perceptual world is ultimately reducible and out of which in the succession of time it is generated. Early Greek philosophy arose in the quest for the <term>first matter</term> <note>original Latin: *prima materia*. This term refers to the most basic, formless stuff from which all physical things are created.</note> of the world : Is it water, air, fire, or something even more ethereal, such stuff as thoughts are made of ?

Substance is the idea of the existence which gives unity and continuity to the world which is the object of experience and to the mind which is the subject of experience. What kind of thing could substance be ? The problem shaped itself in varying ways, but two opposite principles, in one aspect contradictory, in another aspect complementary, tended to assert themselves. The first was the idea that substance must, as the <term>underlying foundation</term> <note>original Latin: *substratum*. Literally 'that which is strewn under,' referring to the permanent reality that supports changing outward appearances.</note> of the real, be actual ; that it must partake of the character of fact ; that as it is self-subsistent and dependent on nothing outside itself, it must be what it is at every moment of existence ; it must, by its definition, be changeless ; it is what is and cannot not be. The other was the idea that substance is of the nature of force ; that its reality must consist in its potentiality at every moment and not in

<vocab>Monad, substance, composites, seventeenth century, first matter (original: prima materia), underlying foundation (original: substratum), force, potentiality</vocab>

<summary>This page introduces the Monad as an indivisible simple substance and examines how the 17th-century concept of substance evolved from ancient Greek inquiries into the fundamental "first matter" of the universe.</summary>
<keywords>Monadology, Leibniz, substance, monad, Greek philosophy, prima materia, substratum, 17th-century metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 42 ---
<meta>This page continues the definition of substance, moving from the historical concept of an underlying foundation to the distinction between fixed actuality and living potentiality, which is central to Leibniz's philosophy.</meta>

# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

its <term>actuality</term> <note>The state of being existing in fact or reality, as opposed to being a mere possibility.</note> at any moment. If we compare the two ideas, we find that each is suggested by the objects of familiar experience. These objects are of two kinds, the inert and the living. The substance of an inert thing seems to be actual and unchangeable; the substance of a living thing seems to be <term>potential</term> <note>Having the power to become something or to act, even if that action is not currently visible.</note>. A living thing is never what it is in its actualization at any moment of its history. It is always becoming. Its reality is its force or power.

The followers of Descartes, including Spinoza, had sought the solution of the problem in terms of actuality. Leibniz abandoned the whole attempt; took his type of reality from the living world; regarded the inert, material world as pure <term>phenomenon</term> <note>An appearance or an object as it is perceived by the senses, rather than the thing as it exists in its own fundamental reality.</note> and conceived substance as **force**. The reality which gives unity and continuity to the world of experience is not an actuality but a potentiality, not matter but force, not fact but power.

The *Monadology* opens without a formal definition of substance. It indicates, however, the kind of existent which will alone satisfy the concept of substance. Substance is that which has in itself the principle of its changes. Leibniz begins with the simple logical consequence, that if there are **composites** there must be **components**, and the components must be units not further decomposable.

Leibniz, like Spinoza, holds against Descartes that there are not two substances but one only. Unlike Spinoza he holds that this substance exists in an infinite number of separate beings. In every existing substance the essence is one and identical, it consists in **effort** <note>original Latin: *conatus*. In 17th-century philosophy, this refers to an innate inclination of a thing to continue to exist and enhance itself.</note> and **perception** <note>For Leibniz, perception is the internal state of the monad representing external things, not necessarily involving conscious awareness.</note>.

Spinoza and Leibniz stand out pre-eminently in the history of modern philosophy on account of the pre-

<meta>This page continues the comparison between Spinoza and Leibniz, focusing on how their differing definitions of substance—one as a single, static unity and the other as a dynamic multiplicity—shape their entire philosophical systems.</meta>

### THE NATURE OF MONADS

29

the precision they have given to their definitions of the fundamental concept of the **substance** of the universe. Each has based his interpretation of nature and mind on the concept of substance. Spinoza’s <term>substance</term> <note>In philosophy, "substance" refers to the most basic reality that exists on its own and does not depend on anything else for its existence.</note> is by definition one; Leibniz’s substance is by definition many. Spinoza’s substance is identical with the concept of God and named God. Leibniz’s substance is simple and named a **monad** <note>A term Leibniz used for the simplest, indivisible units of reality; spiritual "atoms" that make up the universe.</note> to show that it is primarily and essentially multiple.

The difference is fundamental and affects the two philosophies down to the minutest points of detail. When we compare the two, we see that Spinoza’s concept is primarily **objective** and **static**. In all the variety of its infinite <term>modes</term> <note>The specific ways or forms in which a substance exists or is expressed, much like how waves are modes of the ocean.</note>, the substance of the universe is one and identical. The ground of all existence, natural or ideal, is, according to Spinoza, the one substance. There is no determination of one thing by another thing, no power in one thing to be a cause and produce an effect in another thing; there is only the one substance and its modes. The idea of a **transcendent cause** <note>A cause that exists outside or beyond the world it influences; Spinoza rejected this, believing God/Substance is the world itself.</note> is for Spinoza a self-contradiction. **Design**, **purpose**, and **free agency** are merely appearances resulting from the limitations implied in the existence of finite modes. **Necessity** <note>The idea that things must happen in a certain way and cannot be otherwise.</note> is in nature, not in the power which rules nature.

Leibniz’s conception is, on the other hand, **subjective** not objective, and **dynamic** not static. No universe exists independently of the monads, and the universe common to the monads is the "universe of the monads." The activity of the universe is the activity of the monads, and these are free spirits expressing themselves.

The essential difference between the two views is not that one is materialistic and the other spiritualistic,

# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

30

Both philosophers accept the two aspects of the universe: its aspect as a material extension and mechanical movement, and its aspect as an intellectual system of ideas and **volitions** <note>The power of using one's will; acts of choosing or deciding.</note>. The difference is that while Spinoza finds the unity of these two aspects in an identical substance, and the duality in the **concomitance** <note>The state of two things existing or occurring at the same time, like two clocks ticking in unison.</note> of the two series of the modes of the two attributes of that substance, Leibniz, starting with a real plurality, deduces the aspect of the universe as a single mechanistic system from the nature of the individual activities of the monads.

> 2. There must be simple substances since there are composites; because the composite is nothing but a group, or **aggregate** <note>original: "aggregatum"; a collection of distinct parts brought together into a whole.</note>, of the simples.

Leibniz does not start as Descartes did with **"I think, therefore I am"** <note>original: "cogito ergo sum"</note>. Such a beginning presents the factual existence of a simple substance, for the self is simple, **inextended** <note>In philosophy, "inextended" means something that does not occupy physical space or have physical dimensions, like a thought or a soul.</note>, and individual. Leibniz, however, appears rather to reverse the formula and say **"I am, therefore I think"** <note>original: "sum ergo cogito"</note>. Whatever is, is active, and to be active is to think in some form or mode.

"There are composites," that is to say, there is an objective universe presented to the mind and there is a body which mediates the mind's activity. The body is part of and continuous with the universe. The body has parts. The universe is a collection or aggregate of things; its oneness is the unity of a collection, and the reality of the collection is its constituent units. What then are these constituent units? They...

# SIMPLES AND COMPOSITES

are <term>monads</term> <note>From the Greek *monas*, meaning "unit." In Leibniz's philosophy, monads are the fundamental, indivisible building blocks of reality. Unlike physical atoms, they are centers of force or soul-like activity.</note>. Monads are not perceived objects; they are the realities which are implied in the perception of objects.

The ancient philosophy had propounded the same question and given its answer. It had defined simple substances as <term>atoms</term> <note>Literally "indivisibles." Traditional Greek atomism suggested that matter is made of tiny, solid particles that cannot be cut further.</note>. The difficulty of the concept of atoms is that it requires us to postulate a limit to the divisibility of matter, conceived as the content of space, at the same time that we are obliged to admit that there can be no limit to the divisibility of space, conceived as the container of matter. It can be demonstrated that there exists no limit to the divisibility of geometrical space. Space is defined by points and lines and planes and these are themselves undimensional and without magnitude. Yet although space is by definition divisible to infinity, if it be occupied then there must, it was argued, by the very concept of occupancy, be a limit to the divisibility of matter, and atoms therefore are the indivisible units of matter and not mathematical points. In modern philosophy a similar problem arose in regard to events or time occupancy. It led to the concept of a <term>specious present</term> <note>A term describing the "now" we actually experience. Rather than being an instantaneous point in time, it is a short duration that includes a fading memory of the immediate past and an anticipation of the immediate future.</note>, that is, an indivisible moment of time which is not a mathematical point-instant but an interval which holds something of the past and something of the future in the present now.

Atoms and events are not real units. This does not mean that we cannot discern them perceptually or construct them conceptually. It means that the concept of them is self-contradictory. Space-time occupants are by definition <term>composites</term> <note>Things made up of multiple parts.</note> and therefore themselves imply units. It is vain to look for real units in space and time for space and time are principles of <term>multiplicity</term> <note>The state of being many or manifold; the opposite of unity.</note>.

<summary>This page contrasts Leibniz's "monads" with the traditional concept of "atoms," arguing that physical matter and time are infinitely divisible and therefore cannot provide the truly simple, indivisible units required for a foundation of reality.</summary>

# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

Yet we have not to go outside experience to find real units. Our own self-centred perceptive activity, our self as subject of an activity, presents to our reflexion both the fact and the type of simple unity. My existence is <term>self-existence</term> <note>Leibniz argues that the soul or "self" does not rely on the arrangement of physical parts to exist; its existence is inherent to its activity.</note>. It depends on nothing external to itself, that is to say, my existence is affirmed in my activity and my activity is identical with my existence. The universe may contain infinite reals of this nature. In fact every object which we conceive as living, acting and conscious, must, if it have these characters, fulfil, from its own subjective standpoint, exactly the conditions of a <term>simple substance</term> <note>A "simple" substance is one without parts. Unlike a machine or a rock, which can be broken down, a simple substance is an indivisible point of consciousness.</note>. Such an object is not a space-time occupant, whatever part space and time play in its activity. We cannot say of a self that part of it is here and now, part there and then; wherever and whenever it is, it exists altogether as indivisible unity. The spatial and temporal order of the universe is something which the subject of experience perceives or conceives, it does not introduce multiplicity into the subject, or divide and separate the subject of experience into component parts. The subject of experience exists only integrally as simple substance or <term>monad</term> <note>From the Greek *monas*, meaning "unit" or "one." Leibniz uses this term to describe the fundamental, soul-like building blocks of the universe.</note> and such monads are the real units which constitute the universe.

> 3. Where there are no parts, there can be neither extension, nor shape, nor divisibility. Monads are the real atoms of Nature. Briefly, they are the elements of things. <note>This is the third proposition of Leibniz’s *Monadology* (1714). Here, he distinguishes his "atoms" from the physical atoms of Democritus or modern science, which are still technically divisible in thought.</note>

Monads, it will be seen, are what Kant much later will describe as <term>noumena</term> <note>From the Greek *nooumenon* ("thing being thought"). Kant used this term to describe "things-in-themselves" as they exist independently of our senses.</note> and distinguish from <term>phenomena</term> <note>From the Greek *phainomenon* ("that which appears"). These are the objects of our senses—the way things look, feel, and sound—which Leibniz and Kant agree are not the ultimate reality.</note>. They are things in themselves. How

# THE UNIVERSE

<page-num>33</page-num>

How, then, can the physical universe—the universe presented to me as a <term>phenomenon</term> <note>From the Greek *phainomenon*, meaning "that which appears." In this context, it refers to the world as we perceive it through our senses, which may differ from how the world exists in its own right.</note> in my sense-experience—be constituted of <term>noumenal</term> <note>A term used to describe things as they are in themselves, independent of human observation. While we see the "phenomenon" (the appearance), the "noumenon" is the underlying reality.</note> monads? 

The phenomenal universe is composite, meaning it is made up of parts. Its constituent elements are connected in my apprehension by an order of coexistence which I objectify as **space** and an order of succession which I objectify as **time**. The things I apprehend in this order of coexistence and succession, and the space and time which I apprehend as the framework of things and events, seem to exist independently of my activity in perceiving them. 

In what way can I conceive this independence to be real? It is clear that the presence of phenomena in my perception does not of itself endow phenomena with the status of noumenal reality. Sense-perceived objects are relative through and through. There is only one way of conceiving any object known phenomenally as existing noumenally, and that is to conceive it as a <term>monad</term> <note>The fundamental unit of reality in Leibniz's philosophy. A monad is not a physical particle, but a simple, soul-like entity that perceives the universe from its own unique perspective.</note>. To conceive it as a monad is to conceive it as in itself an indivisible, integral, active, self-centred subject with its own experience. 

I habitually endow my fellow men, and to a certain extent all living forms, with this noumenal reality. The phenomenal aspect of these objects—how they look or sound—is mine and not theirs, but the noumenal existence of their experience is theirs and not mine. There is no other way of conceiving "things-in-themselves." If there is a real universe, and if this real universe is composite, if it exists other than as my idea, then monads and not space-occupying <term>atoms</term> <note>Unlike the "atoms" of physics which occupy space and could theoretically be divided, Leibniz’s monads are "metaphysical atoms"—points of force or consciousness that have no extension and cannot be split.</note> are its elements.

<summary>This page explores how the physical world of space and time (the phenomenal) is actually underpinned by a reality of individual, conscious units called monads (the noumenal). It argues that we can only understand the independent existence of "things" by viewing them as subjects with their own internal experiences, much like we view other people.</summary>

<header>THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ</header>

<page-num>34</page-num>

<margin>*</margin> 4. There is no dissolution of a monad to be feared, for there is no conceivable way by which a **simple substance** <note>Leibniz defines a "simple substance" as something that has no parts. Because it has no parts, it cannot be broken, divided, or decayed by natural means.</note> could perish naturally.

The universe of which the monad is a constituent is the physical universe—the universe which is presented in sense perception, conceptualized in thought, and objectified in action. It is the **phenomenal** <note>From the Greek *phainomenon*, referring to the world as it appears to our senses.</note> universe of common sense and scientific reality, the **noumenal** <note>Referring to the "thing-in-itself" or the underlying reality that exists independently of our observation.</note> reality of which in philosophy we seek to understand. It is the elements of this universe which Leibniz declares to be monads. What does this imply in the nature of the universe? 

Since monads are **inextended** <note>A technical term meaning they do not occupy physical space and have no dimensions (length, width, or height).</note> the universe of the monads cannot be in space, and since monads are active the universe of the monads cannot be a succession of inert states in time. The elements of the universe are not therefore a mechanically determined order of coexistent, inert, minimal masses in a mechanically determined order of successive states, such as the <term>atoms</term> <note>The author contrasts monads with the "atoms" of classical physics, which were thought to be tiny, hard pieces of matter.</note> purported to be. 

Now the atoms were conceived primarily for the purpose of finding a basis for the stability of the physical world, for its indestructibility and for the continuity of its materiality. The monads on the other hand seem to offer no such guarantee against dissolution. Leibniz touches here a difficulty which is felt acutely in regard to the modern concept of energy. If mass is a <term>function of velocity</term> <note>A reference to modern physics (specifically Einstein’s Theory of Relativity), suggesting that matter is not a "thing" but a result of energy and movement.</note>, as the modern theory finds it necessary to define it, must we not postulate inert particles in some form? Otherwise it would seem that the cessation of activity would imply the dissolution of the universe. The monad, however, cannot undergo dissolution. First, it does not consist of parts, and

### 5. For the same reason there is no way by which a simple substance could naturally begin to be, since it could not be formed by composition.

This may seem to be merely the converse of the previous affirmation that a simple substance cannot perish by natural means, but in fact it has a peculiar significance of its own. In what it denies, Leibniz may have had in mind **Descartes's** <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the French philosopher who famously said "I think, therefore I am." He viewed the physical world as a machine governed by laws of movement.</note> theory of the generation of individual mechanisms by movement imparted to extension. He is here propounding, however, what to him is a logical consequence of his own concept of **individual substance**. An individual substance contains its attributes in the same way in which the subject in the logical judgment contains the predicates. <term>Subject and Predicates</term> <note>In formal logic, the "subject" is the person or thing being discussed, and the "predicate" is the description given to them. # THE CREATIVE ACT

<page-num>37</page-num>

...that every true predication has some basis in the nature of things, and even when a proposition is not identical—that is, when the predicate <note>The "predicate" is the part of a sentence that provides information about the subject (e.g., in "the apple is red," "red" is the predicate).</note> is not expressly contained in the subject—it is still necessary that it be virtually contained in it. Thus, the content of the subject must always include that of the predicate in such a way that if one understands perfectly the concept of the subject, he will know that the predicate appertains to it also. This being so, we are able to say that this is the nature of an **individual substance** or of a complete being: namely, to afford a conception so complete, that the concept shall be sufficient for the understanding of it and for the deduction of all the predicates of which the subject is or may become the subject.

What he meant was made clearly evident in the correspondence. If we suppose God to create or bring into existence an individual substance, we have not to suppose that to do so God must create all the attributes of that substance and all the forms of expression which its activity will assume. These are all given *a priori* <note>original: "a priori"; meaning "from the beginning" or "prior to experience." In philosophy, this refers to knowledge that comes from pure logic rather than observation.</note> in the nature of the substance itself. What God creates, therefore, is a simple substance with its real nature which will itself express itself. In the **Monadology** <note>The *Monadology* (1714) is one of Leibniz's most famous works, outlining his theory that the universe is made of "monads"—indivisible, soul-like units.</note> Leibniz is solely concerned with the metaphysical concept.

<margin>6.</margin> 6. Monads can only begin to exist or cease to exist all at once; that is to say, they can only begin by **creation** and end by **annihilation**. On the other hand, what is composite begins or ends by parts.

enter in or go out. **Accidents** <term>accidents: in philosophy, these are qualities or properties—like color, shape, or weight—that belong to a substance but are not its essential nature.</term> are not detachable from **substances** <term>substances: the fundamental, independent entities that make up reality.</term> nor able to walk away on their own account as the **sensible species** <term>sensible species: a theory held by medieval scholars suggesting that objects emit "likenesses" or "images" of themselves that travel through the air to enter our senses.</term> of the **scholastics** <note>The Scholastics were medieval philosophers and theologians who sought to reconcile Christian doctrine with the logic of Aristotle.</note> were supposed to do. Neither substance nor accident therefore can enter a monad from outside.

The privacy of the monad is inviolable. It cannot be changed or altered in its nature by an external influence brought to bear upon it. Now precisely the same might be said of the **atom**, but the simplicity of the atom is directly opposite in character to the simplicity of the monad. We cannot change the inner nature of the atom because by definition the atom has no "within." We cannot alter or change the inner nature of the monad because by definition it has no "without." Atoms are distinguished from one another by position and relative disposition alone, and all their relations are external. In atoms therefore there is nothing to alter. Anything which changes a monad must, on the other hand, be in it, for all its activities are within and self-originated.

The often quoted sentence "the monads have no windows" <note>original: "Les Monades n'ont point de fenêtres." This is perhaps Leibniz's most famous metaphor, illustrating that monads are completely self-contained.</note> may easily lend itself to ambiguity. "Windows" generally mean transparencies out of which we look and through which light can enter. **Leibniz** <note>Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716), the philosopher who created this system.</note> means openings of any kind through which there can be free passage. It is true of the monad that it has no windows, in either meaning of the term, but since the activity of the monad is **perceiving**, and since perceiving derives its meaning from the faculty of visualization, it may easily be misleading to interpret

Nevertheless it is as true of the monad to say that nothing can look out from it or look into it as to say that nothing can go out of it or come into it, because there is no outside. It will be said however that the monads are certainly outside one another. This is true in the absolute meaning that every monad is self-contained. It is not true if it implies an independent space within which monads are juxtaposed. The universe is mirrored in the perceptions of every monad and there is only one universe—the universe of the monads, not a universe in which there are monads. <note>Leibniz argues that space is not a "container" that exists on its own; rather, space is just the way we perceive the relationship between monads.</note>

In the *Discourse on Metaphysics* <note>Written in 1686, this is one of Leibniz's most important works, where he explains that every substance (monad) contains all its past and future within itself.</note> Leibniz says (section 26) “Ideas are all stored up within us” and he explains his meaning in this way: Most of us suppose that an idea is the form of what we are thinking about when we think, and that every time we think of the same thing we have another different idea of it, which may be similar to the preceding one. The fact is, however, that our mind possesses the power of representing to itself anything it wishes to think about as the occasion arises, and this activity of our mind in expressing natures, forms, and essences <term>essences: the fundamental qualities that make a thing what it is.</term>, is properly the idea of the thing. 

The idea therefore is always in us whether we are thinking of it or not. Nothing enters naturally into our minds from outside. We have a bad habit of thinking that our minds receive messengers and have, as it were, doors and windows. All our ideas are always in our minds because at every moment the mind expresses all its future thoughts. It is always thinking confusedly <note>For Leibniz, "confused" thought refers to perceptions we have but do not yet consciously notice, like the sound of individual waves in the roar of the ocean.</note> of all that it will ever think distinctly. We cannot learn anything of which we do not already possess the idea. The idea is the material out of which thought will form itself.

## SPACE AND TIME
<page-num>41</page-num>

The contrast in the section we are considering is between the conceptions of units differing only by their absolute position and relative disposition <note>This refers to the traditional view of atoms or physical particles in a system like Newton’s, where position is the only thing that distinguishes one part of matter from another.</note>, and units which are indivisible individuals differing qualitatively and absolutely, not quantitatively <note>These are the monads, which Leibniz describes as unique individuals rather than identical building blocks.</note>. Since the monads are inextended <term>inextended: lacking physical dimensions; having no length, width, or depth.</term> the composite universe cannot be spatial, and since the monads are active the universe cannot be inert. The universe, therefore, does not depend on the fundamental conception of a mechanically determined order of coexistent inert masses and a mechanically determined order of successive states. 

But if space and time are not postulated as an absolute framework, can we give any definition to our idea of a really objective physical world? The answer is that the difficulty is not in the conception, this is both metaphysically and mathematically presentable to the understanding, the difficulty is in the imagination, for we are precluded the use of sense imagery. Space and time are not abolished but they are subordinated. The perceived universe, the universe mirrored in each monad, will then be the sense-intuited aspect of an intelligible reality <term>intelligible reality: a reality that can be understood by the mind or reason, even if it cannot be perceived by the physical senses.</term>. Space and time will belong to this intuited aspect of the universe. Space is then for each monad the order of coexistences <term>coexistences: things existing at the same time.</term> of its perceptions, time the order of successions <term>successions: things following one after another.</term>.

It is not possible to imagine the universe without a spatio-temporal setting. Yet we can find abundant illustrations of real activities in which the necessary mental imagery involves a spatio-temporal setting, but in which this mental imagery can be recognized as no more than a supporting scaffold, and space and time themselves not fundamental conditions on which the reality depends. The living body is such a reality <note>Leibniz uses the living organism to illustrate how a physical entity in space is actually driven by an internal, non-spatial force or "soul."</note>.

<summary>This page explains Leibniz’s revolutionary view that space and time are not absolute "containers" of the universe, but rather the way monads organize their internal perceptions of coexistence and sequence.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, Monadology, space and time, intelligible reality, coexistence, succession, perception, metaphysics</keywords>
