-># THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ<-
<page-num>42</page-num>

It is constituted of elements each of which is independently unfolding its own individual activity and all of which in their harmony become interdependent and subservient to the individuality of the whole. A family, a city, a society, a nation, can be exhaustively described in terms of the independent activities of their constituent individual members and of their interdependent harmony. The monadic universe is such a reality.

The <term>sensible species: a technical term from medieval philosophy describing "images" or "likenesses" that objects were thought to send out to our senses.</term> of the <term>scholastics: medieval philosophers and theologians who followed the teachings of Aristotle and the Church Fathers.</term>, to which Leibniz makes a playful allusion, was the scholastic theory of the origin of what today we refer to as sense-data. It was perhaps the crudest form of the representative theory <note>The idea that we don't see things directly, but only internal "copies" or "representations" of them.</note> in the history of philosophy. The real objects were, it was assumed, capable of casting off from themselves ideal or ghostly representatives or copies which had the power to affect the mind and furnish it with sense-imagery.

<margin>
8. The monads must have qualities, otherwise they would not even be beings. If simple substances were not qualitatively different, there would be no means of being aware of any change in composite things, since what is in the composite <note>A "composite" is anything made of parts; Leibniz argues that the properties of the whole must come from the properties of the individual parts.</note> can only come from its simple ingredients. Were the monads without qualities they would be indistinguishable from one another since they do not differ in quantity. And then it would be like movement in a <term>plenum: a space that is completely full of matter, with no vacuum or empty gaps.</term>, each place would receive only the equivalent of what it parted with and one state of the whole would be indistinguishable from another.
</margin>

<summary>Leibniz argues that the universe is a harmony of independent individuals (monads) and critiques the old "sensible species" theory of perception. He emphasizes that monads must possess unique internal qualities to account for the observable changes and differences in the physical world.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the investigation into the nature of the monad, transitioning from the concept of simple substances to the "qualities" that distinguish them, eventually addressing the theological implications for human freedom and divine foreknowledge.</meta>

->## QUALITATIVE DIFFERENCE<-
<page-num>43</page-num>

By qualities Leibniz does not mean sensible qualities <note>Qualities we perceive through the senses, like color, heat, or smell.</note> but the kind of qualities which we ascribe to individual persons when we characterize their actions. The quality of a <term>monad: the fundamental, indivisible "soul-like" unit of reality in Leibniz's system</term> is not the external appearance it may present to the perception of another, but the character of the internal activity which distinguishes its nature from the nature of another. This opposition of <term>numerical difference: the distinction between two things based simply on the fact that they are separate units (like two identical mass-produced coins)</term> to qualitative difference can be illustrated in the conception of an army. We may regard the soldiers of an army as so many fighting units arranged in the field of battle and we may then neglect altogether the quality of the unit soldiers; or we may conceive each soldier, in his personal activity, ready to respond to the requirements of the action. In this aspect of the units they differ qualitatively.

It is not however the actions of the individual which are the quality of the monad; the actions are the direct outcome of the nature of the monad and this nature is its quality. The distinction is very important, for on it depended the doctrine of individual freedom and human responsibility. God, in Leibniz’s view, in creating the monads created free individuals each with its own distinctive quality, and in doing so he did not decree the actions of each individual although by his omniscience <note>The state of knowing everything.</note> he knew beforehand what actions the free exercise of the nature of the individual monads would actualize. It was this foreknowledge which enabled God in creating the universe of monads to <term>pre-establish its harmony: the theory that God coordinated the "scripts" of every individual monad at the beginning of time so they all align perfectly without actually influencing one another.</term>

Challenged by <note>Antoine Arnauld (1612–1694) was a prominent French theologian and philosopher who engaged in a deep and often critical correspondence with Leibniz.</note> Arnauld that his doctrine implied a fatal necessity <note>The idea that everything is predetermined by fate and that human choice is an illusion.</note>, inasmuch as all the individual members of the human family must be considered qualities of the first parent Adam, seeing that Adam’s actions follow from his nature, and these actions

<summary>This page explains that monads are distinguished by their internal character rather than external traits, using this qualitative difference to argue for human free will within a universe pre-coordinated by God.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, monad, qualitative difference, numerical difference, Arnauld, pre-established harmony, fatal necessity, Adam, free will, divine foreknowledge</keywords>

--- Page 58 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of the monad, moving from the definition of its internal qualities to the relationship between individuals (represented by the lineage of Adam) and the famous principle that no two things in the universe are truly identical.</meta>

->### THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ<-
<page-num>44</page-num>

...involve the existence of his children and children’s children indefinitely, and all therefore that happens in the world is contained for God in the one original concept of Adam <note>Leibniz uses the biblical figure of Adam to explain a logical point: if God knows the "complete concept" of a person, he knows everything that will ever happen to them and their descendants.</note>, Leibniz replied in effect that no monad is the quality of another monad or derives from another its quality or nature. He explained the generations of individuals, and their continuity through ancestry with the first parent, as the <term>pre-established harmony: the theory that God orchestrated the universe at the moment of creation so that every individual substance acts in perfect synchronicity with all others, like perfectly synchronized clocks</term> rendered possible in the creation by the perfection in God of the attribute of <term>omniscience: the state of possessing infinite knowledge, including all past and future events</term>.

To appreciate the significance of the concept of the pre-established harmony we have to bear in mind that Leibniz is writing for a philosophical generation which knows not evolution <note>In the 17th and early 18th centuries, the modern biological theory of evolution did not exist; scholars viewed the world as a finished, logical creation rather than a changing process.</note> and which views every problem from an intellectualist standpoint.

> 9. There is another reason why each monad
> must be different from every other. It is that
> there are never in nature two beings which
> are perfectly alike and between which it is
> not possible to find an inward difference, or
> one based on an intrinsic character.

This is the logical principle of the **identity of indiscernibles**. It is the simple principle that if there is no difference between two things they are not two but one and the same. Of course for Leibniz there are no things, there are only monads and their perceptions, all reality therefore is individual. Yet while the principle is based on the concept of reality, Leibniz was prepared to defend it empirically <note>Empirical evidence is knowledge gained through the senses or direct observation.</note>. In the correspondence with Clarke <note>Samuel Clarke was a prominent English philosopher and follower of Isaac Newton; his letters with Leibniz are among the most famous debates in the history of philosophy.</note> he says: “ A friend of mine, in conversation with me one day in the garden

# CHANGE

...at the gardens of Herrenhausen <note>Herrenhausen was the summer palace of the Royal House of Hanover, where Leibniz served as a librarian and counselor.</note> in the presence of the Electress <note>Electress Sophia of the Palatinate (1630–1714), a close friend and intellectual collaborator of Leibniz.</note> who said he was sure he could find two leaves exactly alike; the Electress challenged him to produce them and he hurried about searching for them a long time but in vain.”

**10. Also I take it for granted that every created being is subject to change, and consequently the created monad changes, and indeed in every monad change is continuous.**

The concept of **change** now replaces the concept of **movement**. This is not the mere choice of another expression, it is the advance to a new idea and it follows from the concept of the monad. Movement in the meaning of a propagation or a translation is inconceivable in the monad, because the monad is not extended nor is it spatially juxtaposed to other monads. Internal movement in the meaning of mechanical rearrangement of parts is equally inconceivable because the monad has no parts, it is simple and indivisible. On the other hand change in the meaning of **becoming**, of **history**, of **identity in difference**, of **duration**, of the **actuality of the past in the present**, is essential because the reality of the monad is activity. Change is not a recurring series but development, self-unfolding. The monad changes because the monad lives.

<margin>x</margin>
**11. It follows that the natural changes of the monads depend on an <insert>(</insert>inner principle<insert>),</insert> because, as we have shewn, an external cause can produce no effect on the inner being of the monad.** <note>Because monads have "no windows" and cannot be physically altered by the outside world, any change they experience must come from a drive or "appetite" already inside them.</note>

<summary>Leibniz argues that since monads are simple and indivisible, they do not "move" in a mechanical sense but instead undergo a continuous "change" or self-unfolding driven by an internal principle. This distinguishes the living, active monad from a mere static point of matter.</summary>

<keywords>Herrenhausen, Electress Sophia, identity of indiscernibles, monad, internal change, movement vs change, inner principle, self-unfolding</keywords>

# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

Leibniz had said in the *Théodicée* <note>original: "Théodicée"; Leibniz’s 1710 work *Essais de Théodicée sur la bonté de Dieu, la liberté de l'homme et l'origine du mal*, which defends God's justice.</note> (396) "According to my view every simple substance, and that is every real substance, must be the real immediate cause of all its actions and of its internal passions <note>In 17th-century philosophy, "passions" refers to the passive states of a substance—what it undergoes or receives—as opposed to its "actions."</note>; indeed, to speak with metaphysical precision, it has no other actions and passions than those it itself produces." In this passage he is combatting the view that God is not only the cause of the existence of the monads by the creation of the world, but that he is also the immediate cause of their individual actions and passions. He holds on the other hand that the universe consists of real natures and that the actions of the monads are the free expression of their free natures and that the wisdom or omniscience <note>All-knowingness; the capacity to know everything infinitely.</note> of God, not his power or omnipotence <note>All-powerfulness; the capacity to have unlimited power.</note>, accounts for the order of the universe.

**12. Besides the principle of change there must also be the differentiation of what changes and this is what gives simple substances their specific character and variety.**

There is no present state of a monad which can be taken by itself and compared with a past state. The reality of the monad is not static but **dynamic** <note>Refers to a system characterized by constant change, activity, or progress, rather than being fixed in place.</note>. Any specific quality it may have must belong therefore to a process and not to a state; and any variety it may have must have regard to the activity in its integral unity and depend on a degree in the process of activity. In modern phrase we might say the variety of the monads is a variation of rhythm in their progression.

<summary>Leibniz argues that monads are self-contained and self-driven, meaning their changes arise from their own internal nature rather than direct divine intervention. He defines the monad as a dynamic process where differences between substances are essentially variations in their "rhythm" or activity.</summary>

<keywords>Leibniz, Monadology, Théodicée, simple substance, omniscience, omnipotence, internal activity, dynamic reality, principle of change</keywords>

-># PERCEPTION<-

**13. This differentiation means that a manifold <note>A 'manifold' refers to a collection of many different things, states, or details existing together within a single unity.</note> is included in the unity, a complexity in the simple. For natural change progresses gradually, which means that something changes and something endures. In the simple substance <note>A "simple substance" is the basic, indivisible building block of reality, which Leibniz calls a monad.</note>, therefore, there must be a plurality of modifications and relations even though there are no parts.**

This completes Leibniz’s definition of the monad. As simple substance it is pure act, as activity it is indivisible and not resolvable into a succession of states. It does not follow that the monad has no manifoldness and no variety, for variety is inherent in the concept of activity.

**14. The fleeting state which encloses and represents a manifold in the unity, or in the simple substance, is no other than what we call *perception*. We must however distinguish *perception* from *apperception* <note>While "perception" is the internal representation of external things, "apperception" is the reflexive knowledge of that internal state—essentially, consciousness or self-awareness.</note> or consciousness, as will appear later. It is here that the Cartesians <note>The Cartesians were followers of René Descartes; they famously argued that the mind is defined strictly by conscious thought and that animals, lacking this consciousness, were merely mechanical machines.</note> have gone wrong. They have supposed that the perceptions we do not apperceive <note>That is, perceptions we are not consciously aware of, like the background noise of waves that we only notice when it stops.</note> count for nothing and this is why they hold that only minds are monads, and that neither beasts nor other <term>entelechies</term> <note>original: "entelechies"; from the Greek *entelecheia*, meaning to have a goal or completion within oneself. Leibniz uses it to describe primitive souls that have internal activity but lack high-level reasoning.</note> have souls. In common with ordinary opinion, they have fallen into the confusion of being unable to distinguish prolonged unconsciousness from actual death. This has led them**

->## APPERCEPTION ##<-

<page-num>49</page-num>

physical in the sense that it is the aspect of activity when we conceive activity not as mechanical movement but as inherent force. An active nature, that is, a substance, which develops activity from an internal principle and is not determined by outward compulsion, must perceive. This does not mean it must be conscious or aware of a reality confronting it, or that it must be self-conscious in the presence of an opposing force; it means that in the concept of a self-developing nature, the stage and range of its influence as it develops must be mirrored or reflected in the progressing action. In other words, activity must assume two aspects according as we have regard to its source in the nature of the <term>monad</term> <note>For Leibniz, a monad is the fundamental, indivisible unit of reality—a spiritual "atom" that contains its own history and future.</note>, or to its expression in the development of that nature, and the two are correlative. This is what Leibniz means by perception.

The error which Leibniz in this article charges to the <term>Cartesians</term> <note>The followers of René Descartes, who argued for a strict separation between the mind (thinking) and the body (matter).</note> is due to their failure to distinguish between what we today call life and knowledge. Perception with Leibniz is the character or quality of living activity, and living activity is found where there is no evidence or trace of the presence of reflective consciousness. The two substances of Descartes, thought and <term>extension</term> <note>A philosophical term meaning "occupying space." Descartes believed that the physical world was defined solely by its spatial dimensions, viewing it as a passive, mechanical machine.</note>, separated the activity of consciousness from the movement propagated in an inert extension. The difficulties which surround the modern use of the term "unconscious" depend on the same separation. The "unconscious" of modern psychology is suppressed, inhibited, automatically controlled consciousness; it is not the unconsciousness of inertia, and it is not the limit or zero of conscious activity, for it is conceived as force or forces which are not even latent. The difficulty of the modern usage is precisely the difficulty of Leibniz’s

# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ #

<page-num>50</page-num>

use of the word <term>perception</term>. Just as the unconscious is not the negation of consciousness but active consciousness in a state of inhibited expression, so perception is identical with <term>apperception</term> <note>While perception is an internal state representing external things, apperception is the conscious, reflective knowledge of that internal state—essentially "awareness."</note> in the nature of its activity, but apperception is the new quality or higher degree which reaches expression by reflexion of the activity on itself. The <term>Cartesians</term> <note>Followers of the philosophy of René Descartes, who famously held that the mind and body are two completely different substances.</note> made a clean cut of this difficulty. For them, there was only the opposition between the activity of conscious perception or thought and the inertia of <term>extension</term> <note>A term used to describe physical matter, defined simply by the fact that it occupies space.</note>. Movement was an externally initiated disturbance of extension, quantitatively constant. The only real activity was thought. This existed in the form of souls or thinking substances, not <term>entelechies</term> <note>A term derived from Greek meaning "having its end within itself." Leibniz used it to describe monads as self-contained units of activity that contain their own blueprints for development.</note>. It followed from the Cartesian principle that living creatures are explained by the mechanism of movement, and that in the human being the active soul is existentially distinct from the mechanically disposed extension, the body.

The immortality of the soul was thought by Descartes to be placed on a rational basis by the separation of soul and body, but if the essential attribute of the soul is apperception or consciousness, its immortality must depend on the continuity of this consciousness. Leibniz bases the immortality of the soul on the individuality and indestructibility and impenetrability of the <term>monad</term>.

In the final sentence of this section, Leibniz gives expression to his view that were the dissolution of the union of the soul and the body to be complete and sudden, it would imply the mortality or death of the soul equally with that of the body. The ground of this opinion is that as reality consists in activity, perfect inactivity would not be a state of natural immortality.

<summary>This page contrasts Leibniz’s view of the soul as an active, indestructible "monad" with the Cartesian view of the soul as merely a thinking substance distinct from the mechanical body. It explains that for Leibniz, immortality is rooted in the continuous internal activity of the soul rather than a simple separation from matter.</summary>

In this, though he does not refer to it directly, he may have had in mind the Pauline argument <note>A reference to the Apostle Paul’s theological argument in the New Testament (1 Corinthians 15) that the soul is never truly "naked" or body-less, but instead exchanges a mortal, "corruptible" body for an immortal, "incorruptible" one.</note> that the spirit, when the body dies, exchanges a corruptible body for an incorruptible one. At the very least, he implies that the idea of a soul existing without any body is like the idea of a substance without any qualities or nature—a pure abstraction. 

Some light on his meaning may be obtained from a passage in the *New Essays on Human Understanding* <note>original: "Nouveaux Essais". This was Leibniz's book-length critique of John Locke’s philosophy, written as a dialogue between two characters.</note> (Book II, Chapter 23, 21). The character Philalethes <note>A fictional character representing the views of the English philosopher John Locke.</note>, representing Locke’s doctrine, says: “We have only to reflect on the separation of the soul from the body at death in order to be convinced of the soul’s movement.” Théophile <note>A fictional character representing Leibniz’s own views.</note>, representing Leibniz, replies: “The soul might cease to function in this visible body, and if it then should cease to think altogether, as Locke maintains, it could be separated from the body without being united to another body; in this case, its separation would be without movement. But for my part, I believe that as it always thinks and feels, so it is always united to some body, and indeed that it never quits suddenly and completely the body to which it is united.”

<term>Appetition</term> <note>From the Latin *appetitus*, meaning "desire" or "striving." In Leibniz’s philosophy, this is the internal principle that drives a monad to change from one perception to the next.</note> is the general term for what, in each monad, is effort. Since <term>monads</term> <note>The fundamental, indivisible units of reality in Leibniz’s system—the "atoms" of nature, but spiritual rather than material.</note> are forces, their activity is not simple, mechanically propagated movement, but rather a tendency or internal effort. It is because each monad receives the impressions of the tendencies or efforts of all the other monads that it represents the entire universe. In a letter to the Electress Sophia <note>Sophia of the Palatinate (1630–1714), a sophisticated intellectual and patron who was a frequent correspondent of Leibniz.</note> in 1706, Leibniz had said: “We know in fact that one and the same body can receive at the same time an infinite number of impressions, each of which has its effect; and in a mass of tendencies or efforts under pressure, the minutest part resists the efforts of all the rest, and this could not happen unless each received the im-” <note>The sentence breaks here and continues on the following page.</note>

15. The principle which brings about internal change in the monad itself, or makes it pass from one perception to another, is **appetition**. <term>Appetition: In Leibniz's system, this is the internal "striving" or drive that moves a monad from one thought or state to the next; it is the soul's equivalent of physical motion.</term> **Appetition** strives toward the completeness of perception and though it may fail to attain it, something is always gained and there is continual advance to new perceptions.

This internal principle of change is not only essential to Leibniz’s concept of the monad, but it marks a distinct advance on the **Cartesian principle**. <note>The Cartesian principle refers to the philosophy of René Descartes, who viewed the physical world as "extension" (taking up space) and required external intervention from God to explain change and the passage of time.</note> Perception, abstract and pure, has no duration. For external perception all nature exists at an instant, and according to the Cartesian principle extension only exists in this momentary way. Change, in the Cartesian physics, is the alteration of relative position within extension, due to the movement imparted to it. The continued existence of the universe from moment to moment, and the determination of one moment by the condition existing at the preceding moment, was attributed by **Descartes** to the direct action of God. Not only for the **Cartesians** is God the direct cause of the continued existence of the universe, but God is also the **occasional cause** <term>Occasionalism: A doctrine popular among followers of Descartes, like Malebranche, which argues that created things have no power to cause anything themselves; instead, God is the only true cause, acting on the "occasion" of a physical or mental event.</term> of the changes in the soul which correspond to the changes in the universe. Leibniz holds on the contrary that the act of creation, the act which brought the world into existence, was a creation of individual...

The difficulty in such a conception of the act of creation, namely that the future of such a universe could not be foreseen, was met by an appeal to the divine attribute of omniscience and led to the theory of the **pre-established harmony**. <term>Pre-established harmony: Leibniz’s theory that God perfectly synchronized every soul and physical object at the moment of creation, so they appear to interact despite being independent, like two clocks kept in perfect time.</term>

16. We ourselves experience multiplicity in a simple substance since the least thought of which we can be conscious embraces a variety in its object. Thus all who acknowledge the soul to be a simple substance must recognize this multiplicity in the monad, and Monsieur Bayle <note>Pierre Bayle (1647–1706) was a prominent French philosopher and skeptic whose works often challenged the rationalist systems of his time.</note> ought not to have found any difficulty in this. # THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

Criticizing this doctrine Bayle <note>Pierre Bayle (1647–1706), a French philosopher known for his "Historical and Critical Dictionary," which famously used skepticism to point out contradictions in the rationalist systems of his time.</note> says:

> “I can understand how a dog passes immediately from pleasure to pain if while it is satisfying its hunger and devouring food someone strikes it with a stick. What I cannot understand is how it can be so constructed that at the moment when its body is struck its soul must feel pain independently of whether it is struck or not, and even though it should go on eating without disturbance or interruption.”

Further on and referring now to the human soul, he says:

> “It would be possible to understand that it should be so were we to suppose that a man's soul is not a mind but a legion of minds, each with its own functions which begin and end precisely as the changes which go on in the human body call for them, but, in this case, man's soul would not be a substance, it would be an <term>aggregate being <note>original: "ens per aggregationem"</note>: A philosophical term for something that is not a single unified thing, but merely a collection of parts, like a pile of stones or a flock of sheep.</term>, a mass or heap of substances just as material beings are. What we find the soul to be, however, is a unique being which produces now joy now sorrow; what we do not find is several beings one of which produces hope, another despair.”

It is probably to this criticism that Leibniz makes the reply that variety in a simple substance ought to present no difficulty seeing that it is what everyone experiences in his simplest thought.

->### MECHANISM<-

<page-num>55</page-num>

17. Perception, and all that it means, is not to be explained by reasons which hold good in *mechanics* <note>In the 17th century, 'mechanics' referred to the study of physical bodies in motion and the laws of impact, much like the workings of a clock.</note>. We cannot, that is to say, explain perception by figures <note>original: "figures"; here meaning geometric shapes or the spatial arrangement of parts.</note> and movements. Let us imagine a machine capable of thinking, feeling, and perceiving; let us conceive it magnified, still preserving its proportions, until we can fancy ourselves entering into it as into a mill. On going in we should see the interlocking parts, but we should not see anything which would explain a perception <note>This is the famous "Mill Argument." Leibniz suggests that no matter how much we understand the physical "gears" of the brain, we will never find the actual experience of "seeing red" or "feeling happy" within them.</note>. It is not in the *composite*, the *machine*, but in the *simple substance* <note>A "simple substance," or Monad, is an indivisible, non-physical entity that Leibniz believes is the true building block of the universe.</note> that we have to look for perception. In the simple substance the one and only thing which we could find would be perception, that is to say, perceptions and their changes. All internal actions of simple substances can only consist in perception.

The force of this distinction is emphasized in modern science in the inability ever to pass from physiology <note>The study of how biological bodies function.</note> to psychology <note>The study of the mind and behavior.</note>. We are able by reason of the great advance in neuro-cerebral physiology in recent times to enter the brain, in the very way which Leibniz imagined our entering into the mill. We are able to lay bare the whole mechanism of the brain, to follow its response to stimulation, its influence on the muscles and the control of them, the neural inhibitions and coordinations. We are nonplussed <note>Caught at a standstill or confused because an explanation is missing.</note> by the factor of perception. Perception is of paramount importance,

<header>THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ</header>

we see the utility of its function, yet the machine gives us no clue to its formation or to the nature of its existence. We can see exactly where perception intervenes and it seems as though it must be manufactured when and where it arises, and yet we cannot discover any process of its manufacture, or any kind of mechanical interaction which actually does, or conceivably could, produce it. Leibniz gives us the reason. Perception is the internal activity of a simple substance, or, as we might say, of an individual nature, it is not an effect produced by mechanical contrivances. Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), a French philosopher who famously argued that animals were merely complex machines without souls.</note> is right, we may imagine Leibniz to say, in denying perception to the automatic organism, wrong in failing to see that it is inseparable from the concept of internal activity.

18. Simple substances, or created monads, may all be named Entelechies <note>A term from Aristotle meaning "having its end within itself." It refers to something that is fully realized or contains its own purpose.</note>, because they have in them a certain perfection <note>original Greek: ἔχουσι τὸ ἐντελὲς, meaning "they possess completion" or "perfection."</note>. They have a self-sufficiency <note>original Greek: αὐτάρκεια (autarkeia); the ability to function independently without external aid.</note> which makes each a source of its own internal actions, and we may describe them as incorporeal automata <note>In the 17th century, an "automaton" was any self-moving machine. By "incorporeal," Leibniz means these "machines" are made of spirit or mind, not physical matter.</note>.

Leibniz here invokes the Aristotelian word *entelechy* to indicate that the internal activity of the simple substance is self-originated and self-contained. He refers us for the reason to a section (87) of his *Theodicy* <note>original French: 'Théodicée', Leibniz’s 1710 work defending God's justice.</note>. Aristotle, he there tells us, has given to the soul the generic name of entelechy or act. There are two kinds of act, the permanent and the successive. The permanent or enduring act is what Aristotle calls the form and he distinguishes forms into substantial and

<page-num>57</page-num>

<header>->SOULS<-</header>

accidental. The substantial form <note>In traditional philosophy, the "substantial form" is the core essence that makes a thing what it is; Leibniz argues the human soul is the ultimate example of this.</note>, of which the human soul is an example, is absolutely permanent (this at least Leibniz tells us is his own view), the accidental form <note>Qualities that can change, like temperature or position, without the substance itself ceasing to exist.</note> is only permanent for a time. The passing act, the act whose nature is transitory, consists in the very action itself. What endows the term *entelechy* <note>A term borrowed from Aristotle, meaning a thing that contains its own purpose or "end" within itself.</note> with its peculiar significance and attraction for Leibniz is that it enables him to designate a permanent substantial form which carries with it not merely a simple faculty of being active but also what he calls force, effort, *conatus* <note>original: "conatus" — Latin for "striving" or "effort." Leibniz uses this to describe an inner "push" or tendency toward action.</note>, something which, while not itself action, leads to action, and something from which action must follow if there be nothing to stop it. Force if it is not derivative but primitive <note>A "primitive" force is one that is fundamental to the soul’s nature, rather than being a temporary effect or something added from the outside.</note> (as it is in the soul) is ingredient in the substance itself and not a detachable quality of it.

19. If we give the name *soul* to whatever has perceptions and appetitions <note>The internal drives or "appetites" that move a soul from one perception to the next.</note>, in the general meaning which I have just explained, then all simple substances, or created monads <note>From the Greek "monas," meaning "unit" or "one." For Leibniz, these are the indivisible, spiritual building blocks of reality.</note>, may be named souls. But because feeling is something more than a simple perception I prefer to use the general term monad or entelechy for simple substances which have only perception, and to keep the name *soul* for simple substances whose perception is more distinct and accompanied by memory.

Leibniz reaches the concept of the soul and its activities by a metaphysical deduction <note>A logical argument based on first principles about the nature of being, rather than on physical observation.</note> and not by a psychological investigation of human experience.

<page-num>58</page-num>

# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

The metaphysical motive is essential to the understanding of the doctrine. Memory quite as much as perception <note>In Leibniz's philosophy, "perception" refers to the internal state of a monad representing external things, not necessarily conscious awareness.</note> is deduced from the concept of the monad. Memory is not different in nature from perception. It is not a faculty of recalling the past or a power of manufacturing images wherewith to furnish the mind. It implies no psychological theories of association whatever logical laws it may be found to subserve. Memory, for Leibniz, is nothing other than perception of which it is a higher degree. The possession of this higher degree marks its possessor as belonging to an order of monads nobler in dignity and more exalted in the hierarchy. When a monad's perceptions are accompanied by memory its perceptions then not only coexist in the moment in which they come to birth and death, they also succeed one another continuing the past into the present. The <term>rational monad</term> <note>Also referred to as a "spirit," these are monads (like human souls) capable of reason and consciousness, distinguishing them from basic "simple monads."</note> perceives an order of coexistences <note>Leibniz defines space not as a "thing," but as the "order of coexistences"—how things exist together at once.</note> and an order of successions <note>Leibniz defines time as the "order of successions"—the sequence in which perceptions follow one another.</note> in its perceptions.

> **20.** We may experience in ourselves a state in which we remember nothing and are without any differentiations in our perception, as when we faint or are overcome by deep dreamless sleep. In such a state our soul is in no way different from the simple monad. Yet since such a state is not lasting, and since the soul recovers from it, the soul is more than a simple monad.

Leibniz here points to the significance of the fact that in our own individual experience we can refer to states of perception in which discrimination is zero.

**Key Terms:**
*   **Leibniz:** Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716), the philosopher who proposed the Monadology.
*   **Monadology:** The theory that the universe is made of "monads"—simple, indivisible substances.
*   **Monad:** From the Greek *monas*, meaning "unit"; the fundamental building block of reality.
*   **Perception:** The internal representation of the universe held by a monad.
*   **Memory:** A heightened state of perception that allows a monad to link past states to the present.
*   **Rational monad:** A monad with consciousness and reason (a soul/spirit).
*   **Simple monad:** A basic substance with only "slumbering" or unconscious perceptions.
*   **Coexistences:** The relational structure of things existing at the same time (Space).
*   **Successions:** The relational structure of things happening one after another (Time).

<summary>This page explains how memory distinguishes higher souls from simple monads by allowing for the continuity of time and consciousness. It features Section 20 of the Monadology, which uses fainting and sleep as examples of "simple" perception in humans.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, Monadology, memory, perception, rational monad, simple monad, coexistences, successions, consciousness, metaphysics</keywords>

## CONFUSED PERCEPTIONS

and yet not nothing. We need have no difficulty therefore in conceiving the perception of the simple monad <note>Leibniz’s term for the fundamental, indivisible units of reality. Every "thing" is ultimately composed of these centers of force.</note>. In his fully developed theory of the soul there is something positive and not merely negative in the confusedness of perception. The passions <note>Leibniz uses "passions" to describe states where the soul is "passive" or acted upon, which he equates with having unclear or "confused" thoughts.</note> which enslave the soul are confused perceptions. 

> "According to my system everything whatever which occurs in the soul depends only on itself, its future state follows only from itself and its present state, how then can there be a greater independence than it enjoys? And yet it is also true that there is some imperfection in the constitution of the soul. Everything which happens to the soul depends on the soul but it does not always depend on its will; that would be too absolute. Everything is not indeed even fully known or distinctly apperceived <note>To "apperceive" is to perceive something with conscious awareness or self-reflection. Leibniz distinguishes this from mere "perception," which can be unconscious.</note>. There is in the soul an order of distinct perceptions and this gives it its domination, but then there is also a range of confused perceptions or passions which puts it in bondage. There is nothing surprising in this for had the soul only distinct perceptions it would be a divinity." (*Theodicy* <note>Original: *Théodicée*, Leibniz's 1710 work examining why evil exists in a world created by a good God.</note>, 64)

Leibniz uses this to describe a state where a soul's perceptions are so confused and numerous that none stand out, similar to being in a deep, dreamless sleep or a faint.</note></term>, just as when we turn continually in the same direction several times together there comes on a giddiness which may make us faint and leave us unable to distinguish anything. A scientific concept of death (such as exists for modern biology with its theory of the continuity of life through successive generations of individuals, and its rejection of the theory of <term>spontaneous generation<note>The now-discredited theory that living organisms could arise spontaneously from non-living matter, such as maggots appearing from rotting meat.</note></term>), was non-existent for Leibniz. When he speaks of death, he means the word to be taken in its ordinary meaning as the complete cessation of a specified kind of organized activity which is followed by the dissolution of the organism. In this sense he finds nothing contradictory or even incredible in the notion that animals may actually die and come to life again. It was commonly supposed, for example, before the fact of the migration of birds was observed, that the swallows concealed themselves underground during the winter, literally dead, and coming to life again in the spring <note>A common folklore belief in the 17th and 18th centuries to explain the seasonal disappearance of birds before migration patterns were scientifically understood.</note>. In the case of insects it was hardly doubted at all. One is reminded of the words of Jesus in the fourth gospel:

> "Truly, truly, I say unto you, except a grain of wheat fall into the ground and die, it remains alone: but if it die, it brings forth much fruit." <note>John 12:24. Original Latin: *Nisi granum frumenti cadens in terram mortuum fuerit, ipsum solum manet: si autem mortuum fuerit, multum fructum affert.*</note>

Also the answer of the Apostle Paul to the imagined skeptic concerning the belief in the resurrection of the body:

> "You fool, that which you sow is not brought to life, unless it dies." <note>1 Corinthians 15:36. Original Latin: *Insipiens, tu quod seminas non vivificatur, nisi prius moriatur.*</note>

In these passages death is conceived as a state or stage of a continuous...

active process. Leibniz points out the metaphysical implication. If <term>substance<note>In Leibniz’s philosophy, a "substance" (or monad) is the fundamental building block of reality. Unlike a physical atom, it is an individual, active center of force and consciousness.</note></term> be conceived as force or activity then there can be no such thing as death in the absolute meaning, for in the absolute meaning death would mean annihilation and this would negative the essential attribute of substance itself, that is to say, it would negative the reality of the real, the existence of the existent.

->22. Every present state of a simple substance is a<-
->natural consequence of its precedent state,<-
->so that the present is big with the future.<-

We are to notice here the essential difference between Leibniz's concept of the continuity of an active force and the <term>mechanistic<note>A worldview, common in the 17th century, that compares the universe to a machine (like a clock) where every movement is caused by one part physically pushing another.</note></term> concept of continuity as an interlocking system of events determined by external relations of cause and effect. “It is one of the rules of my system of the general harmony, that the present is big with the future, that whoever sees all sees what will be in what is.” (<term>Theodicy<note>Original French: *Théodicée*. This refers to Leibniz’s 1710 work, *Essays on Theodicy on the Goodness of God, the Freedom of Man and the Origin of Evil*, in which he justifies the existence of a perfect God despite the presence of suffering.</note></term>, 360). The perfect knowledge of God, according to Leibniz, is not that of a mathematician, possessed of all the factors and able to calculate any future state of the universe from its present state, in the manner of a skilled astronomer foretelling an eclipse; it is perfect knowledge of the kind a psychologist would possess who, by knowing the nature of a conscious being, could foretell his behaviour and thereby determine before-hand what his actions will be under given conditions.

23. When we awake from a stunned condition we become conscious of, or <term>apperceive<note>In Leibniz’s philosophy, "apperception" is the act of becoming aware of one’s own internal states. It is the transition from a subconscious feeling to a conscious thought.</note></term>, perceptions; it is evident therefore that they were existent immediately before, even though we were not then conscious of them: for a perception can only come naturally from another perception, as a movement can only come naturally from a movement.

The point insisted on here is that the perceptions of the <term>monad<note>The fundamental "building block" of Leibniz's universe. Unlike physical atoms, monads are centers of force and consciousness with no "windows" to the outside; everything they experience comes from within.</note></term> arise and produce one another by reason of the monad’s nature and not by its power of free creation. They are not dependent on volition <note>The faculty or power of using one's will.</note>. They come naturally from one another just as one movement naturally gives rise to others. The full expression of this doctrine is in the *Theodicy* <note>original: "Théodicée", Leibniz's 1710 work defending the justice of God.</note>. It comes in reply to an objection of Bayle <note>Pierre Bayle (1647–1706), a French philosopher known for his skeptical dictionary that challenged many religious and philosophical certainties of the time.</note> who had argued that it is impossible to form ideas unless we know how ideas are formed, a sceptical argument which cuts at the root of any theory of perception. Leibniz replied: “We do not form our ideas because we will to form them; they are formed in us and by us not in consequence of our will but according to our nature and to the natures of things. They are formed in us as the <term>fœtus<note>A Latin term for offspring; Leibniz uses biological metaphors to show how ideas "grow" out of previous states.</note></term> is formed in the animal, as a thousand other marvels are produced by a kind of instinct placed by God in nature, that is to say, by virtue of the divine preformation which has created these <term>automata<note>Self-operating machines. Leibniz views the soul as a "spiritual automaton" that acts out its internal program perfectly.</note></term> that produce of themselves mechanically such marvellous effects. It is easy to think of souls as being just such spiritual automata, only far more wonderful, and that it is by the divine preformation that our soul produces the

# REPRESENTATION
<page-num>63</page-num>

> ideas in which our will has no part and to which our art cannot attain. The working of such <term>spiritual automata<note>Leibniz describes the soul as a "spiritual automaton" because it acts according to its own internal laws and "programming" rather than being pushed by outside physical forces.</note></term>, that is to say of souls, is not indeed mechanical, yet it does contain within it eminently all that is wonderful in mechanics. The movements developed in the body are concentrated in the soul by <term>representation<note>In this context, representation is the soul's ability to mirror or express what is happening in the physical world through its own internal states.</note></term>, forming as it were an ideal world which expresses the laws of the actual world and their consequences. There is however this difference between the perfect ideal world which exists in God and the ideal world of the soul, that the greater part of the soul’s ideal world is <term>confused perception<note>Feelings or sensations that are crowded together and not yet understood clearly by the mind.</note></term>. For it is essential to understand, that every <term>simple substance<note>Another name for a monad—the fundamental, indivisible unit of reality.</note></term> includes the universe in its confused perceptions or feelings, that the succession of perception is regulated by the particular nature of the substance, yet always in a way which expresses the whole of <term>universal nature</term> ; and that every present perception tends to a new perception, just as every movement which it represents tends to another movement. It is impossible however that the soul should know its own nature distinctly, and <term>apperceive<note>To become consciously aware of a sensation or thought.</note></term> how this innumerable number of <term>small perceptions<note>original: "petites perceptions." These are the tiny, subconscious impressions that we do not notice individually, like the sound of a single drop of water in the roar of a waterfall.</note></term>, jammed or rather concentrated together, is there formed. To know this the soul would need to know perfectly the whole universe which it encloses, it would need to be a god.” (*Theodicy* <note>original: "Théodicée", Leibniz's 1710 work defending the justice of God and explaining the nature of the world.</note>, 403)

24. We may see by this that had we nothing differentiated in our perceptions, nothing so to speak, standing out in relief, nothing to give a distinctive flavour, we should be always in the stunned condition. Such is the state of the bare naked <term>monads<note>Monads that possess perception but lack memory or conscious awareness, existing in a state similar to a deep, dreamless sleep.</note></term>.

<meta>This page concludes the discussion from page 63 regarding the soul as a "spiritual automaton" and begins a more detailed explanation of how these simple substances, or monads, process information from the world through perception and sense organs.</meta>

->THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ<-
<page-num>64</page-num>

This concludes the doctrine of the nature of **substance** as <term>active force<note>Leibniz’s revolutionary idea that the fundamental "stuff" of the universe is not passive matter, but a kind of internal energy or power to act.</note></term>. What is especially worthy of note is the way in which the <term>dualism<note>The traditional view, held by followers of Descartes, that the mind and the body are two completely different and separate things.</note></term> of the <term>Cartesians<note>Followers of the philosopher René Descartes.</note></term> is replaced by a **double aspect theory** of substance. In realizing mind we idealize nature, and in idealizing nature we realize mind.

**Perception** is practically a physical thing, for it is universal and does not imply consciousness. **Consciousness** is not different from perception in kind, and strictly speaking it is not even different in degree; it is <term>apperception<note>A higher level of awareness. While "perception" is just the internal state of reflecting the world, "apperception" is the mind's ability to be conscious of that reflection—knowing that you are perceiving.</note></term>, which is a reflexion of perception, a perception of perception. Again, there is no division between the **real** and the **ideal**, no separation between the world which exists in actuality and the world which exists in ideal representation.

The <term>monad's<note>The fundamental, indivisible "atoms" of reality in Leibniz’s system. They are not physical atoms, but centers of force and perception.</note></term> perceptions *are* the universe, not perceptions *of* the universe. That is to say, there are not two things for the monad—its perceptions of the universe and the universe it perceives—there is only one thing: **its universe**. The universe-in-itself is the monads, each of which exists in itself and for all the others.

25. Moreover, we see that nature has given animals some perceptions which "stand out in relief" <note>This refers to perceptions that are more distinct or vivid than the "confused" background noise of the universe.</note>, for it has provided them with **sense organs**. These organs concentrate numberless rays of light or numberless sound waves of air so that, by being brought to a focus, they may be made more <term>efficacious<note>Effective or powerful; here, it means the organs amplify the world’s signals so they are strong enough for the animal to notice.</note></term>. There is something approaching the same contrivance in the senses of odour, taste, and touch, and possibly in a number of other senses unknown...

<summary>Leibniz argues that mind and nature are two sides of the same substance and distinguishes between simple perception and conscious "apperception." He explains that sense organs act as biological tools to concentrate and clarify the perceptions of the soul.</summary>
<keywords>active force, dualism, Cartesians, apperception, monad, sense organs, perception vs consciousness</keywords>

<page-num>65</page-num>

### THE ORIGIN OF IDEAS

...to us. Further on I will explain how what happens in the soul represents what goes on in the sense organs.

It is interesting to compare this account of sense perception with the contemporary theory of <term>Locke<note>John Locke (1632–1704), an English philosopher who believed that all knowledge comes from sensory experience.</note></term>, which <term>Leibniz<note>Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716), a German philosopher and mathematician who argued that the mind has innate structures and is not just a passive receiver.</note></term>, some years before he wrote the *Monadology*, had submitted to detailed criticism in the *New Essays* <note>original: "Nouveaux Essais"</note>, but had withheld from publication and set aside because Locke, before he could reply, had died. In Locke's view the mind is a **blank slate** <note>original: "tabula rasa"; the idea that the mind is born without built-in mental content.</note>; the causes of knowledge are external, independently existing material substances; the objects of knowledge are ideas, and the origin of ideas is the qualities of objects which by affecting the sense organs leave impressions on the mind. Taken in the reverse order, the impressions on the mind give rise to ideas, which are the immediate objects of the mind when it thinks; these ideas are sense perceptions, and sense perceptions arise through affections of the sense organs by the various sensible qualities of external objects. 

The dilemma in such a theory, which is obvious to us and which was exposed by <term>Berkeley<note>George Berkeley (1685–1753), an Irish philosopher who argued that "to be is to be perceived," meaning that material things do not exist outside of the mind.</note></term>, (the dilemma, of which the one horn is that if the perception is not the real object of knowledge then the real object of knowledge is unknown, and the other horn is that if the perception is the real object of knowledge there is no object existing independently of its being known) is avoided altogether by Leibniz. The universe for Leibniz is the perceptions of the mind. Knowing is **apperceiving**, that is, becoming aware of the perceptions which exist already and which in their very nature are possible ingredients of consciousness.

<summary>This page compares Leibniz's philosophy with Locke's "blank slate" theory, explaining that Leibniz avoids the problem of whether "real" objects exist outside our minds by defining the universe as the mind's own perceptions.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, John Locke, George Berkeley, tabula rasa, New Essays, apperception, sense perception, theory of knowledge</keywords>

--- Page 80 ---
<meta>This page continues the analysis of how the soul processes information. While the previous page contrasted Leibniz’s internal "innate ideas" with John Locke’s "blank slate" theory, this page focuses on the physical role of the sense organs and the nature of memory in animals compared to human reason.</meta>

-># THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ<-
<page-num>66</page-num>

The role that our sense organs play is not to produce or even reshape our perceptions. Instead, their job is to concentrate, focus, and select information. By doing this, they heighten our experiences to provide clarity and distinctness to what would otherwise be a confused, overwhelming mass of sensations.

26. **Memory** provides <term>souls<note>In this context, Leibniz refers to "monads" or simple substances that have perception and memory, including those of animals.</note></term> with a kind of **consecutiveness** <note>A sequence of events where one thing follows another based on habit or experience rather than logical deduction.</note> that imitates reason, yet we must be careful to distinguish it from true reason. We see this in action when we observe animals noticing something they have perceived before. They are led by the representation in their memory to expect that whatever was joined with that perception in the past will happen again. This leads them to have feelings similar to the ones they had before. For example: if we threaten a dog with a stick, it will remember the pain that the stick once caused and will howl and run away.

Because of his **metaphysical** <note>Philosophy concerning the fundamental nature of reality, including the relationship between mind and matter.</note> concept of reality as timeless, <term>Leibniz<note>Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716), a German philosopher who argued that the universe is made of "monads," or simple soul-like substances.</note></term> is spared the specific difficulty that modern psychology faces when trying to define memory. For a psychologist, "perception" is defined by its connection to an existing object, while "memory" refers to the past, which no longer exists. Therefore, "perceiving the non-existent" seems like a contradiction, yet that is exactly what memory claims to do. For Leibniz, however, the past is not non-existent; it lives on in the present, which is <note>original: "le présent est gros de l'avenir"; a famous Leibnizian principle suggesting that every current state of a being contains the seeds of all its future developments.</note> **big with the future**. Perceptions therefore—

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Point 26 regarding memory and transitions into Point 27 of Leibniz's Monadology. It concludes the thought that memory is the perception of continuous experience, whereas reason is the logical application of principles to reality.</meta>

-># IMAGINATION<-
<page-num>67</page-num>

...perceptions do not simply arise and perish; rather, they are continued into one another. Memory is simply the ability to perceive this continuity—this preservation of the past and the sequential nature of our active experience. <term>Leibniz<note>Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716), a philosopher and mathematician who argued that the universe is composed of simple substances called monads.</note></term> is not concerned with defining memory itself, but rather with differentiating it from **reason**, which is the application of logical principles to the interpretation of reality.

27. The **strong imagination**, which has such a striking and disturbing effect on animals, comes either from the **multitude** <note>A large number of repeated experiences.</note> or from the **magnitude** <note>The great strength or intensity of a single experience.</note> of their previous perceptions. Frequently, a single forceful impression will suddenly produce the same effect that is usually the result of long habit or many repetitions of ordinary perceptions.

Imagination, for Leibniz, is not a "faculty" of the soul in the same way as reason or understanding. Here, he is focused on proving that it is not imagination or memory that distinguishes the human soul from the animal soul. Both of these can be explained in terms of **perception**, which is the universal activity of the <term>monad<note>The fundamental, indivisible unit of reality in Leibniz's philosophy; a "simple substance" that perceives the world from its own perspective.</note></term>. The mental image is not produced by *aesthetic* creation; on the contrary, it is a phenomenon that occurs because of an absence of "clear and distinct" perception. For example, the tree that I see is a single, blurred image precisely because I have not yet distinguished its individual component parts, such as the leaves, the stem, the roots, and so on.

<summary>This page distinguishes memory and imagination from human reason, explaining that animals also possess memory through the association of intense or frequent perceptions. Leibniz argues that imagination is actually a result of "confused" perception—seeing a whole (like a tree) without yet perceiving its individual parts.</summary>

<keywords>Leibniz, Monadology, imagination, memory, reason, perception, monad, animal soul, clear and distinct perception</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the comparison between human reason and animal behavior, specifically focusing on Points 28 and 29 of Leibniz's Monadology. -># THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ<-
<page-num>68</page-num>

28. Men act just like animals insofar as the **consecutiveness** <note>The way one thought or perception follows another in a sequence.</note> of their perceptions is due only to the principle of **memory**. They are like the **empirics** <term>empirics<note>In Leibniz's time, "empirics" referred to medical practitioners who relied solely on observation and trial-and-error rather than understanding the underlying scientific theories or causes.</note></term> who have a simple practice without theory. We are all empirics as to three-fourths of our actions. When we expect the sun to rise tomorrow, for example, we are simply acting empirically, for our expectation has no other ground than that up to now it has always been so. It is only the astronomer who takes the trouble to know the **reason why**.

The true distinction between man and the animals is not that in man a substantial, sentient and rational soul is united to a material organism, as the **Cartesians** <term>Cartesians<note>Followers of the philosopher René Descartes, who argued that animals were merely complex machines without souls, whereas humans possessed a soul joined to a body.</note></term> held. Perception, memory, imagination, association of ideas, and learning by experience are found in animals as well as in man, and the difference is not in the degree or quantity or quality of these characteristics. Man is **rational**, and this character of reason places him in a higher order of being. In man there is a **spiritual nature** and the possibility of a spiritual life. Man has, and the animal has not, a **rational soul**.

29. It is the knowledge of **necessary and eternal truths** <note>Fundamental principles of logic, math, and ethics that are always true, regardless of circumstances.</note> which distinguishes us from simple animals and, in endowing us with **reason** and with the **sciences**, raises us to the knowledge of ourselves and of God. This is what in ourselves we name **rational soul** or **mind**.

<summary>Leibniz argues that while humans often act out of habit and memory like animals, the "rational soul" allows us to understand the underlying reasons for things and grasp eternal truths. This capacity for reason and science is what elevates human beings and allows for the knowledge of God and the self.</summary>

# THE RATIONAL SOUL

Each human monad is a rational soul or mind, capable of reflection, and we now proceed to consider the nature and mode of working of reason in man. The first indication of its reality is the knowledge we find we possess of **necessary and eternal truths**. <note>Leibniz refers to truths that must be true in all possible worlds, such as logic and math, which do not depend on physical experience.</note> This knowledge transcends experience, which the simple animal soul cannot do. The propositions of mathematics are necessary and eternal truths. The multiplication table, for example, is not discovered by the human individual empirically <note>By observation or trial and error.</note>. As soon as it is understood it is seen not to depend on observed uniformities, but to lay down a rule to which all experience will necessarily conform.

<margin>✓</margin>
<margin>30.</margin> 30. It is by the knowledge of necessary truths, and by abstractions from them, that we rise <margin>{</margin>to the stage of **reflective acts**, which give us the thought of what is called **I**, and which lead us to think that this or that is within us. Thus it is that in thinking of ourself we think of **being**, **substance**, the **simple** or the **composite**, the **immaterial**, and even God himself, for in conceiving what is limited in us, we conceive what is without limits in God. These reflective acts furnish the chief objects of our reasonings.

In this article it is clear that Leibniz has in mind the **Cartesian principle** <term>Cartesian principle<note>Referring to René Descartes' famous statement "Cogito, ergo sum" (I think, therefore I am), which establishes the thinking self as the foundation of all knowledge.</note></term> of the "I think therefore I am" as the starting point of reasoning, and he points out how his own theory goes behind this principle. The *I think* of the proposition is an **apperception** <term>apperception<note>Conscious awareness of one's own perceptions; not just seeing something, but being aware that *you* are the one seeing it.</note></term> and

<summary>This page explains that human reason is defined by the grasp of eternal truths (like math) and the ability to reflect on the self, which allows humans to understand concepts like substance, the soul, and God.</summary>

### THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

**apperception** <note>In Leibniz's philosophy, apperception refers to self-conscious awareness, as opposed to simple perception, which can be unconscious.</note> presupposes perception. *I think* in its simplicity and immediacy is not a reflective act. To be reflective it must take some such form as “I think myself thinking” or even the more direct “I think about thinking” <note>original: "Cogito cogitare"</note>. The “I think” must be the thought thought before the “I am” (the idea of being or substance) can arise.

31. Our reasonings are based on two great principles. The first is the **Principle of Contradiction**. By virtue of this we judge the self-contradictory to be false, and its opposite or that which contradicts the false to be true.

The Principle of Contradiction declares that of two contradictory propositions one is false and the other is true. The opposite or contrary of a false proposition is not necessarily true, but the contradictory of a false proposition is true. For example: if the proposition “this paper is white” is false, then the proposition “this paper is not white” is true; but it does not therefore follow, because black is the contrary to white, that the proposition “this paper is black” is true. <note>This is a fundamental rule of logic: while "white" and "not-white" cover every possibility, "white" and "black" do not, as the paper could be red or blue.</note> The connection of this sentence with the previous one seems to indicate the source from which Leibniz derives this logical principle. It is from the act of **reflection**. This is more evident when we take the principle in the form of the **judgment of Identity**, A is A, or, A is not not-A. To have significance and be raised from pure **tautology** <note>A statement that is true by necessity but provides no new information, like saying "a circle is round."</note> it must suppose the reflection of the subject of the proposition on itself.

<summary>This page explains that self-consciousness arises from the act of thinking and introduces the Principle of Contradiction as a foundation of human reasoning. It clarifies the logical difference between contradictory and contrary statements, rooting these laws of logic in the human ability to reflect upon ourselves.</summary>

<keywords>apperception, reflection, Principle of Contradiction, logic, identity, tautology, self-awareness, Leibniz</keywords>

# THE TWO LOGICAL PRINCIPLES

->F<-

32. The second is the **Principle of Sufficient Reason**. <note>This is the philosophical idea that everything must have a reason, cause, or ground.</note> By virtue of this we think that no fact can be real or existent, and no statement true, unless it have a sufficient reason why it should be thus and not otherwise, notwithstanding that in most cases these reasons cannot be known by us.

This is the principle which forbids us to regard anything as due to pure chance. It tells us that whatever happens has a cause, however ignorant we may be of the cause, or however impossible for us its discovery may be. It is this principle which has been submitted to the most continuous criticism from Leibniz’s time to the present day. It seems to us that unless science adopts it, at least as a postulate <note>A claim assumed to be true as a basis for reasoning.</note>, science is itself impossible; and yet it is impossible to derive from matters of fact or from science itself any rationality of the principle.

This is how Leibniz describes the principle in the *Theodicy* <note>original: "Théodicée"; a 1710 work by Leibniz exploring the "justice of God" and the problem of evil.</note> (Section 44).

> “The principle is that nothing happens without a cause or at least a determining reason why it happens, something, that is to say, which can offer a reason from the start <note>original: "a priori"</note> why it exists in this fashion rather than in any other. This great principle operates in regard to all events and there is no case of the contrary. And although most often these determining reasons are very imperfectly known we can never think they are non-existent. Without this principle not only should we be unable to prove the existence of God, we should be deprived of an infinite number of the soundest and most useful arguments of which the principle is the only basis. It admits moreover no...”

<summary>This page defines Leibniz’s Principle of Sufficient Reason, arguing that every event has a cause—even if unknown—and asserting its necessity for both scientific reasoning and the proof of God’s existence.</summary>

33. There are also two sorts of truths, *truths of reasoning* and *truths of fact*. The truths of reasoning are necessary, and their opposites are impossible. The truths of fact are contingent <note>In philosophy, "contingent" refers to something that happens to be true but could have been otherwise without a logical contradiction.</note> and their opposites are possible. When a truth is necessary we can find its reason by <term>analysis</term> <note>Analysis: The process of breaking a complex idea down into its simplest parts to understand how it works.</note>, by resolving it into ideas and into simpler truths until we come to primaries <note>The most basic, foundational ideas that are self-evident and cannot be broken down further.</note>.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;We are now introduced to the important distinction between truths of reasoning and matters of fact, which in one form or another has occupied and still underlies all philosophical discussion. There are some propositions which we judge true because their falsehood is impossible, there are others which we judge true (and of course necessarily true) although no logical impossibility is implied in their falsehood. If two straight lines are radii of the same circle they are of equal length. If a lighted match be thrown into a barrel of gunpowder there will be an explosion. So far as belief and practice founded on belief are concerned there is no difference between the truth of these two propositions and the inconceivability of their

34. Thus in mathematics, speculative theorems and practical canons are reduced by <term>analysis</term> to definitions, axioms, and postulates.

The two methods are divergent. In the case of truths of reasoning, we proceed by the analysis of concepts. As Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650) was a French philosopher and mathematician who argued that we can understand shapes by the way they are "generated" or drawn.</note> had already made clear, the definitions and properties of geometrical figures are obtained from the act by which they are generated. Thus, the equality of the radii of a circle follows as a necessary analytical deduction of the mode in which the circle is constructed—that is, by rotating the point of the free arm of a pair of compasses round the point of the other fixed at the centre.

They are the possession of the rational monad <note>Monad: In Leibniz's system, a monad is a simple, indivisible substance that makes up the universe. # EFFICIENT AND FINAL CAUSE

Into the efficient cause <note>Efficient cause: The physical force or action that brings something about—essentially the "how" an event occurs.</note> of my present action in writing there enter an infinite number of postures and movements present and past, and into the final cause <note>Final cause: The purpose, goal, or intention behind an action—the "why" an event occurs.</note> of this same present action there enter an infinite number of small inclinations and dispositions of my soul present and past.

A contingent fact <note>Contingent fact: An event that happens to be true in our world, but is not logically "necessary"—meaning it is possible to imagine a world where it didn't happen.</note> is determined but not necessary. My present writing, Leibniz would say, is a determined fact with its sufficient reason <note>Sufficient reason: Leibniz’s famous principle that nothing happens without a reason why it should be so rather than otherwise.</note> connecting it causally with the past, and also it is itself determining the future into which it is entering as a sufficient reason of other contingent facts. God by his perfect knowledge of the sufficient reason could foreknow the contingent fact of my present writing. Yet though this contingent fact could have been foreknown, and therefore was certain to exist, it is not a necessary existence because no contradiction is involved in denying the existence of the fact, that is, in conceiving that at this moment when I am writing I should not be writing. In fact it is of the essence of the fact that its non-existence should be as conceivable as its existence.

<meta>This page continues the argument from the previous page concerning "truths of fact." It explains that because any chain of causes in the physical world could go on forever (an infinite regress), the final explanation for why anything exists at all must sit outside of that chain. This leads into Leibniz's definition of God as the "necessary substance" and the source of all "contingent" things.</meta>

...this detail of contingencies <note>Contingencies: Events or facts that happen to be true in our world, but are not logically "necessary"—we can imagine a world where they didn't happen.</note>, the last or ultimate reason, lies beyond the series, however infinite the series be.

This is the argument on which Leibniz depends for his theory of creation. It follows from the consideration of the infinite regress <note>Infinite regress: A sequence of reasoning or causes that goes back forever without ever reaching a starting point.</note> of contingencies that the first cause cannot be a first sufficient reason of the first contingent fact, it must be a cause which transcends the series. This cause is the bringing into existence by a creative fiat <note>Fiat: A formal decree or act of will; specifically, God’s command to bring the world into being.</note> the whole universe of monads <note>Monads: The "simple substances" or soul-like building blocks that Leibniz believes make up the entire universe.</note>. The act of creation is single because though the contingencies are a multiplicity the sufficient reason of one contingent fact involves the sufficient reason of all.

<margin>x</margin>
38. Thus it is that the ultimate ground of things must be in a necessary substance <note>Necessary substance: A being that must exist by its very nature, whose non-existence would be a logical contradiction.</note>, in which the detail of the changes can only exist eminently <note>Eminently: A technical term meaning that a cause possesses a quality in a higher, more perfect form than the effect it produces.</note>, as in the source. This necessary substance is what we name God.

As this challenges comparison with Spinoza's concept of the one substance <note>Spinoza's substance: Unlike Leibniz, who believed in many individual "monads," Baruch Spinoza argued there is only one single substance in the universe, which he identified as God or Nature.</note> it is important to see wherein Leibniz differs. In the *Theodicy* <note>original: "Théodicée"—Leibniz's 1710 work defending God's justice.</note> he says (7),

> "God is the primary ground of things: for things which are limited, as is everything we see and experience, are contingent and have nothing in them which makes their existence a necessity. It is obvious that time, space and matter, single and uniform in themselves and altogether indifferent, might have totally different movements and shapes and these in a different order from that which they actually have. We have then, to seek the ground of the existence of..."

<summary>Leibniz argues that the "ultimate reason" for the world's existence must be a "necessary substance" (God) existing outside the infinite chain of cause and effect. He distinguishes his view from Spinoza's by presenting God as a transcendent creator who chose this specific world from many possibilities.</summary>
