# THE DIVINE SUBSTANCE
->77<-

the actual world, which is the complete assemblage of contingent things <note>Contingent things: Facts or objects that exist in our reality but could theoretically have been different or not existed at all without violating the laws of logic.</note>, and we must look for it in that substance which contains in itself the ground of its own existence and is consequently necessary and eternal. So too this cause must be intelligent; because the existing world is contingent and an infinite number of alternate worlds could equally claim existence without contradiction, the cause of the existence of this world must have had regard to all the possible worlds and their relations in order to decide on this one. This concern of an existing substance for simple possibilities or its relation with them can be no other than an understanding which has ideas of them, and the decision in favour of one can be nothing else but the act of will which chooses it. It is the power of this substance which renders its will efficacious <note>Efficacious: Having the power to produce a desired effect; here, it means God’s will is not just a wish but a force that brings the world into being.</note>. Its existence argues power, its truth wisdom and understanding, its goodness good-will. This intelligent cause must be infinite in all ways and absolutely perfect in power, wisdom and goodness, since it carries out completely what is possible. As all is bound up together there is no room for more gods than one. His understanding is the source of essences <note>Essences: The fundamental nature or "blueprints" of things; the logical possibilities of what could exist, held within the mind of God.</note>, his will the origin of existences. There in few words is the proof for the existence of a God with his perfections, and of the origin of things by him ".

It will be seen, therefore, that in basing the argument for God’s existence on the principle of sufficient reason <note>Principle of sufficient reason: The philosophical rule that nothing happens without a specific reason why it is "so" and not otherwise.</note> and not on the principle of contradiction <note>Principle of contradiction: A rule of logic stating that something cannot be both true and false at once. Leibniz argues instead that God is a separate, intelligent creator.</note>. # THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ
->78<-

There is no contradiction in attributing both understanding and will to God. These attributes are necessary, for they are not—as **Spinoza** <note>Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677), a philosopher who argued that God does not have a "will" or "purpose" in the human sense, but rather that everything follows from God's nature by logical necessity.</note> suggested—imperfections that would conflict with the concept of infinite perfection. The **principle of sufficient reason** <term>Sufficient Reason: The philosophical rule that nothing can exist or happen without a reason why it is so and not otherwise.</term> as applied to the world implies a positive understanding in the Creator in order to envision all ideal possibilities, and a good-will in order to actualize the best among those possibilities.

> 39. Now as this substance is a **sufficient reason** of all this detail, and as all this detail is linked together in one whole, there is only one God and this God suffices.

The persuasive force of this argument depends on three considerations: (1) the vast multiplicity of **contingent facts** <note>Contingent facts: Things that happen to be true in our world but could have been different without being logically impossible.</note>; (2) the unity of the sufficient reason which binds these facts into a single universe; and (3) the impossibility of reaching a final answer within the endless chain of details that scientific search discloses.

If human reason is justified in using the principle of sufficient reason to find the ultimate ground of the universe itself, and if this ground reveals a staggering multiplicity of detail, then clearly the "sufficient reason" intended to transcend this detail must be conceived as a **unity**, rather than just more detail. Yet, to conceive this "**unconditioned ground**" <term>Unconditioned Ground: A starting point or ultimate cause that does not depend on anything else for its existence.</term> as a unity is, in a sense, to place a condition upon the unconditioned. It may have been this very dilemma which led **Kant** <note>Immanuel Kant (1724–1804), who later argued that while we must think of God as a first cause, we cannot "prove" His existence through pure logic alone.</note> to deny the possibility of attaining certain theoretical knowledge of God through pure reason.

<summary>This page argues that God must possess intellect and will to choose the best of all possible worlds and concludes that there can only be one such supreme substance to account for the universe's unity.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, Monadology, Sufficient Reason, Spinoza, God, Kant, Unconditioned Ground, Unity of God, Divine Will</keywords>

# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

### GOD’S PERFECTION

40. We may also conclude that the supreme substance, which is unique, universal, and necessary; which has nothing outside it that can be independent of it; which is a simple consequence of possible being; must be incapable of limitation and must contain as much reality as possible.

Substance is here conceived not according to the philosophy of **Spinoza** <note>Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677) famously argued that God is the only substance and that all finite things (like people or trees) are merely "modes" or expressions of God. Leibniz disagrees, arguing instead that finite things have their own distinct natures.</note> as the reality of the finite beings which are modes of it, but as the reason which suffices for their existence. Finite things have their own natures, and therefore their reality, yet they are dependent on God for the **sufficient reason** <term>Sufficient Reason: The principle that there is a logical explanation for why every fact is as it is and not otherwise.</term> of their existence. One is reminded throughout this proof based on the principle of sufficient reason of **Pascal’s** <note>Blaise Pascal (1623–1662) was a French philosopher and mathematician. Leibniz here references Pascal's critique of Jesuit theology regarding "grace."</note> ironic description of the **sufficient grace** <note>A theological term for the divine help granted to humans; Pascal mocked the idea that God could give "sufficient" help that nevertheless failed to help the recipient.</note> which is insufficient. "Is this not something similar to that sufficiency which is not enough?" <note>original: "N'est-ce point ici quelque chose de semblable a cette suffisance qui ne suffit pas ?"</note>

41. Whence it follows that God is absolutely perfect; the perfection being nothing but the magnitude of precise positive reality when the limits or bounds to reality in the things which possess it are removed. Where reality is unbounded, that is to say in God, there is absolutely infinite perfection.

The idea which underlies the conception of the perfection of God in this section is that of the divine wisdom manifested in creation. Creation is the...

***

**Key Terms:**
*   **substance**: the fundamental "stuff" or foundation of reality.
*   **Spinoza**: a philosopher who viewed God and Nature as the same thing.
*   **sufficient reason**: the logical requirement for a cause or explanation.
*   **Pascal**: a philosopher who questioned the logic of religious "sufficiency."
*   **sufficient grace**: divine assistance that should be enough for salvation.
*   **perfection of God**: the state of having no limits on one's reality or power.
*   **creation**: the act of bringing the world into being.
*   **infinite perfection**: reality that has absolutely no boundaries.

<summary>Leibniz argues that God, as the unique and necessary substance, must be limitless and absolutely perfect, serving as the "sufficient reason" for all other things without absorbing them into his own identity.</summary>

bestowal of existence on natures which have limitations, this means that their reality is limited by possibility. The omniscience <term>Omniscience: The attribute of having complete or infinite knowledge.</term> of God has enabled him to realize the highest possibility.

42. It follows also that while creatures <note>In this context, "creatures" refers to all created things or "monads," not just living animals.</note> derive their perfections from the divine influence, they derive their imperfections from their own nature. This nature is incapable of existing without limitations. It is in this incapability of creatures to be infinite that they are distinguished from God.

In the translations of Latta and Montgomery, the following sentence is included in this section. "An instance of this *original imperfection* of created beings may be seen in the *natural inertia of bodies*." The authority for this would seem to be a note in Gerhardt’s edition of the *Philosophical Writings* <note>original title: *Philosophischen Schriften*</note> (VOL. VI.) "In the first copy revised by Leibniz, the following sentence is also found here: <note>original: "In der von Leibniz revidirten ersten Abschrift findet sich hier noch folgender Satz :"</note> *This original imperfection of creatures is observed in the natural inertia of bodies*." <note>original: "Cette imperfection originale des créatures se remarque dans l'inertie naturelle des corps."</note> Leibniz seems to be referring to an illustration which he had used in the *Theodicy* <note>A work written by Leibniz in 1710 to justify the goodness of God despite the existence of evil in the world.</note> (30) and also in other writings to elucidate his reconciliation of the existence of evil in the creature with moral perfection in the creator. The illustration is of vessels variously laden all of which are being transported on the same stream. The heavily laden move more slowly than the others, and this retardation is not due to anything lacking in the stream as the source of the movement but to something negative in the vessels themselves.

### THE SELF-CAUSED <note>original: *CAUSA SUI*</note>
->81<-

This completes the first proof of the existence of God. It is the argument from the conditioned universe to the unconditioned cause. It is in one essential the same as the argument of Spinoza, insofar, that is to say, as it affirms the first cause to be of necessity the <term>self-cause: The philosophical concept that God is the reason for His own existence (Latin: *causa sui*).</term> The unconditioned cause of the series of conditions cannot itself be resolved into one of the conditions in the series. Leibniz avoids the deterministic and pantheistic conclusion of Spinoza by an intellectual device. God is perfect intellect. Omniscience enables God to foresee the future actions of free spirits in the simple contemplation of their natures. Therefore in creating a world of free spirits he can by prevision foreordain their harmony.

<margin>A pencil bracket marks the first four lines of this paragraph.</margin>
43. It is true moreover that in God we have not only the source of existences but also of essences, insofar as essences are themselves the reality or stand for what is real in the possibility. This is because God's understanding is the region of the eternal truths or of the ideas on which they depend, and because without God there would be nothing real in the possibilities, not only nothing existent but nothing even possible.

The argument of this important section is that not only what is actual but also what is possible must have its source in something actual and real, and this source must be anterior both to existence and possibility. This source is the understanding of God, the <term>place: A conceptual region or seat of ideas (Latin: *locus*).</term> or region of the ideas anterior to their realization.

<summary>This page outlines Leibniz’s argument that God is the necessary "self-cause" and the ultimate source of both reality and the potential for things to exist. It explains that for anything to even be possible, it must first exist as an idea within the divine intellect.</summary>
<keywords>existence of God, Spinoza, self-cause, Leibniz, omniscience, essences, eternal truths, divine understanding</keywords>

### THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ
->82<-

It is by this distinction that Leibniz reconciles the existence of evil. God’s understanding is not something God has created; it is the source or ground of God’s actions. The eternal truths, not matter, are the source of things. God’s understanding is therefore the ideal cause of evil as well as good. <term>Evil: For Leibniz, evil is not a positive "thing" created by God, but rather a "privation" or lack of perfection in finite creatures.</term> Evil consists in privation. It has no efficient cause <note>An "efficient cause" is the agent or force that brings something into being; Leibniz argues no such agent "makes" evil.</note> but only an ideal cause. It is a kind of inertia, or resistance in the nature of things, which has to be overcome.

**44.** For if there be a reality in the essences or possibilities, or indeed in the eternal truths, this reality must necessarily depend on something already existent or actual, consequently on the existence of the necessary being whose essence includes existence, or in whom it is sufficient to be possible in order to be actual.

This is the second proof of the existence of God. It had been argued that eternal truths could exist independently of whether God did or did not exist, inasmuch as they are conceived as true of any possible world. Leibniz argues that they have no meaning except in reference to an understanding. A world not dependent on ideas would be a world without order. The fact that there is order in nature is the affirmation of mind.

**45.** Thus a privilege attaches to the concept of the necessary being, God, inasmuch as we can affirm of God, that if he be possible, he must

### ETERNAL TRUTHS
->83<-

...be. And God is possible, for nothing can hinder the possibility of that which is enclosed within no boundaries, has no negation, and consequently contains no contradiction. This possibility alone is sufficient for knowledge *prior to experience* <note>original: "a priori"; reasoning that proceeds from theoretical deduction or definitions rather than from observation.</note> of God's existence. We have already proven this existence *prior to experience* from the existence of eternal truths, and now we are able to prove it *from observed effects* <note>original: "a posteriori"; reasoning that works backward from effects in the world to their ultimate cause.</note> from the existence of contingent beings <term>Contingent beings: Things that exist but do not have to exist; their existence depends on something else.</term>, the ultimate or sufficient reason <term>Sufficient reason: The philosophical principle that nothing can exist or happen without a reason why it is so and not otherwise.</term> of which can only be the necessary Being—the Being who is himself the sufficient reason for his own existence.

This third proof of God's existence, encompassing as it does the other two, provides the absolute foundation of Leibniz's system. It is essentially identical to the general basis of metaphysical science as accepted by René Descartes, Baruch Spinoza, and other seventeenth-century philosophers. It is the vantage point from which Leibniz challenged Isaac Newton's doctrines regarding space and time, as well as John Locke's theories on how we acquire knowledge.

**46.** Yet we are not to suppose, as some philosophers have imagined, that because the eternal truths <note>Leibniz refers here to the laws of logic and mathematics, like 2+2=4, which he believes are true in all possible worlds.</note> are dependent on God, they are therefore arbitrary and dependent on his will. Descartes, and later Monsieur Poiret <note>Pierre Poiret (1646–1719), a French mystic and philosopher.</note>, appear to have taken this view. It is contingent truths <note>Facts about the physical world that could have been different, such as the color of a flower.</note> alone which can be said to depend on...

<summary>Leibniz argues that God’s existence is logically necessary to account for both the laws of logic and the existence of the physical universe. He maintains that while God is the source of all truth, logical laws are rooted in God’s understanding rather than being arbitrary choices of His will.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from paragraph 46, contrasting Leibniz's view of "eternal truths" (like logic and mathematics) with the views of René Descartes. While the previous page established God as the necessary foundation for all existence, this section clarifies that for Leibniz, these truths reside in God’s understanding rather than being subject to His arbitrary will.</meta>

# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

...God’s will, for the principle of these is fitness <note>original: "convenance"; Leibniz uses this term to describe the logic of "the best." Unlike necessary truths which *must* be true, "fit" truths are chosen by God because they result in the most perfect world.</note>, they imply power to choose the best. Necessary truths depend solely on God’s understanding of which they are the internal objects.

The opinion of Descartes <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the French philosopher famous for the statement "I think, therefore I am."</note> that the “eternal truths” are dependent on God’s will, is expressed in two very interesting passages in his correspondence. Writing in 1630 to Father Mersenne <note>Marin Mersenne (1588–1648), a French monk and mathematician who acted as a "clearinghouse" for scientific ideas, corresponding with almost every great thinker of his era.</note> he says: “In my Physics (referring almost certainly to the early work which had been withdrawn from publication) I have left untouched several metaphysical problems and this in particular: that the metaphysical truths which you call eternal have been established by God and depend entirely upon him just as all the rest of creation. To say that these truths are independent of him would be in effect to speak of God as we speak of a Jupiter or a Saturn <note>The Roman names for the Greek gods Zeus and Cronus.</note> and make him subject to the Styx and to the Fates <note>In classical mythology, the Styx was the river of the underworld by which gods swore unbreakable oaths, and the Fates were the three goddesses who controlled human destiny; even the gods were often depicted as being unable to change what the Fates had decreed.</note>. I beg you will not hesitate to assure everyone and publish everywhere my view that God has established these laws in Nature as a king establishes laws in his kingdom.” He then goes on to meet the objection that God might change the eternal truths in the same way that a king can change his laws. “Yes,” he replies, “if God’s will can change.” “But God’s will is free!” “Yes,” he replies again, “but his power is incomprehensible; and generally we can be well assured that God can do what we can understand, but not that he can do what we cannot understand, for it would be rash to suppose that our imagination has as wide an extension as his power.”

<summary>This page explains Leibniz’s disagreement with Descartes regarding the nature of logic and math, which Descartes believed were created by God's will like a king making laws, whereas Leibniz believed they were inherent to God's intellect.</summary>

### GOD’S UNDERSTANDING

In another letter written by Descartes about 1644 to an unnamed Jesuit father, he says: “With regard to the difficulty of conceiving how it was free and indifferent to God to make that it should not be true that the three angles of a triangle are equal to two right angles, or generally that two contradictories cannot exist together, we can easily avoid it by considering that the power of God can have no limits, and then by considering also that our mind is finite, and created with such a nature that we can conceive as possibles what God has willed to be really possibles, but not of such a kind that we can conceive as possibles those which God could have made to be possibles but which he has actually willed to make impossibles.” <note>Descartes famously argued for the "Creation of Eternal Truths," suggesting that even logic and math are not necessary in themselves, but are only true because God willed them to be so. This differs from Leibniz’s view that God *must* follow the laws of logic.</note>

Pierre Poiret (1646-1719), author of a treatise on *The Divine Economy* <note>original: "Economie divine"; a work detailing Poiret's system of religious thought and the management of God's grace.</note> was a Protestant minister. He was at first a Cartesian <note>A follower of the philosophy of René Descartes.</note> but became a mystic. He held in opposition to Malebranche <note>Nicolas Malebranche (1638–1715), a priest and philosopher who argued that we "see all things in God."</note> that the world of ideas is the free creation of God, who could have created other ideas and other truths. As, however, ideas are representations of the divine nature they could not in any case be other than good and true. Poiret is quoted at some length in a note of Bayle’s <note>Pierre Bayle (1647–1706), author of the influential "Historical and Critical Dictionary."</note> to the article *Rorarius* <note>Jerome Rorarius (1485–1556); Bayle’s article on him was a famous site for debates on animal souls and the nature of the mind.</note>. He seems to have held a curious theory that the body which the soul parts with at death is only of the nature of an outer bark or envelope, beneath which is a real body of **subtle matter** <note>A hypothetical "refined" or "fluid" form of matter that early modern thinkers used to explain things like light, animal spirits, or the connection between soul and body.</note> to which the soul is inseparably united. It was these **subtle bodies** made visible which appeared to the favoured disciples who beheld Moses and Elias discoursing with Jesus at the **Transfiguration** <note>A New Testament event where Jesus is seen in a glorified, radiant state alongside the prophets Moses and Elijah.</note>.

<summary>This page explores Descartes' radical view that God's power is so absolute He could have made even mathematical contradictions true, and contrasts this with the mystical Cartesianism of Pierre Poiret.</summary>
<keywords>René Descartes, Pierre Poiret, Divine Economy, Nicolas Malebranche, Pierre Bayle, subtle matter, eternal truths, Transfiguration, mysticism, Cartesianism</keywords>

### THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

<margin>J</margin>
47. Thus God alone is the original unity, or the simple primary substance of which all created or derivative monads are the products. They are born, so to speak, by continual flashes <note>original: "fulgurations"; Leibniz uses this metaphor to describe the way God constantly sustains the existence of all things, much like light radiating from a source in pulses.</note> of the divinity from moment to moment, limited by the receptivity of the created beings—a limitation that is an essential part of their nature.

Leibniz’s idea would seem to be that God produces monads continually as we produce thoughts. At first these are ideas in God’s mind, then they are realized by his free act.

Leibniz is now proceeding to set forth his theory of the order of genesis <note>The origin or mode of formation.</note> of the universe of monads.

48. In God there is *power*, which is the source of everything, then *knowledge*, which contains the details of ideas, and finally *will*, which effects changes or productions according to the principle of the best possible. And what these are in God corresponds to the perceptive and appetitive faculties <term>Appetition: The internal drive or tendency of a "monad" to move from one state of perception to the next.</term> in the created monads, the faculties which constitute their basis as subjects. In God the attributes—power, knowledge, and will—are absolutely infinite or perfect, while in the created monads or in the entelechies <note>From the Greek *entelecheia*, meaning a state of completion or a thing that carries its own purpose within itself.</note> (or *possessors of perfection* <note>original: "perfectihabies"; a Latin term coined by the Renaissance humanist Hermolaus Barbarus (1454–1493) to translate the Greek word "entelechy."</note>, as Hermolaus Barbarus translates the word)

### ESSENCE AND EXISTENCE

To understand the monadology <note>Leibniz's "Monadology" is a philosophical system where the universe is composed of "monads"—individual, soul-like units that do not interact physically but are coordinated by God.</note>, we must distinguish between the **essence** and the **existence** of the monad. The world, the universe of the monads, has been created. What is implied thereby? 

First, the **intelligence** of the cause; for God must have understood the possibilities represented by the essences <note>In philosophy, "essence" refers to the core properties that make a thing what it is, even if it doesn't exist yet in the physical world.</note>. Second, the **goodness** of the cause; for God must choose among the possibilities. Third, the **power** of God; for he gives existence to the universe he has chosen as the best of the possibilities. 

The monads could not have come into existence as the act of a blind or unintelligent power, because their creation involves the choice of possibilities, and it is impossible to exercise choice of possibilities unless there be the knowledge whereby to determine the choice. Consequently, the essences witness to the perfection of understanding in God, the existences to the perfection of good-will. 

But why then is there evil? And how does evil exist for God? Evil exists for God in his understanding only; that is, he knows evil as a possibility, for it is essential he should know the evil if he would choose the best. In choosing the best, the goodness of God appears in its perfection. Evil comes by God’s knowledge but not from God’s choice.

***

Hermolaus Barbarus <note>original: "Hermolaus Barbarus" (1454–1493); also known as Ermolao Barbaro, an Italian Renaissance scholar who sought to strip away medieval interpretations of Aristotle to find the original Greek meaning.</note> (1454-1493) was a noble and learned Venetian, the author of numerous works some of which were still being republished in the sixteenth century. The book from which Leibniz quotes was probably *Themistius's Paraphrase of Aristotle's Posterior Analytics* <note>original: "Themistii paraphrasis in Aristotelis posteriora"; a commentary on Aristotle's work concerning logic and scientific demonstration.</note>.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of Leibniz's philosophical system, moving from the attributes of God mentioned on page 87 to a specific technical definition of the "entelechy"—the internal force or soul-like quality that animates a monad—drawing heavily on Leibniz's work, the "Theodicy," and classical Aristotelian philosophy.</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>

## THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

...Analytics translated into Latin <note>original: "analytica latine versa"</note>. (Paris, 1511). In the *Theodicy* <note>Leibniz’s 1710 work, "Essais de Théodicée," where he argues that our universe is the "best of all possible worlds."</note> Leibniz is rather more explicit and deals more fully with the meaning of the word <term>entelechy</term> <note>From the Greek word "entelecheia," referring to a state of completion or a vital force that moves a potentiality into an actuality.</note>.

> "Aristotle has given the generic name entelechy or act to the soul. The word entelechy is apparently derived from the Greek word which signifies perfect, and this is why the renowned Hermolaus Barbarus <note>A 15th-century Italian humanist scholar famous for his translations of Aristotle.</note> expresses it in Latin, word for word <note>original: "verbatim"</note>, as possession of perfection <note>original: "perfectihabia"</note>, for the act is the accomplishment of the power. There was no need for him to have consulted the devil, as he is reputed to have done, in order to learn this."

(*Theodicy*, 87). The passage is a little obscure as it stands, but Leibniz goes on to elucidate it by reference to the Aristotelian distinction of two kinds of act: the permanent and the successive. The permanent or durable act is identical with the form, and the form may be either <term>substantial</term> or <term>accidental</term> <note>In scholastic philosophy, a "substantial form" gives a thing its essential nature (like a soul to a human), while an "accidental form" is a non-essential quality (like being tan or sitting down).</note>. Leibniz then gives as his own view of this distinction that the substantial form, of which the soul is the instance, is entirely permanent, while the accidental form is only temporary. The act which is transient—that is, the act of which the nature is to be transitory—consists in the action pure and simple. Here, he tells us, comes in the value of the term entelechy, for entelechy carries with it not only the idea of a simple faculty of acting but also the idea of what we call force, effort, or striving <note>original: "conatus"</note>—the idea of something from which action must follow so long as there is nothing to stop it. "Faculty" is only an attribute of substance, or even it may be a mode, but "force," when not an ingredient of substance itself (that is, when it is not primitive but derivative), is a quality distinct and separable from substance. We can conceive the soul as a primitive force, modified and varied by qualities and exercised in actions.

<summary>This page explores Leibniz's definition of "entelechy" as the soul's inherent "force" or "striving," distinguishing it from mere faculty or temporary action. It uses Aristotelian concepts of substantial and accidental forms to explain how the soul acts as a permanent, internal power within a monad.</summary>

<keywords>Leibniz, Monadology, Theodicy, entelechy, Aristotle, Hermolaus Barbarus, soul, substantial form, accidental form, conatus, force, substance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the technical exploration of Leibniz’s "Monadology," specifically moving from the linguistic origins of the term "entelechy" (discussed on page 88) to its functional application in defining the difference between active and passive states of being in created substances.</meta>

# ACTION AND PASSION

Leibniz does not appear to have gone direct to Aristotle for his idea of <term>entelechy</term> <note>A term Leibniz revived from Greek philosophy to describe an internal force that contains the end or goal of its own actions, much like a soul.</note>. He seems to have been particularly struck with the Latin jargon "possession of perfection" <note>original: "perfectihabies"</note> (a word which will not be found in the classical dictionary) as expressing the kind of perfection which every <term>monad</term> <note>The "simple substances" or spiritual atoms that Leibniz believed constitute the entire universe.</note> possesses in common with the supreme monad; that is to say, a perfection which the monad can possess without being perfect in the transcendent meaning. It is the perfection of the self-regulated machine. Entelechy is perfection in the meaning that it is the principle of the whole, controlling and subordinating the parts, without external compulsion. The differentiated organic activities act together for a common end.

**49.** The created monad is said to *act* outwardly insofar as it is perfect; and to *suffer* <note>In this philosophical sense, "suffering" refers to "passion"—the state of being passive or acted upon by something else.</note> inwardly or be acted upon by another insofar as it is imperfect. Thus *action* is attributed to the monad to the extent that its perceptions are *distinct* and *passion* to the extent that its perception is *confused*.

Here we enter on the important part of the monadology which treats of the nature of finite individuals or created monads. God, for Leibniz, is unconditioned. He is neither self-created nor self-creating. He creates the monads not in order to actualize modes of his own substance but to give existence to ideas which have presented themselves as possibilities in his mind. Monads are other selves. This is involved in the concept of God's perfection. The concept of God is all sufficient, including God's existence and excluding

any need for development or urge to create new expression. The creation is the outcome of perfect wisdom and goodness, the actualization of the possibilities ideally present to God. God in creating <term>monads</term> <note>The fundamental, "simple substances" or spiritual atoms that Leibniz believed make up the entire universe.</note> is not fulfilling a need of his own nature, for God is perfect intellect.

What then are the finite individuals or created monads? They are simple substances brought into existence by the creative act of an omniscient <note>All-knowing.</note> God. As possibilities, they were present in God’s mind as his thoughts or ideas before they were actualized <note>Brought from a state of potential into real existence.</note>, and their actualization was consequent on God’s perception of the **harmony** which would result from their activity. We have therefore to conceive the creation not as the successive actualization of possibilities but as the actualization of the whole universe once for all.

What then is the nature of these finite individuals, this universe of self-acting monads? The first crucial point is that every created monad of necessity falls short of perfection, in the very fact that it is created not uncreated, finite not infinite. We have to understand therefore in what way every monad can be a complete substance and yet there can be infinite differences between one monad and another. How then can a monad be at the same time perfect and imperfect? Every monad is perfect in the sense that it perceives the whole universe <note>Leibniz famously argued that every monad is a "mirror" of the entire universe, containing a representation of everything else from its own perspective.</note> and yet a monad may know infinitely little or nothing of the universe it perceives. For Leibniz, this is one aspect of the general **problem of evil** <note>The philosophical question of why an all-powerful, good God allows for imperfection or suffering.</note> and he brings to its solution the principle which serves him in the general case, namely, the degree of *confusedness* in the monad’s perception. The finite monad is both **active** and **passive**, acting and acted on, but while activity is a positive attribute, passivity is

# THE HIERARCHY OF THE MONADS

privation <note>In philosophy, "privation" refers to the absence or lack of a quality that should naturally be present, rather than the presence of a "bad" quality.</note>. Like evil, and error, and malice, ignorance is privation. The tower which at a distance appears round is really square. That I should think of it as it appears follows naturally from what I see. The false judgment consists in stopping short, in not examining further.

The hierarchy of the <term>monads</term> <note>The "monad" is Leibniz’s term for the basic building blocks of the universe—simple, soul-like substances that do not have physical parts.</note> is based on this degree of perfection or imperfection. Monads differ in dignity not by the positive attributes which some possess and others lack but by the degree in which <term>perception</term> <note>For Leibniz, perception is the internal state of a monad representing external things.</note> is distinct and adequate, or obscure and confused.

In this doctrine of the privative character of passion <note>In this context, "passion" means being "passive" or acted upon by something else, rather than an emotion.</note>, Leibniz is in complete accord with Spinoza <note>Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677), a major Dutch philosopher who, like Leibniz, sought to understand the nature of substance and God.</note>. Thus Spinoza says: “Our mind acts certain things and suffers others: namely, insofar as it has adequate ideas, thus far it necessarily acts certain things, and insofar as it has inadequate ideas, thus far it necessarily suffers certain things.” (*Ethics* <note>original: "Ethica"</note> book iii, proposition <note>original: "prop."</note> i.) Again after the proof of the proposition that, “the actions of the mind arise from adequate ideas alone, but passions depend on inadequate ideas alone”, (iii, 3) he adds the note: “We see thus that passions have no reference to the mind save insofar as it has something which involves a negation, or insofar as it is regarded as a part of nature which through itself and without others cannot be clearly and distinctly conceived.”

50. One created monad is more perfect than another insofar as there may be found in one the <term>a priori</term> <note>Knowledge or reasoning that proceeds from theoretical deduction rather than from observation or experience.</note> reason of what is happening in another, and this is what we mean when we speak of one monad acting on another.

-># THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ<-

Were there no relations between the created <term>monads</term> <note>Leibniz’s term for the fundamental, soul-like building blocks of the universe.</note> there would not be the universe of monads. The monads as <term>simple substances</term> <note>Substances that have no parts and therefore cannot be physically divided or destroyed.</note> are independent and it is inconsistent with the definition of them to represent their relations in the manner in which physical things are represented in reciprocal relations of action and reaction. Yet they must have relations, otherwise they would not compose an <term>aggregate</term> <note>A collection of individual things that appear to us as a single unit, like a pile of stones or a physical body.</note>. Further, their relations must be such that they constitute only limitations or <term>privations</term> <note>The absence or lack of something that should naturally be there; for example, cold is the privation of heat.</note> of the self-originated, self-directed, actions of the monads themselves. Thus a monad may avail itself of the activities of other monads by making their activity serve its activity, but a monad cannot make use of another monad as a man uses a tool. Abundant illustration of this kind of harmony of independent activities is presented in nature. <term>Biology</term> is unintelligible without such relations as privations and inhibitions.

Leibniz now proceeds to expound his doctrine of the nature of the relations between the monads.

### 51. Leibniz has to reconcile two seemingly incongruous
facts; first, that the universe is composed of <term>simple
substances</term> <note>Indivisible units of reality that have no parts and are therefore indestructible by natural means.</note>, and second, that the aggregate of these
substances is a universe. Neither fact can be denied
or explained away and the two facts are mutually
interdependent. There is a composite universe and
its constituents are simple substances. Neither of
these facts is unconditioned, both, by an *a priori* <note>original: "a priori"; reasoning based on logical deduction rather than observation.</note>
necessity of thought, require subsumption under the
<term>principle of sufficient reason</term> <note>The logical principle that nothing happens without a reason why it should be so rather than otherwise.</note>. The nature of the
substances is finite and their activities are organically
interdependent. Hence we must conclude that the
monads have come into existence by creation, and that
creation was a unique act, since the many monads are
one universe. To reconcile the two facts Leibniz
refers both of them to God and deduces from them
the divine attributes, wisdom, goodness and power.
The facts themselves, he reasons, were possibilities
before they were existences. As possibilities their
reality was purely ideal. To exist in idea is to arise
as thoughts in a mind, to exist in fact is to be self-acting
and independent. Hence the conception of the <term>pre-
established harmony</term> <note>The theory that God coordinated all independent substances at the moment of creation so they remain in sync, like two perfectly timed clocks.</note>—the creation of reals which

themselves, it is said, are hopelessly irreconcilable, so
by an ingenious stage contrivance God is introduced

# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

94

This view fails to appreciate the insight of Leibniz. His doctrine may be unacceptable in the light of the development of modern scientific theory, but it is not fantastic, and the God of the **pre-established harmony** <note>Leibniz’s theory that God perfectly synchronized every substance in the universe at the moment of creation, so they appear to interact even though they are actually independent.</note> does not **step out of a machine** <note>A reference to the "deus ex machina" (god from the machine), a literary device where a deity is lowered onto a stage to solve a plot hole. The author argues that Leibniz’s God is a logical necessity of the system, not an arbitrary fix for a weak theory.</note>.

The relation of the created monads to God is conceived on the analogy of the relation of our actions to our personality. Before our actions are actualized, they exist ideally in our mind as ends or purposes or as means to ends. In acting, we give existence and independence to our ideas. Acts become deeds. In like manner, God actualized his ideas in creation. It is true that our actions are performed continuously while God's creation is conceived as effective once and for all. This is because Leibniz conceives God, in common with the accepted formula, as the infinitely perfect being and therefore as archetypal. The idea of God as identical with the living principle, the **vital impulse** <note>original: "élan vital"; a term for the creative force of life.</note>, would have clashed with the **preformation theory** <note>An early biological theory which Leibniz supported, suggesting that all living things exist as fully formed miniature versions of themselves within "seeds" or embryos before they begin to grow.</note> which Leibniz held in common with the advanced scientific opinion of his time.

There are in fact only two alternative views of the nature of finite individuality. In contemporary thought, they are referred to as the **substantival** <note>The view that individuals are independent "substances"—real, self-contained entities.</note> and the **adjectival** <note>The view that individuals are not independent, but are merely "adjectives" or properties of one single, ultimate reality.</note> views. If the individual is a **substance**, a **monad** <note>The fundamental, indivisible building blocks of reality in Leibniz’s philosophy.</note>, a self-owned activity circumscribed in its range, then its relations must be ideal, for real relations would clearly be destructive. Leibniz has shown this in his criticism of the theory of atoms and the void. If, on the other hand, relations are real—if the universe consists of actions and reactions—then the individual is ideal, an adjectival mode of being. Leibniz rejects the adjectival view and adopts the substantival view. This commits him to the conception of God as the

...sufficient reason of the existing world and requires that he should discover the logical implications of the act of creation. Where else but in the divine mind can he put the **ideal relations**?

The intervention of God therefore has a distinctive meaning for Leibniz as an essential part of his metaphysical system. The doctrine is not to be confused with Malebranche's **vision in God** <note>The theory of Nicolas Malebranche (1638–1715) suggesting that humans do not perceive the world directly, but rather see "ideas" of things within the mind of God.</note> or with Berkeley's **continuous perceiver** <note>The idea from George Berkeley (1685–1753) that physical objects only exist because they are being perceived; they continue to exist because God is always watching them.</note>.

52. Thus it is that between created **monads** <term>monad: The fundamental, indivisible "soul-atoms" that Leibniz believed make up the universe.</term> actions and passions are mutual. For God, comparing two simple substances, finds in each reasons obliging him to adapt one to the other. Consequently, what is **active** in some respects is **passive** from another point of view; it is active, insofar as what we know **distinctly** <note>For Leibniz, "distinct" knowledge is clear and detailed, whereas "confused" knowledge is vague. A monad is "active" when it has clear thoughts that explain what is happening elsewhere.</note> in it serves to give a reason for what occurs in another; and it is passive insofar as the reason of what occurs in it is found in what is distinctly known in another.

The problems here may be stated as follows: If the relations between monads are **ideal** <note>meaning they exist as ideas or logical connections rather than physical contact.</note> and not physical interactions, how are we to account for the mutual adaptations of the monads to one another which appear as action in one monad and as reciprocal passion in another? # THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

...distinctness or of its degree of confusedness and obscurity. This double aspect must have entered into the creative design itself. We have already seen that **passion** <note>In this philosophical context, "passion" does not mean strong emotion, but rather the state of being acted upon or being "passive."</note> is privative <note>Meaning it represents a lack or a limitation of something—in this case, a lack of clear perception.</note>, we may now see how **action** can be viewed from two standpoints according to the distinctness or obscurity of the perception which gives it significance. The instance which seems to be continually present to Leibniz in this relation of the monads is the connexion of soul and body. The soul-body relation appears as pure action in the soul and as pure passion in the body. The interpretation is that the soul perceives distinctly in itself what serves as a reason for the action of the body, and the action of the body is experienced as passion, because its **sufficient reason** <term>sufficient reason: The principle that nothing happens without a reason why it should be so rather than otherwise.</term> is only known distinctly in the soul.

53. Now in God’s ideas are an infinity of possible universes only one of which will exist; there must therefore be a **sufficient reason** of God’s choice determining which one rather than any other shall be.

This and the next three sections expound Leibniz’s doctrine that the world which exists is the **best possible**. It is a little curious to see that he has no use in his philosophy for the **teleological argument** <note>Also known as the "argument from design," which suggests that the complexity of the world proves the existence of a creator, much like a watch implies a watchmaker.</note>. On the contrary there is a complete reversal of the ordinary demonstration which takes as its premises the evidence of design in nature and concludes from it the wisdom of the designer. The reason is that Leibniz’s concern is not to glorify the work of God in creation, but to interpret rationally the existence of evil. The world, though it may be imperfect and contain evil, is the best...

->THE DEGREES OF PERFECTION<-

...of all possible worlds simply by reason that it exists. If evil be necessary, then necessary evil is not evil but good. The world for Leibniz is not an adaptation of means to end, a carrying out of purpose by devising skilful contrivances, and God is not a worker in clay. The universe consists of free self-acting natures, and the wisdom of God in creation is not that of an artificer <note>A skilled craftsman or inventor.</note> but that of an adapter. His wisdom is displayed not in craftsmanship but in choice.

This concept of God is as essential and necessary a part of Leibniz’s system as are the constituent units. Without God both the logical principle of non-contradiction <note>The logical law stating that something cannot be both true and false at the same time and in the same respect.</note> and the logical principle of sufficient reason <term>sufficient reason: The principle that nothing exists or happens without a reason why it is so and not otherwise.</term> would lack their metaphysical counterpart.

54. The sufficient reason of God’s choice can only be found in the *fitness* <term>fitness: In the original French, *convenance*; this refers to the internal harmony and degree of reality that makes one possible world more "suitable" for existence than others.</term>, or the degrees of perfection that these possible worlds contain; for every possible (ideal) world has a right to claim (actual) existence in proportion to the perfection it contains.

The reason of God’s choice which has determined the existence of the world is a self-evident principle and may be expressed in a perfectly natural way and as an ordinary scientific concept. Suppose a naturalist <note>A scholar of natural history, similar to a modern biologist.</note> marvelling at the ingenuity displayed in the construction of an organ serving a living function such as the human eye. The parts are infinitely complex and the slightest lack of foresight in the construction or lack of adaptation in the assembling would clearly spell failure. Yet the eye most clearly has not been manufactured in this way; the unity and the simplicity...

In such way Leibniz points to the existence of the world as furnishing at once why it is what it is and not other <note>This is a reference to the Principle of Sufficient Reason: the idea that there is a specific reason why everything is the way it is and not otherwise.</note>. Just as the mechanism of the eye is subservient to its function, and the function of the eye is that for which the mechanism exists, so the individual monads are subservient to the universe, and the universe is that for which the monads are created and to which they are adapted.

55. The reason why the best of all possible worlds is the world which exists, is that God’s wisdom makes him know, his goodness makes him choose, and his power makes him produce, the best possible.

The existence of the world is not arbitrary but necessary in the meaning that it follows from God’s attributes and not from a capricious <note>Guided by whim or impulse rather than reason.</note> exercise of his will. The interest of the doctrine is that it brings us in view of the modern naturalistic concept of **creative evolution** <term>creative evolution: A philosophy (later associated with Henri Bergson) suggesting that the universe is not a finished product but a continuous process of creation. Leibniz anticipates this by seeing the world as an unfolding of internal possibilities.</term>, so far at least as such a view was possible in a mathematical age. God is conceived as pure intellectual activity, and every possible self-acting nature is present to his mind as idea. The creation is not the projection of a plan and the constructive work of its realization, but the changing of ideal possibilities into independent actualities.

### PERSPECTIVES

...the others, and consequently each <insert>[</insert>created monad is a perpetual living mirror <note>original: "miroir vivant perpétuel"</note> of the universe.

Leibniz is following out the implications of his guiding concept. The universe is not static, it is not a system of interacting material objects with real relations of position, succession, action and reaction. <insert>[</insert>The universe is dynamic, a living universe without any inert substratum <term>inert substratum: A passive, underlying "stuff" or matter that has no power of its own. Leibniz rejected this, believing everything is made of active force.</term>, its unit activities are really independent though ideally related. These ideal relations are not qualitative chemical properties of things, nor yet are they quantitative mathematical coordinates; they are adaptations which harmonize the activities of the individuals in the general activity of the whole. The universe consequently is mirrored in every monad in the sense that in every monad can be read the adaptations which make its presence and activity realizable in the living whole.

<margin>/</margin>57. As one and the same city looked at from different sides appears different and presents innumerable aspects, so likewise it comes about that there are as many different universes as there are simple substances <term>simple substances: The fundamental, indivisible building blocks of reality—the monads—which have no parts.</term> the multitude of which is infinite. All these different universes are, however, only perspectives of one universe from the different <margin>✓</margin>view-points of each monad.

This illustration enables us to represent distinctly Leibniz's scheme of the fundamental metaphysical...

--- Page 114 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of how the universe is perceived from the unique viewpoint of each monad, specifically addressing the distinction between the "one real universe" and the "many perspectives" of it. It follows the analogy of the city used on page 99.</meta>

<page-num>100</page-num>

reality of the universe. Taken in its existence there is one universe, the universe of the monads <term>monads: The fundamental, indivisible units of reality that Leibniz describes as "soul-like" substances.</term>. This universe is a composite or aggregate of the individual activities each of which in its existence is inviolate <note>inviolate: Meaning "windowless" or self-contained; no external force can change the internal state of a monad.</note>. The activity of each monad, perception, brings it about that the universe is mirrored in each monad. The aspect of the universe thus mirrored is different for every monad and private to it. What each monad possesses therefore is a perspective, and every perspective is different by reason of the fact that it is the monad’s individual viewpoint. There are therefore as many different universes as there are individual monads although these different universes are only perspectives of the one real universe which is the composite of the individual monads.

It will be seen therefore that Leibniz does not mean (as his doctrine is so frequently represented as meaning) that the one universe is the totality of perspectives. As well might one suppose the city to be only the totality of the views which have been or can be taken of it. We must distinguish Leibniz’s doctrine of reality from the theory of some present-day philosophers according to which objects are composed of sense-data <term>sense-data: The raw, immediate information gathered by the senses, such as a specific color or sound, before it is processed by the mind.</term> and any particular object is no more than the class of its appearances. Leibniz has a realistic theory of the universe which implies a purely idealistic theory of knowledge <note>Leibniz is a "realist" because he believes the universe exists independently of our minds, but an "idealist" because he believes the components of that universe are themselves mind-like or spiritual in nature.</note>.

58. This is the means of securing the greatest possible variety <note>original: "autant de variété qu'il est possible"</note> with the highest possible order <note>original: "le plus grand ordre qui se puisse"</note>, that is to say, it is the means of obtaining as much perfection as possible.

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Leibniz’s metaphysics, transitioning from the discussion of individual perspectives on page 100 to the distinction between the perfection of God and the perfection of the created universe.</meta>

-># NATURE OF GOD’S PERFECTION <-

<page-num>101</page-num>

The perfection of the created world is of a different order to the perfection which is a **divine attribute**. The perfection of the actual existing universe, that is to say, an existing perfection as distinct from an *ideal* perfection, consists in the infinite variety of free activities, independent individual unit constituents, developing in absolute harmony. The perfection of God is the perfection of a **simple substance**, the activity of the **supreme monad** <term>supreme monad: Leibniz’s term for God, the primary substance from which all other monads originate.</term> raised to its highest power. 

The perfection of the created universe is the perfection of a **composite** <term>composite: Something made up of many parts; in this context, the universe is a collection of many individual monads.</term>. Were every created monad perfect in the sense in which God is perfect, inconsistency and contradiction would be introduced into the very notion of a composite, and would make a universe unintelligible. There cannot exist a plurality of perfect monads, either as a universe or in any other way, for the same reason that there cannot be a plurality of Gods. This is plain to ordinary reflexion. Only one God can be **infinite** for if there are two infinites both are finite <note>If two beings were "limitless," they would eventually limit each other's reach, meaning neither would truly be infinite.</note>. Only one God can be **omnipotent** <note>omnipotent: All-powerful.</note> and **omniscient** <note>omniscient: All-knowing.</note>, for two would limit and therefore negate the power and knowledge of each. Insofar therefore as the universe of created monads of necessity falls short of the perfection which belongs to God only, it must present an aspect of imperfection, and its perfection will exist only in being the greatest degree of perfection possible. In precisely the same way we may speak of the perfection of an **organism** as consisting in the variety of function of the constituent cells and the harmony and unity of the whole.

> 59. Moreover it is only this hypothesis, the truth of which I claim to have proved, that brings into full relief the greatness of God. Even Monsieur <note>original: "Monsieur" (Mr.)</note> Bayle <note>Pierre Bayle (1647–1706), a French philosopher and skeptic whose famous *Historical and Critical Dictionary* challenged many theological and philosophical orthodoxies of his time.</note> may be said to have acknowledged this in the very objections he brings against it in the *Dictionary* (*Article, Rorarius* <note>Jerome Rorarius was a 16th-century writer; Bayle used the entry on him to critique Leibniz’s theory of the soul.</note>). He is inclined to think that I assign too much to God, in fact more than is possible. Yet he is unable to allege any reason why this **universal harmony** <term>universal harmony: Also known as Pre-established Harmony, this is Leibniz’s theory that God synchronized every substance in the universe to correspond with every other substance, like clocks set to the same time, without them actually touching or influencing each other.</term>, which causes every substance to express exactly all the others by the relations it has to them, should be impossible.

We touch here on a difficulty which is not satisfactorily overcome. Leibniz shows himself to be acutely aware of it even while claiming to have surmounted it. God’s **omniscience** <term>omniscience: The attribute of having total, infinite knowledge of all things.</term> enables him to read the eventual actions of all free agents in the fact that the nature of each agent is open to God’s inspection, or rather present to God in idea before creation. Yet in creating the natures God does not **predestine** <term>predestine: To determine an outcome or action in advance by divine decree.</term>, or decree, as the theological doctrine termed it, the actions. Does this relieve God of responsibility for the actions when creating the natures? 

Leibniz insists that it does. It is rather like arguing that introducing a wolf into the sheep-fold, well knowing the wolf-nature, involves no responsibility because it is not your knowledge which gives efficiency to the wolf’s actions. The interest of this is the instance it affords of an ambiguity in Leibniz’s conception of God. If God is a **monad** <term>monad: In Leibniz’s philosophy, the fundamental, indivisible units of reality. God is considered the "Supreme Monad."</term> then not only is God’s individuality inviolate,

-># THE MIRRORED UNIVERSE <-

<page-num>103</page-num>
<sig>H</sig>

...but even God cannot violate the individuality of the created monads. The **monad of monads** <note>original: "monas monadum." In Leibniz’s philosophy, this term refers to God, the supreme substance that coordinates all other substances.</note>, just like the simple monad, has no windows <note>Leibniz famously stated that "monads have no windows through which anything could come in or go out," meaning they are self-contained and do not physically interact.</note>, and exists in an ideal relationship only with the universe.

60. Furthermore, in what I am now going to say, we may see the **logic-based** <note>original: "a priori." This refers to reasoning that comes from theoretical deduction rather than from observation or experience.</note> reasons why things cannot be other than they are. It is because God, in regulating the whole, must have had regard for each part and particularly for each monad. Since the nature of the monad is **representative** <term>representative: The idea that every individual substance acts as a "mirror" of the entire cosmos, containing information about the whole universe from its own perspective.</term>, its representation cannot be restricted to only a part of things. It is true that regarding the details of the whole universe, this representation is **confused** <note>In Leibniz's view, "confused" perception is like hearing the roar of the sea: you hear the whole sound, but you cannot distinguish the sound of every individual wave.</note>, and each monad’s distinct representation is confined to a small part only—namely, to those things which are nearest to it or else of the greatest importance in relation to it. If it were not so, each monad would be a divinity. It is not in the object itself, but in the **shaping of the knowledge** <note>original: "modification of the knowledge."</note> of the object that the monads are restricted. They all, in their confused perception, reach toward infinity—toward the whole—and they are limited and distinguished by the different degrees of their clear and distinct perceptions.

The monads perceive the same universe, each from an individual point of view. But although the whole...

# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

<page-num>104</page-num>

universe is mirrored in each monad, it is only a very small part which is expressed **distinctly**, namely, that part which is in relation to the body to which the monad corresponds. The body accordingly constitutes the monad's **view-point** <term>view-point: Leibniz’s concept that every individual substance (monad) perceives the entire universe from a unique physical and metaphysical position, much like different perspectives of the same city.</term>.

What distinguishes the monads, and grades them, is not any geometrical or numerical relations which may be supposed to assign them separate ranges of activity, but the proportion of **distinct perception** in the **confused perception** <note>In Leibniz’s philosophy, a "confused perception" is like hearing the collective roar of the ocean—you perceive the whole, but you cannot distinguish the individual sound of every single wave.</note> of the whole universe which is mirrored in each monad. Thus to take our own selves, we have **distinct perceptions** of our own body, more or less distinct perceptions of the things in the environment of our body, and a general **confused perception** of the background. At this point however the concept of **infinity**, and the contradictions involved in the ordinary attempt to apprehend it, present a serious difficulty. Leibniz appears to think this is sufficiently met by contraposing to the infinite variety of the universe the **infinite comprehension of God**. The world as represented ideally in God's mind is infinite and God's mind in its comprehension is infinite. Therefore, Leibniz argues, the infinite universe is adequately represented by **clear and distinct ideas** <note>original: "idées claires et distinctes." A term popularized by René Descartes referring to thoughts that are so self-evident and precise they cannot be mistaken for anything else.</note> in the infinite mind. The natural inability of the finite to apprehend the infinite does not make the infinite object finite, nor does it cut out all objects beyond a certain range, it simply brings it about that the infinite object is perceived confusedly and devoid of clear outlines.

It is possible that this idea of **confused perception** may have been suggested to Leibniz by the fact that objects when magnified by the **microscope** <note>At the time of writing, the microscope was a revolutionary tool. Philosophers like Leibniz saw the microscopic world as proof that life and complexity continue infinitely downward, even into what appears to be dead matter.</note> do not resolve themselves into enlarged units but gain an ever increasing and apparently unlimited complexity.

It also addresses how God's infinite mind resolves the paradox of a finite being perceiving an infinite world, using the microscope as an analogy for nature's endless complexity.</summary>

# THE PLENUM

<page-num>105</page-num>

It is most important to keep constantly in mind that the units of the monadic universe are not **infinitesimal** <term>infinitesimal: Something infinitely small. Leibniz is clarifying that monads are not just "tiny pieces" of matter, but metaphysical points of existence.</term> either in their **extension** or **intension** <term>extension and intension: "Extension" refers to taking up physical space, while "intension" refers to the internal depth or degree of reality and power a thing possesses.</term>.

61. In this characteristic of the monads (that their restrictions are not in the object but in the modifications of knowledge of the object) the **composites** <term>composites: Complex things made of many parts, like a human body or a stone.</term> accord with the **simples** <term>simples: The fundamental, indivisible building blocks of reality—the monads themselves.</term>. For the whole is a **plenum** <term>plenum: A space that is entirely full. Leibniz argued against the existence of a "vacuum" or empty space; for him, the universe is packed tight with substance.</term> and it is this being a plenum which binds all matter together so that every movement has an effect on distant bodies proportional to the distance. Each body then is not only affected by the bodies in immediate contact with it and responds in some way to everything which happens to them, but through them it also responds to what happens to them by reason of their contact with other bodies, and this communication will extend indefinitely however great the distance. Consequently each and every body responds to everything whatever which is going on in the universe. Anyone therefore who could see all would be able to read in each what is happening everywhere, even including all that has happened and will happen; he would observe in the present what is distant in time as well as what is distant in space. **All things breathe together** <note>original: "Σύμπνοια πάντα" (Sympnoia panta). A famous quote attributed to the Greek physician Hippocrates, suggesting that the universe functions like a single, interconnected living organism.</note> said Hippocrates. A soul, however, can only

This physical interconnectedness allows the entire history and state of the universe to be 'read' within any single part of it, reflecting a universal harmony.</summary>

<page-num>106</page-num>

Leibniz has now completed his theory of the **simple substance** <term>simple substance: The most basic, indivisible building block of reality, which Leibniz calls a "monad." It has no physical parts and is purely internal or mental in nature.</term> and he turns to the still more important theory of the **composite** <term>composite: Any physical thing made of multiple parts, such as a rock, a tree, or a human body.</term>. He seems to contemplate with never-ceasing wonder and awe the living universe as it reveals the omniscience and power of the **divine artificer** <note>original: "divin artisan." A common philosophical term for God, depicted here as a master craftsman or engineer who designs the world with perfect precision.</note>. The universe of God's creation is not a mere aggregate of simple substances exhibiting an infinite variety combined with an absolute harmony, it is much more. It is a **divine machine**, every part of which is a machine, which could only be actualized by the nature of the units God has chosen for its construction.

There are two sides from which we may regard the nature of the **monad** <term>monad: From the Greek "monas," meaning unity. For Leibniz, these are the true atoms of nature—not material atoms, but "atoms of soul" that make up everything.</term>. We may consider it in what is implied by its individuality—the freedom of its activity, the inviolability of its existence, its **windowless isolation** <note>Leibniz famously wrote that monads "have no windows" through which anything can enter or leave. This means they are not physically influenced by the outside world, but instead unfold their own internal programming.</note>. It has another aspect. The monad is by nature representative and the representation essential to it is unlimited. It follows, therefore, that to anyone to whom the monad's nature is open to inspection, and who can read what is there represented, the universe of monads is inscribed in every monad. It is with this material—with monads each representative of and representing all—and not with potter's clay, that the great artificer has worked.

We are introduced at once therefore to the crucial problem, the relation of **soul and body**. It is well for clear understanding to take as the type of the simple substance the rational soul, and as the type of the...

# SENSE EXPERIENCE

<page-num>107</page-num>

composite the living body, and then try to apply Leibniz’s analogy of **movement in a plenum** <term>plenum: A space completely filled with matter, where no vacuum exists. In this model, every movement, no matter how small, causes a ripple effect throughout the entire universe.</term>. We must keep constantly in mind that space and time, which are inseparable from the concept of movement, are for Leibniz **ideal relations** <note>This means that space and time are not physical "containers" but are the ways our minds organize and relate different perceptions to one another.</note>, only referring to the representative order of the **monad’s** <term>monad: The most basic, indivisible building block of reality. Leibniz argues these are not physical atoms, but spiritual or mental units of existence.</term> perceptions and not to the relations between the monads themselves. 

The particular application of the analogy of movement is that while God is able to read at once in the representations in the soul the whole universe therein represented, it does not follow that the soul itself is able to unfold in distinct ideas the universe confusedly mirrored to it and represented in it. Thus when we present the universe to ourselves in its material aspect as a **plenum** we see that every particle of matter though affected by every movement of any particle, however distant, is only immediately affected by the particles with which it is in contact. 

Precisely in the same way the soul is affected by everything which happens in the universe but is only immediately affected by the body. The whole nature of the function which the senses perform in knowledge, and the general role of **sense experience** in mediating knowledge of the infinite universe, is suddenly illuminated. The soul has in its immediate vicinity, as it were, (the metaphor is spatial) a ring of its clear and distinct ideas. These are the body, or perhaps we ought to say the ideas which represent the body (for the monads of the body have their claim to reality). The body therefore is the soul’s clear and distinct ideas, and through this body the outer penumbra of confused perception affects the soul.

How then, it may be asked, does this accord with the theory of **pre-established harmony** <term>pre-established harmony: Leibniz's solution to the mind-body problem. He suggests God coordinated the soul and the body at the moment of creation so they function together perfectly without actually "touching" or influencing one another directly.</term>? The relation

<meta>This page continues the exploration of how the soul and body relate to one another. While the previous page discussed how the soul perceives the universe through the "filter" of the physical body, this page focuses on why this relationship is considered "ideal" (mental or logical) rather than "real" (physical) and compares Leibniz’s views with those of his contemporary, Spinoza.</meta>

->THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ<-

<page-num>108</page-num>

Between the soul and body in Leibniz’s scheme is an **ideal** <note>Meaning the relationship exists in the way God organized the universe’s logic, rather than through a physical "push and pull" interaction.</note> not a **real relation**. The source or spring of the activity exists in each and is not derived from the other, and the thoughts which express the body are independent. They harmonize in the manner in which **two clocks** accord when regulated in advance by a skilful clock-maker <note>This is Leibniz’s famous "pre-established harmony" analogy: if two clocks are perfectly synchronized, they show the same time not because they are connected, but because they were both started perfectly by the maker.</note>. It is another aspect of the problem which here engages Leibniz. On what principle, he enquires, can they enter into a **composite**? <term>composite: A whole made of several parts. Leibniz is trying to explain how a spiritual soul and a material body can function as a single unit.</term> For the answer he appeals to their **representative** and not to their **monadic** nature. It is because the soul has clear and distinct ideas of the body that the more or less confused ideas of the universe, of which soul and body are part, can affect the soul. The soul cannot "read" in the body what is represented therein in regard to the universe. It can only read what is represented in itself, and it is the clearness and distinctness of its ideas of the body which in its representation separate the body from the world and make the soul-body a composite.

Spinoza has a very similar doctrine. “The object of the idea constituting the human mind is the body, or a certain mode of extension actually existing and nothing else:” (*Ethics, Part II, Proposition XIII.* <note>original: "Ethica II., prop. XIII."</note>) It has however a different bearing conformably with the very different metaphysical principle of Spinoza. Mind and body are for Spinoza two **aspects of one reality**, each aspect, however, having its indefeasible claim to existence, the mind as a **mode of thinking**, the body as a **mode of extension** <term>extension: A philosophical term for physical matter, defined by the fact that it takes up space (it "extends" in length, width, and depth).</term>, both thinking and extension being **attributes** <note>In this context, attributes are the fundamental characteristics that describe the nature of a substance.</note> of the one **substance**, God <term>substance: The most basic "stuff" of reality that does not depend on anything else to exist. Spinoza believed there was only one substance (God), while Leibniz believed there were infinite substances (monads).</term>. Leibniz on the other hand takes the simple substances as independent reals, and the composites as formed by ideal relations, made possible by the representative nature of the reals.

<summary>This page explains Leibniz's "clock analogy," arguing that the soul and body don't physically interact but are synchronized by God. It also contrasts Leibniz's view of many independent substances with Spinoza's view that mind and body are just two different sides of a single divine substance.</summary>
