<meta>This page continues the exploration of how the soul and body relate to one another. While the previous page discussed how the soul perceives the universe through the "filter" of the physical body, this page focuses on why this relationship is considered "ideal" (mental or logical) rather than "real" (physical) and compares Leibniz’s views with those of his contemporary, Spinoza.</meta>

->REAL AND IDEAL RELATIONS<-

62. Thus although each created **monad** <term>monad: From the Greek 'monas' (unity). In this context, it refers to the basic, indivisible building blocks of the universe, which are mental or spiritual in nature rather than physical.</term> represents the whole universe, it represents more distinctly the body which is particularly affected by it and of which it is the **entelechy** <term>entelechy: A term borrowed from Aristotle, used here to describe the internal force or "soul" that gives a living thing its purpose and completes its existence.</term>. As this body expresses the whole universe through the connexion of all matter in the **plenum** <term>plenum: A concept in physics and philosophy where all space is entirely filled with matter, leaving no vacuum or empty "holes."</term>, so the soul also in representing its body, which belongs to it in an especial way, represents the whole universe.

To follow the important argument which runs through the remaining sections of the *Monadology* <note>The Monadology is a short text written by Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz in 1714 that summarizes his entire philosophical system in 90 numbered paragraphs.</note>, we must keep in mind that it is no longer the *reality* but the *ideality* of the world which Leibniz is interpreting. The relations between the monads are ideal relations because the nature of the monads is to be representative. Real relations in Leibniz’s conception of the universe would simply be destructive of the reality of the constituent units <note>Leibniz believed that if monads could physically push or pull each other, they wouldn't be truly independent, simple substances. Instead, they only "interact" because God synchronized their internal thoughts.</note>. Let us recapitulate : The universe is a composite of created monads; the creation of the monads means that they arose in God’s mind as ideas and were actualized ; the universe is therefore the realized expression of God’s thoughts, the outcome of omniscience and power ; the universe is a divine machine, a living whole in which the living parts conspire together (this is the meaning of <note>original: "σύμπνοια πάντα"</note> **all things breathe together**). We have now to follow out the nature and working of the ideal bond which joins together all and makes the many one.

It is worth remarking that the same problem of reality and ideality which Leibniz here presents in its metaphysical form underlies modern scientific realism.

<summary>This section explains how each individual soul-unit (monad) focuses on its own body while still reflecting the entire universe. It emphasizes that the universe is a "divine machine" where all parts are synchronized by God rather than physically connected.</summary>

<keywords>Leibniz, Monadology, Entelechy, Plenum, Idealism, Metaphysics, Divine Machine, Interconnectedness</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion from section 62, moving from the distinction between "ideal" and "real" relations to how these concepts apply to biological life and the physical sciences. It bridges the gap between the physicist’s view of matter and Leibniz’s metaphysical view of the soul.</meta>

### THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ
110

The physicist has to distinguish sharply at the outset the ideal order—in which knowledge of the external world arises through sensory experience and takes the form of sensations and perceptions—from the real order of those actual units which determine the character of that knowledge. No sooner, however, is the object conceptualized and reduced by scientific analysis to its simplest ultimate real units—such as **quanta** <note>In modern physics, a "quantum" is the smallest possible unit of energy; here, the author uses it as an analogy for the most basic building blocks of matter.</note> of radio activity, single electric charges, or whatever the irreducible units may be—than a problem arises: how can the mere complexity of how these units are arranged (which is all that physical science allows for) result first in the phenomena of the living world, and then in the phenomena of the mind? 

Even the physicist is compelled to fall back on a theory of **parallelism** <term>parallelism: The philosophical theory that mental and physical events occur in a coordinated way without one directly causing the other, like two clocks synchronized to the same time.</term> to account for these two orders, the real and the ideal, which are united in practice even though they are impossible to reconcile in theory. Leibniz, fully conscious of this difficulty from the start because he begins with a metaphysical interest, anticipates and prevents this problem. To him, the real units of the universe are activities rather than inert particles of mass; therefore, they are already "ideal" or mental in their very nature.

63. What we call a *living thing* is a **monad** <term>monad: From the Greek 'monas' (unity); the fundamental, indivisible "soul-like" unit of reality in Leibniz's system.</term> which is an **entelechy** <term>entelechy: A term from Aristotle meaning "having its purpose within itself"; it refers to the internal force that makes a living thing develop and function.</term> together with its body; what we call an *animal* is a monad which is a soul together with its body. Now the body of a living thing or of an animal is always **organic** <note>By "organic," Leibniz means that every part of the body is itself a structured, living system, like a machine made of smaller machines, infinitely downward.</note>, for since every monad is a mirror of the universe in its own way, and since the order which rules throughout the universe is perfect, it follows that the body must also reflect that order.

<summary>This page explains how Leibniz’s philosophy avoids the gap between mind and matter by viewing the fundamental units of reality as active, "soul-like" forces rather than dead matter. It defines living things and animals as combinations of these internal forces (entelechies or souls) and their organized, physical bodies.</summary>

<keywords>Leibniz, Monadology, physics, entelechy, monad, parallelism, metaphysics, organic body, mind-body problem</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the commentary on Section 63 of Leibniz’s Monadology. It transitions from the general definition of a living thing to the specific relationship between the body’s physical order and the soul’s internal perceptions, specifically focusing on the concept of the "entelechy."</meta>

## ENTELECHY
->III<-

There must also be an order in the body which is representing the universe; that is to say, as there is an order in the perceptions of the soul, so there must be an order in the body through which the universe is represented in the soul.

The term **“entelechy”** <term>entelechy: From the Greek word *entelecheia*, meaning to have one's purpose or "end" within oneself. Leibniz uses it to describe the vital principle that gives life and movement to a substance.</term> is used by Leibniz with wider comprehension than the term **“soul,”** but there is no difference in kind. There is an entelechy of a plant or of any living thing even though it have no distinct perceptions; a soul has always some degree or range of distinct perception, and a **rational soul** <note>Also called the "spirit" in Leibniz's system; it is the level of soul found in humans.</note> has also **apperception** or self-consciousness.

The idea which Leibniz develops in this and the following sections is as follows: the body, insofar as it fulfills the function of body to the soul, is not a reality of the same order as the soul. It is composite and not, like the soul, a **simple substance**. The relation of the soul to the body is that of the *dominant* to *subservient* **monads** <term>monads: The fundamental, indivisible "atoms" of existence in Leibniz's philosophy. They are not physical particles, but centers of force and perception.</term>. 

Now the constituent monads of the body are, like all monads, inviolable. The organization of the body—in respect of which the various activities subserve and obey the behests of the soul—is rendered possible by the representative nature of every monad and the ideal nature of monadic activity. The dominance of the soul is a consequence of the amount and range of clear and distinct perception which illuminates the zone of action. Leibniz does not hold that the marvelous adaptation of soul and body arises naturally as a simple and direct effect of the monad’s perception; it is a **pre-established harmony** <note>Leibniz’s famous solution to the mind-body problem: God synchronized souls and bodies at the beginning of creation, like two perfectly tuned clocks that always show the same time without being physically connected.</note> which points to the wisdom of God, manifest in creation.

<summary>This page explains Leibniz’s concept of the "entelechy" as the internal driving force of all living things and distinguishes between basic living forces, animal souls, and rational human spirits. It also introduces the "pre-established harmony" as the divine coordination between the soul's perceptions and the body's movements.</summary>

<keywords>entelechy, Leibniz, soul, perceptions, apperception, self-consciousness, monads, pre-established harmony, simple substance, dominant monad</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Leibniz’s Monadology, specifically building on the discussion of entelechy and the hierarchy of monads from page 111. It moves from the internal perception of the soul to the mechanical but infinite nature of the physical body.</meta>

# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

What is important in this theory from the modern standpoint is that monadology avoids the **Scylla of dualism** and the **Charybdis of monism** <note>These are two monsters from Greek mythology situated on opposite sides of a narrow strait; to avoid one was often to fall victim to the other. Here, it refers to the difficulty of choosing between "dualism" (the belief that mind and matter are separate) and "monism" (the belief that everything is made of just one substance).</note>. It does so by conceiving its unit reals not as mass units <note>i.e., physical atoms or bits of matter</note> but as activities, not as objects or things but as subjects. Subjective activity means that there are ideal relations, and these alone enable us to understand the relation of soul and body. The soul, whose activity is seen in its domination over the free activities of the **monads** <term>monads: Indivisible, soul-like units that Leibniz believe make up the entire universe.</term> which enter into the organization of the body, may itself be dominated by a higher monad and with its whole activity enter into an organization. On either side of us, above and below, our ideal nature opens the possibility of harmonious action. The universe is a composite.

64. Thus each organic body of a living creature is a kind of **divine machine** or **natural automaton** <term>automaton: A machine that is relatively self-operating or follows a predetermined sequence of responses.</term> surpassing infinitely every kind of artificial automaton. For a machine made by human art is not a machine in every part of it. For example, the geared tooth of a metal wheel is not, so far as its material is concerned, artificial to us, and there is nothing in the material which denotes the special use to which the wheel is destined in the machine. But Nature’s machines, living bodies, are machines even in their minutest parts and to infinity. This is what constitutes the difference between nature and art, between the divine art and our human art.

<summary>This page explains how Leibniz’s monadology bridges the gap between mind and matter by viewing the body as a "divine machine" that is infinitely complex, unlike human-made machines.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, monadology, dualism, monism, divine machine, automaton, organic body, philosophy of nature</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the exploration of Leibniz’s Monadology, moving from the description of the body as a "natural machine" to the underlying reason for its complexity: the infinite divisibility of matter. It explains how Leibniz replaces the idea of "dead" or "inert" matter with a universe of infinite activity coordinated by a divine plan.</meta>

<page-num>113</page-num>
# INFINITE DIVISIBILITY

This refers to Leibniz’s favorite theme of **pre-established harmony** <term>pre-established harmony: The theory that God coordinated the universe in advance so that every independent soul and every bit of matter acts in perfect synchrony without actually influencing one another.</term>. A man-made machine is designed for one specific purpose; its material is chosen specifically for that task <note>original: "ad hoc"</note> and without considering the material's own "point of view." In the universe of **monads** <term>monads: Simple, soul-like units that Leibniz believed make up the entire universe; they are the fundamental "atoms" of existence, but are centers of force rather than bits of matter.</term>, activities are so harmonized that each monad, while fulfilling its own internal life, also serves the activity of the others. In this way, the entire universe is bound together in one divine purpose.

65. The creator of nature was able to employ this divine and infinitely marvelous craftsmanship <note>original: "artifice"</note> because every portion of matter is not only divisible to infinity—as the ancient philosophers <note>Referring to thinkers like Aristotle or the Scholastics who debated whether space and matter could be divided forever.</note> recognized—but is actually subdivided without end. Each part is broken into further parts, and each of those parts possesses its own unique movement. If it were otherwise, it would be impossible for every tiny piece of matter to "express" <note>In this context, to "express" means that every part of the universe contains a reflection or representation of the state of the entire whole.</note> the universe.

Is there then in Leibniz’s philosophical <note>original: "metaphysical"</note> system no limit to activity, no inert material, no underlying foundation, no unchangeable matrix? Does he merely adopt a skeptical <note>original: "agnostic"</note> attitude and blame our ignorance on a weakness of human reason? Is it not a necessity of thought to assume something other than "activity" exists as its foundation or support—however impossible it might be to actually know it? The supreme value of Leibniz’s philosophical doctrine lies in his firm rejection of this assumption. On the negative side, the **principle of non-contradiction** <term>principle of non-contradiction: A fundamental law of logic stating that a proposition cannot be both true and false at the same time.</term> protects him from it; on the positive side, the mathematical concept of infinity replaces it. The development of modern scientific theory has come to justify his view.

<vocab>Leibniz, pre-established harmony, monads, infinite divisibility, metaphysics, non-contradiction</vocab>

<summary>Leibniz argues that matter is infinitely subdivided into active parts, allowing every portion of the universe to reflect the whole through a pre-arranged divine harmony. This view rejects the existence of "dead" or "inert" matter, relying instead on the logic of infinity and non-contradiction.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, pre-established harmony, monads, infinite divisibility, metaphysics, non-contradiction, philosophy of science</keywords>

--- Page 128 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the infinite divisibility of matter, explaining that for Leibniz, there is no such thing as "dead" matter; instead, even the smallest particle contains an entire universe of life.</meta>

<page-num>114</page-num>
# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

In other words, from a negative perspective, allowing for the possibility of a completely inactive <note>original: "inert"</note> reality would destroy his theory of substance at its very core. On the positive side, his understanding of infinity is the foundation for his theory that "substance" is defined by activity. If he could have seen how much our knowledge of the physical universe has grown today, would it have changed his mind? Clearly not. No matter how we look at the progress of science, it supports Leibniz’s main idea. 

No matter where you look in the universe revealed by science, you find a living world—or at least a world that can only be understood through the lens of life. If you place yourself at any point in the world’s life, an infinite expanse opens up around you—above and below, before and behind—and the entire universe is mirrored within you. 

For instance, today we observe a stellar universe—one of a multitude of stellar universes—and we calculate the spatial distances in hundreds of thousands of light-years. This is simply imagining a living being for whom all these worlds are as tiny and invisible as atoms are to us. The same is true for the infinity below us. An electron is simply the boundary of physical building blocks for a being living on our level of activity. If we were to shrink the human scale down to the size of an electron, its dimensions would seem like massive cosmic magnitudes, complete with "infra-atoms" and "infra-electrons" <note>Leibniz suggests a fractal-like universe where every level of existence contains smaller worlds within it, infinitely.</note>. Ultimately, wherever you stand, it is you—as the active observer—who is the "real" thing, and the physical world you see is your internal perception <note>original: "idea"</note>.

66. From this, we see that a world of creatures, living beings, animals, **entelechies** <term>entelechies: A term Leibniz used for the "soul" or internal force that gives a living thing its purpose and organized form; from the Greek for "having its goal within."</term>, and souls, exists in the tiniest part of matter.

He suggests that what we perceive as "matter" is actually a reflection of the observer's own internal activity.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the infinite divisibility of matter, specifically addressing Leibniz’s rejection of the idea of "dead" matter in favor of a universe defined by constant, living activity.</meta>

<page-num>115</page-num>

-># LIVING ACTIVITY<-

There was a widely held hypothesis, originating in Greek mythology and taking various fanciful forms during the Middle Ages, that the earth—and the physical world in general—is a single animated organic body <note>This refers to the concept of the *Anima Mundi* or World Soul, an idea Leibniz argues against.</note>. In this section and the three which follow, we see that Leibniz’s doctrine has nothing in common with that idea. His theory is that the appearance matter presents to us—however minutely we divide it and isolate parts for analysis—always turns out to be a **mass effect** <note>Leibniz suggests that what we perceive as solid, static "matter" is actually the collective result of countless tiny, active forces working together.</note>. The real substance of the world is **living activity**.

Even this, however, does not fully exhaust the idea. Not only is there no "dead limit" to the universe—no inert or lifeless unit at its physical base—but there is also no limit at which a subjective activity would cease to function. It will always find the means to clothe its <term>entelechy</term> <note>From the Greek *entelecheia*, referring to a soul or internal force that contains the power to realize its own purpose.</note> or soul with an organic body. By rejecting the concepts of absolute space and time, and regarding them instead as a subjectively improvised order, Leibniz raises the concept of "infinity" from a negative value to a positive one. 

Theology supports him in this through its affirmation of infinity in God, and it is undoubtedly from this concept that his doctrine is derived. What he observes is that the positive infinity of God, expressed in omniscience <note>The state of knowing everything.</note> and omnipotence <note>The quality of having unlimited power.</note>, requires a corresponding objective infinity in the created world. What is affirmed with such clarity in these sections is that just as there is no limit of divisibility in a physical reality when conceived geometrically, there is also no limit to individuality in a reality conceived **vitalistically** <note>A philosophical view that life processes are not purely mechanical or physical, but are governed by a vital life-force.</note>. There is no point at which life ceases to be unique and individualized and becomes merely undifferentiated or "blind" activity.

<summary>This page explains Leibniz’s argument that matter is not a dead, solid substance but a "mass effect" of infinite, individual living activities. He links the infinite complexity of the physical world to the positive infinity of God's own nature.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, Entelechy, Vitalism, Infinity, Mass Effect, Monadology, Omnipotence, Organic Body</keywords>

<page-num>116</page-num>

# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

67. Each portion of matter may be conceived as a garden full of plants and as a pond full of fish. But every stem of the plant, every limb of the animal, every drop of sap or blood is also such a garden or pond.

The principle which underlies this whole conception is that of the constancy of magnitudes for all observers and the relativity of magnitudes for all systems of reference. The <term>entelechy</term> <note>From the Greek *entelecheia*, meaning an internal soul-like force that drives a living thing to realize its full potential.</note> of an <term>amœba</term> <note>A single-celled organism that Leibniz uses here to represent life at its most fundamental, microscopic level.</note> or of a white blood corpuscle is not, from its own standpoint, a minute activity in a minute universe with a narrowly restricted range. In endowing it with consciousness, perception, or <term>apperception</term> <note>Leibniz's term for self-awareness; the ability not just to perceive, but to be aware that one is perceiving.</note>, I am thereby raising its magnitudes from the <term>infinitesimal</term> <note>Refers to quantities so small they cannot be measured, approaching zero but never reaching it.</note> character they appear to have to me to equality with my own.

68. And though the ground and air interspersed between the plants of the garden, or the water interspersed between the fish of the pond, may not themselves be plant or fish, yet they contain them, usually of a subtlety which renders them imperceptible.

The obvious objection to the concept of universal individual activity is that, by definition, it is conditioned. Activity implies something active and something acted on. Individuality implies an environment and the presence therein of some reality contraposed either as inert material or as active force. Leibniz meets the objection with the idea of an **improvised inertia** <note>original: "ad hoc inertia"; Leibniz suggests that "matter" or "resistance" is not a separate substance, but rather a temporary effect created for the purpose of interaction.</note>, the effect of **confused perception**. <note>Leibniz argues that we only see "dead" or "solid" matter because our senses are not sharp enough to see the infinite number of tiny living forces within it.</note>

<summary>Leibniz illustrates his theory of infinite life using the analogy of a pond and a garden, arguing that what we perceive as "size" is relative to the observer. He explains that "dead" matter is actually a result of our own confused perception of countless tiny, active living forces.</summary>

->### THE UNIVERSE OF CREATURES<-
->117<-

69. Thus there is nothing arid, sterile, or dead, in the universe, no chaos, no confusion, save in appearance ; exactly as a pond would appear to us at a distance were we able to see only the confused movement of the swarming fish and not the fish themselves.

This completes the image of the universe which is constituted of substantial living units with no substantial inert matrix <note>Leibniz argues against the existence of "dead" matter; even the space between things is filled with microscopic life that we simply cannot perceive.</note>. The conception stands or falls by its coherence and consistency. It cannot be saved by calling in aid the principle of an inactive, non-living substratum <note>An underlying layer or foundation.</note> to its support. The admission of such a reality would be fatal, and so far as Leibniz is concerned there is never the suspicion of a temptation to resort to it. The great merit of this philosophy as a <term>metaphysic</term> <note>The branch of philosophy that examines the fundamental nature of reality, including the relationship between mind and matter.</note> of reality is its clear enunciation of the principle that activity is original and not derived. It is this which makes the old-fashioned theological garments of small consequence in comparison with the importance of its scientific significance.

70. Each living body has a dominant <term>entelechy</term> <note>original: *entelecheia*. A term borrowed from Aristotle, referring to the vital force or "soul" that directs a living being toward its natural purpose.</note>. In the animal this is the soul. The members of this living body are full of other living beings—plants, animals—each of which has also its dominant entelechy or soul.

We are now confronted with the problem of the way in which the entelechy dominates the organism. Existentially the entelechy is a <term>monad</term> <note>original: *monas*. From the Greek for "unit" or "one." Leibniz defines these as the "atoms of nature"—simple, indivisible substances that make up the world.</note> and as such substantially distinct, separate from the body, and

<summary>Leibniz asserts that the universe contains no truly dead or chaotic matter, only life that appears confused from a distance. He establishes that every living body is governed by a dominant soul or entelechy, which in turn presides over a hierarchy of smaller living beings.</summary>

independent of it. *Essentially* it is inseparable from the body for the whole meaning of its activity is the expression of its ends or purposes and these are only expressed in the actions of the body. The body on its part is not a single simple substance or <term>monad</term> <note>From the Greek *monas* (unit); in this context, the fundamental, indivisible building blocks of reality.</note> in ideal relation to the monad which is <term>entelechy</term> <note>original: *entelecheia*. The vital principle or "soul" that directs a living being toward its natural purpose.</note>. The body is a composite, and existentially an aggregate. More than this, the relation of entelechy to organism is repeated within the body indefinitely and holds of all its inexhaustible individual living components <note>Leibniz envisions a "fractal" nature of life, where every part of an organism is itself made of smaller organisms, like a pond full of fish.</note>. How then is it possible to rationalize and represent the actualization of such a relation ? Common sense would say that if there are entelechies they must constitute a class of entities and that their presence in or absence from any group of existents is a simple question of fact. The <term>Gordian knot</term> <note>A metaphor for an intricate problem; Leibniz argues we cannot "cut" the problem by simply assuming the soul and body are separate physical things that occasionally meet.</note> cannot be cut in this direct manner. For Leibniz it is a complete misapprehension of the fact and of the problem. The essence of entelechy is the direction it imposes on the bodily actions. If there are no bodies performing organic actions, integrally controlled, responding to stimuli with co-ordinated voluntary or involuntary movements, then there are no entelechies, or at least nothing which calls for entelechy even as a hypothesis. On the other hand the organism is a closed system of movements. Every movement in the system has its mathematical equivalent, the quantity is conserved in a way which excludes the intrusion even of a directing force <note>Leibniz refers here to the laws of physics; he believed the physical world follows strict mathematical conservation, meaning the "soul" cannot physically push or pull matter.</note>.

How then, does Leibniz meet the difficulty ? There is no interaction between the substance which is the entelechy of a bodily organism and the substances which constitute the existence of that organism each of which is also entelechy in its own domain. quality arises, is perception, and it is in perception that one substance differs from another. This implies that the relations between <term>monads</term> <note>In Leibniz's philosophy, monads are the fundamental, indivisible "atoms" of existence, though they are mental or spiritual rather than physical.</note> are ideal and that every monad is in ideal relation to the whole universe of monads. It is therefore by perception alone, and especially by distinct perception, that the <term>entelechy</term> <note>The "soul" or internal force that directs a living thing toward its purpose.</note> influences the organism. This however gives us no more than an ideal influence. Whence comes the real influence, for there seems to be real influence, when the entelechy acts in and through the organism? Leibniz’s answer is: there is no real influence; what we interpret as real influence is the **harmony of the universe**. This harmony, because it is real, and because it is not brought about by external interference, and because God is not a supervisor adapting individual activities to one another from moment to moment as the needs of the occasion demand, must have been **pre-established**. It entered into the scheme of the creation and bears witness to the wisdom and power of God.

It is interesting to note that Leibniz’s problem arises in precisely the same form in modern psychology in the attempt to define and interpret the relation of mind and body. The **psycho-physical parallelism** <note>The theory that mental and physical events happen at the same time but do not cause one another, like two clocks kept in perfect sync.</note> of the modern psychologist is taken direct from the metaphysical doctrine of Leibniz, with what for Leibniz was the saving feature of the theory, the pre-established harmony, left out.

->120<-

...other inferior living beings which are destined to be always at its service. For all bodies are in a **perpetual flux** <note>original: "perpetual flux" — A reference to the ancient Greek idea (associated with Heraclitus) that everything is in constant motion, like a river that is never the same twice.</note> like rivers; their constituents are continually entering into them and passing out.

There are <term>entelechies</term> <note>From the Greek "entelecheia," meaning to have one's goal within. Leibniz uses this term to describe the soul-like spark that gives a substance its internal life and purpose.</note> wherever there are reals and every real is an entelechy. But entelechy implies the existence of an **organism** over which and in relation to which it is the entelechy. Moreover, it is impossible to conceive entelechies detached and floating, in isolation from the bodies they animate. How then are we to represent the relation between the entelechy and the organism which is its counterpart? It seems to be an attempt to reconcile two opposite and contradictory conceptions. The entelechy by definition both exists in itself and depends on another. 

Leibniz is able to overcome the apparent circularity of his argument by his concept of an <term>actual infinite</term> <note>In philosophy, this refers to a completed infinity that exists all at once, rather than a "potential infinite" which is just a count that never ends.</note>. He conceives the infinite mathematically as actually without limit, although his infinite universe is not spatial and temporal. He thinks the physical universe is inexhaustible by a finite intellect and that this inexhaustibility is only apprehensible by an infinite intellect. We must not read into Leibniz the modern concepts of **“creative evolution”** <note>A reference to Henri Bergson’s 20th-century theory that a "vital impulse" drives evolution.</note> and **“pure activity,”** concepts which are attempts to meet the metaphysical difficulty which he met with his concept of God.

This section touches on a problem which is as acute and baffling in modern theory as it was in Leibniz’s day before the advance of scientific knowledge. How are we to conceive the relation of **form to matter**? <note>These are classic Aristotelian terms: "form" is the organizing principle or essence, while "matter" is the physical substance.</note>

<meta>This page continues the philosophical exploration of Leibniz’s Monadology, moving from the concept of "perpetual flux" in bodies to the relationship between matter and form, and how individual "monads" compose a unified universe.</meta>

# THE MANY ONE

I

There is no form without matter and no matter without form. At what point and precisely how does matter determine form and form determine matter? If, following the general trend of physical theory, we resolve matter into the deformation of a structureless <term>ether</term> <note>In late 19th-century physics, the "ether" was a theorized medium that filled all space, thought to be the substance through which light waves traveled.</note>—if ultimately matter is radiation, a passing form engendered by movement—we must conclude that there cannot be material identity in the organism. Although the organism is the condition of thought, consciousness, and personal identity, it does not endure even for a fraction of a second.

For Leibniz, mind and body are not two separate substances—one thinking and the other taking up space—nor are they two attributes of a single substance. The mind, insofar as it dominates the body, is a <term>simple substance</term> <note>A substance that has no parts and cannot be divided; for Leibniz, this is the definition of a soul or "monad."</note>, individual, a monad. The body is not an opposing reality. In itself, it is not a substance. It is neither permanent nor real in the way the mind is. The constituent <term>monads</term> <note>From the Greek "monas," meaning unity or unit. Leibniz used this to describe the fundamental, indivisible building blocks of the universe.</note> of the body are themselves <term>entelechies</term> <note>A term meaning a soul-like entity that contains its own purpose or "blueprint" within itself.</note>, each dominating an organism, each the "mind" of its own body. The mind-body relation arises not out of the physical meeting of these monadic individuals, but out of the ideal nature of the monad’s activity: its **perception**. This ideal activity determines the composition of the universe of monads without violating the individuality of the components. In this, also, we may see an instance of the contrast, always present to Leibniz, between **physical** and **meta-physical** atoms. If the units of nature are atoms in the sense of the old philosophy (tiny bits of matter), then the world is a mere multiplicity, for there is no principle to hold them together. Monads, on the other hand, by their ideal nature—which makes each monad a **mirror of the universe**—"enter into composites" and make the many one.

| :--- | :--- | :--- | :--- |
| form | organism | substances | metaphysics |

<summary>This page explores Leibniz's argument that the body is not a solid substance but a collection of "monads" or soul-like units. He suggests that while physical matter is in constant flux, the mind provides a unifying principle that allows a complex universe to function as a single whole.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, monadology, matter and form, entelechy, substance, metaphysics, mirror of the universe, atomism</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the mind-body relationship in Leibniz’s system, specifically addressing how the soul persists through physical changes and the unique status of God as a purely spiritual being.</meta>

# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ
122

72. Thus the soul changes its body only gradually and by degrees, so that it is never suddenly despoiled of all its organs. And while in animals there is often **metamorphosis** <note>A biological transformation, such as a caterpillar becoming a butterfly.</note>, there is never **metempsychosis** <note>From the Greek *metempsychosis*, meaning the soul’s move into a new body; often used to describe reincarnation or the "migration" of a soul into an entirely different species.</note>, nor transmigration of souls. There are no disembodied souls, no bodiless **genii** <note>original: "genies." In the 18th-century context, these are minor spirits or supernatural intelligences.</note>. God alone is without body.

We have here the most important practical deductions from the **monadic theory** <note>The belief that the universe is made of "monads"—indivisible, soul-like units that each reflect the entire universe from their own perspective.</note>. First, there are no disembodied minds; second, there is no transmigration of souls; third, God alone is pure mind without body. The last is not, as might appear by its verbal form, in contradiction with the first. “God is a spirit” does not mean that God is disembodied but that embodiment is inapplicable to God. The infinite mind has no body because it possesses **adequate ideas** <note>A philosophical term for thoughts that are perfectly clear, distinct, and complete, leaving no room for confusion.</note> of all existence. In the divine mind there is no region of confused and obscure perception, and it is on this distinction that the mind-body relation depends.

The whole gist of the monadic doctrine is revealed in the rejection of the notion of disembodied minds and its corollary, the transmigration of souls. These ideas belong to the common sense and scientific view of the material basis of the physical world. Living bodies are material things and are conceived as enjoying a status of their own independently altogether of the minds or spiritual substances which animate them. Bodies are inert or passive things, minds are immaterial activities. **Descartes** <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), a French philosopher who argued for "dualism," the idea that the mind and the body are two completely different types of substances.</note> based his argument that the soul is naturally immortal on the fact that it is substantially distinct and separate from the body and

<summary>This page explains Leibniz’s rejection of reincarnation (metempsychosis) in favor of a gradual physical transformation, emphasizing that all created minds must have a body, while God alone is purely spiritual.</summary>
<keywords>Monadology, Leibniz, metempsychosis, transmigration of souls, Descartes, mind-body dualism, divine mind, adequate ideas</keywords>

# BIRTHS AND DEATHS
->123<-

...therefore not involved in the disintegration which overtakes the organism at death. The **monadology** <note>Leibniz’s philosophical system where everything is composed of "monads"—simple, soul-like substances that do not interact physically but reflect the entire universe from their own perspective.</note> bases the argument for immortality on an entirely different ground, namely, on the nature of the monads. Some monads are souls and have bodies, but the bodies are not dead material stuff which the soul animates. The body consists of monads and the soul is the dominating monad. Each of the dominated or subservient monads in its turn has its own range of domination. A disembodied soul therefore would be, for the monadic theory, a self-contradiction. It would be an activity cut off from its perception, a thinking cut off from its thought. The logical basis of the monadic conception is the impossibility of defining existence except in terms of activity. Anything is what it does. Therefore to separate being from doing, thinking from living, mind from body, body from mind, and endow each with an independent substantial reality, is on the monadic principle a **reduction to absurdity** <note>original: *reductio ad absurdum*; a logical method where a claim is proven true by showing that the alternative would lead to an impossible or nonsensical result.</note>.

73. It follows from this that there is neither absolute birth nor complete death in the precise meaning of the separation of the soul and body. What we call *births* are developments and unfoldings, and what we call *deaths* are foldings and shrinkages <note>Leibniz held a "pre-formationist" view, believing that organisms don't start from nothing, but rather expand from an already organized microscopic state.</note>.

Leibniz here is clearly dominated by the concept of the nature of an organized living thing which the microscope seems to have suggested to him <note>In the late 17th century, early microscopists like Leeuwenhoek discovered tiny living "animalcules" in drops of water, leading Leibniz to conclude that life and organization exist at every level of the universe, no matter how small.</note>. The development of an organism, plant or animal, is the opening out of what lies already there, dormant and...

<summary>This page explains Leibniz’s view that souls always remain attached to some form of body, and that birth and death are merely the expansion and contraction of these living structures rather than a beginning or end of existence.</summary>

folded up. Death, he suggests, is the reverse process. He cannot have had microscopic confirmation of this, but the analogy is natural and the argument is supported by the Christian doctrine of the resurrection of the body.

74. Philosophers have been very embarrassed hitherto in their attempts to account for the origin of forms, **entelechies** <term>entelechies</term> <note>derived from the Greek *entelecheia*; Leibniz uses this to describe a soul or vital force that carries its own purpose and "blueprint" within it.</note>, or souls. Today however, exact researches on plants, insects and animals have come to establish the fact that natural organic bodies are not spontaneously produced from a chaos or from putrefaction <note>Leibniz is here rejecting the ancient theory of "spontaneous generation," the belief that living creatures like maggots or mice could be born directly from rotting matter or dust.</note> but always from seeds or germs. In these seeds there can be no doubt there is already some **preformation** <term>preformation</term> <note>the 17th-century biological theory that an embryo does not "form" from scratch, but rather "unfolds" from a tiny, pre-existing version of itself already present in the seed or egg.</note> before their germination. It has been conjectured indeed in the case of the animal that not only is the organic body already existent before conception, but even that there is a soul in this body, and in a word that the identical animal actually exists before conception. Conception is a means of a great transformation of disposition which makes it become an animal of another kind. We see indeed something very closely approaching **generation** <note>original: *génération*; in this context, the process of coming into being or biological birth.</note> when maggots turn into flies, caterpillars into butterflies.

<summary>Leibniz argues against the spontaneous generation of life, suggesting instead that organisms are "preformed" in seeds and that birth and death are merely transformations of a soul that has always existed.</summary>

# PREFORMATION

Leibniz uses the term *conception* here in its technical physiological meaning to denote a stage in the progress of uterine fetal development <note>In the 17th century, "conception" was seen not just as the start of life, but as a major physical expansion or transformation of a creature that already existed in miniature.</note>. The idea of **preformation** <term>preformation</term> <note>The historical theory that an organism exists in a fully formed, though microscopic, state within the seed or egg before development begins.</note> is a very old one and enters largely into the mythology and philosophy of the ancient world. Unfortunately we know today as little as Leibniz knew how and at what stage of the fetal life the individual principle or **entelechy** <term>entelechy</term> <note>Derived from the Greek *entelecheia*; Leibniz uses this to describe a soul or vital force that carries its own purpose and "blueprint" within it.</note> takes form and enters on its work. The metaphysical doctrine, which he supports by these reflections on the development of the embryo, is that every **monad** <term>monad</term> <note>From the Greek *monas* (unit); for Leibniz, these are the fundamental, indivisible building blocks of the universe.</note> came into being with the creation of the universe and can only go out of existence with the annihilation of the created universe.

75. There are therefore two orders or grades of animal existence. First, the animals which by means of *conception* rise from the lowest to the highest grade: they may be distinguished by the term *spermatic* <note>Leibniz is referring to "animalcules" (spermatozoa) observed under early microscopes; he believed these tiny creatures contained the preformed "seeds" of larger animals.</note>. Second, those which remain in the *lower* grade and never rise above it. These, which are overwhelmingly the greater number, come to birth, multiply, and are destroyed, just like the animals which rise to the higher order, but they remain on the lower stage. Only a very small elect number passes upward to the great stage.

The preformation hypothesis, which was very attractive to thinkers in the seventeenth century, has ceased to be scientifically tenable, yet the arguments which Leibniz based on it are easily adaptable to modern theories of development.

<summary>This page explains Leibniz’s theory of preformation, which suggests that all souls have existed since the creation of the universe and merely transform in size and complexity. It introduces the distinction between "spermatic" animals that undergo major development and the vast majority of microscopic life that remains in a lower state.</summary>

theories of evolution and to the facts of heredity. In this section he seeks to explain a simple consequence of the **preformation theory** <term>preformation</term> <note>The 17th-century belief that an embryo is not "built" from scratch, but unfolds from a tiny, fully-formed version of the adult already present in the seed or egg.</note>, namely, how it is that so many, indeed the vast majority, of fully constituted individuals never get the chance to play their part, and what governs the selection of the select few.

In the case of plants, only a very few out of a vast number of fully potential individuals actually reach the stage of germination, growth, flowering, and fruiting; and in the case of animals there are vast numbers of germs—ova and spermatozoa—that live and die unfertilized, without reaching the stage of conception or any passage to the higher life of the organic living being. It follows that there is in nature a hierarchy of opportunities as well as a hierarchy of activities. The prodigality <note>Nature’s "prodigality" refers to its extreme wastefulness or abundance; for every one seed that grows, thousands are lost.</note> of nature is amazing. What Leibniz emphasizes is that the individual life which plays its part in the world—that is, the individual animal of a species born to an independent life—does not date its existence from its birth, or even from conception in the womb. It has no actual beginning other than the act of creation which brought the universe of **monads** <term>monad</term> <note>From the Greek word for "unit," these are the simple, indestructible soul-like entities that Leibniz believed made up the entire universe.</note> to existence.

76. In this, however, we have no more than half the truth, and I am led to the conclusion that if the animal has no natural beginning, it can have no natural end. Not only is generation <note>In this context, "generation" refers to the creation of life from nothing.</note> impossible, but equally an entire destruction. There is no death in the absolute meaning. The **arguments based on experience** <note>original: "a posteriori"</note> <term>a posteriori</term> <note>Knowledge gained from looking at the world and conducting experiments.</note> based on experiments which lead to this conclusion agree perfectly with my principles **deduced from reason** <note>original: "a priori"</note> <term>a priori</term> <note>Knowledge gained through logic and self-evident truths, independent of physical experience.</note>.

<summary>Leibniz argues that since no life truly "begins" at birth—having existed as a "preformed" germ since the dawn of creation—no life can truly "end" at death, suggesting a form of natural immortality for all living things.</summary>

->THE UNION OF SOUL AND BODY<-

The *a priori* <note>original: "a priori"; reasoning based on theoretical deduction and logic rather than observation.</note> principles to which Leibniz refers are those which establish the indestructibility of the **monad** <term>monad</term> <note>From the Greek word for "unit," these are the simple, indestructible soul-like entities that Leibniz believed made up the universe.</note> as a simple substance. The *a posteriori* <note>original: "a posteriori"; reasoning based on observed facts and empirical evidence.</note> arguments are those which conclude that the organic individual is preformed in the ovum. The **preformation theory** <term>preformation</term> <note>The 17th-century belief that an embryo is not "built" from scratch, but unfolds from a tiny, fully-formed version of the adult already present in the seed or egg.</note> makes it impossible to fix any point of development as an absolute beginning, and equally makes it impossible to fix any point as an absolute ending. If birth is not a natural beginning, death is not a natural ending. The argument is not a special plea for the immortality of the soul; it is rather an expression of satisfaction that ordinary empirical facts accord with the metaphysical principle of the **pre-established harmony** <term>pre-established harmony</term> <note>Leibniz’s theory that the soul and body do not directly affect each other, but were synchronized by God at the beginning of time like two perfectly timed clocks.</note>.

77. We are able to say then not only that the soul (*mirror of an indestructible universe*) is indestructible, but the animal itself is indestructible, although its machine <note>Leibniz viewed the physical body as a "divine machine" or a complex biological mechanism that follows the laws of motion.</note> often perishes in part as it puts on or takes off organic wrappings.

Leibniz is preparing to develop his metaphysical doctrine on the **finalistic side** <note>The "finalistic" side refers to teleology, or the study of purposes and ends, rather than just mechanical causes.</note>. The realm of ends which is now coming into view must be based on clear and distinct ideas of the nature of the reality of the universe.

78. These principles enable me to offer a natural explanation of the union of soul and body, or, as I should rather say, of the conformity of the soul with the organism. The soul

<meta>This page concludes a discussion on the indestructibility of the "animal machine" and transitions into a detailed comparison of the three major philosophical theories regarding how the soul and the body interact.</meta>

# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

...obeys its own laws, the body also its own, and the two accord by virtue of the **pre-established harmony** <term>pre-established harmony</term> <note>Leibniz’s famous theory that the soul and body do not directly influence each other; instead, God synchronized them at the moment of creation like two perfect clocks that always show the same time without being connected.</note> between all substances since all are representations of one and the same universe.

It appeared to Leibniz that the relation of mind and body could only be one of three possibilities. These are: **direct action** of one on the other; **continuous adaptation** of one to the other; and **pre-established harmony** between the two. The first was rejected in Leibniz's day on metaphysical <note>Metaphysics is the branch of philosophy that deals with the fundamental nature of reality, including the relationship between mind and matter.</note> grounds, and it is rejected today by those who reject it on scientific grounds. An action of mind on matter, or matter on mind, is impossible to conceive, and as matter of fact is inexpressible in any mathematical equation. The second requires as its postulate the continuous and uninterrupted activity of God. It was advocated in the seventeenth century by **Malebranche** <note>Nicolas Malebranche (1638–1715) was a French philosopher and priest who believed that created things have no power of their own and that God is the only true cause of change.</note> and the **occasionalists** <term>occasionalists</term> <note>A school of thought arguing that mind and body only *seem* to interact; in reality, God intervenes on every "occasion" (for example, when you decide to move your hand, God moves it for you).</note>. The third also presupposes the intervention of God but identifies the relation with the unique principle which underlies the creative work itself. "**Conformity**" much better than "**union**" expresses Leibniz's idea of this relation. The various organs of the body and their different functions are unified in a quite different meaning to that in which soul and body conform in one activity. The soul is an organization of spiritual experience obeying its own laws, the body is an organization of mechanical movements quantitatively compensated. Ultimately there is only one principle on which the whole universe holds together and that is the principle by which every real constituent activity mirrors in itself the one and identical universe.

<summary>Leibniz argues that the soul and body do not directly affect one another, but rather function in "conformity" through a divine synchronization, rejecting the idea of constant divine intervention (Occasionalism).</summary>

### PRE-ESTABLISHED HARMONY

of which it is a constituent, and in this way ideally represents within itself all other activities. It is this principle which makes every <term>monad</term> <note>In Leibniz's philosophy, a monad is an indivisible, soul-like point that makes up the fundamental building block of the universe.</note> dominant within a certain range and sphere. It is the ideal relation alone which makes the universe one. The mind-body relation, accordingly, is a relation of end to means—of purpose to performance. It enables the <term>entelechy</term> <note>From the Greek for "having a goal within," this refers to the soul or the active force that gives a living thing its vital purpose.</note> to find expression and to do work; however, it is a kind of **a kingdom within a kingdom** <note>original: "imperium in imperio"; a phrase used to describe a self-governing entity that exists within a larger one, yet follows its own laws.</note>, not fundamental like the relation which makes each <term>monad</term> a mirror of all others.

To give full expression to Leibniz’s thought, we would have to deny the existence of the body in the same sense in which he denied that "things" exist. The body is no more than the transient shape and passing form which gives expression to the activities of the soul. In itself, the body is neither one, nor spatial, nor temporal; it is a "many"—a composite without real internal relations. Its appearance in space and time is merely the expression to others of the activity of the dominant <term>entelechy</term>, for which alone the body exists as an instrument of activity.

From this we may see why <term>pre-established harmony</term> <note>The theory that God synchronized every substance in the universe from the beginning, so they appear to interact even though they only follow their own internal "scripts."</note> is so essential a part of Leibniz’s scheme. No <term>monad</term> is or can be forced by another. Therefore, the sort of relation which brings about action and devises means to an end must be conformity, and conformity can only follow from understanding. This is the ground of Leibniz’s intellectualism.

Bodies act in accordance with the laws of **efficient causes** <note>The "how" or the mechanical force behind an action (e.g., the muscles and nerves move the arm through physical energy).</note> or movements. The well known illustration Leibniz used to express his idea of the nature of the relation of the mind to the body—the two clocks of the skilful clockmaker so consummately designed that without requiring supervision the state of one would always indicate the state of the other—although it serves to explain what is meant by <term>psycho-physical parallelism</term> <note>The theory that mental and physical events happen simultaneously and in perfect sync without actually causing or touching one another.</note> is misleading if taken as a complete account of the matter. The body, which is independent of the mind at the same time that there is point to point correspondence between the two activities, is not one thing but many things, and the composition of the many is not invariable but changing continually, and yet in relation to the mind the body acts as one. It is not however the <term>monads</term> <note>The fundamental, indivisible units of reality in Leibniz’s system.</note> which compose the body but the relation of these <term>monadic activities</term> to the activity of the mind which constitute the body a unity. The body is a unity because in exhibiting the <term>laws of efficient causation</term> or movement it carries out the ends of the mind, the mind exhibiting the <term>laws of final causes</term>.

We may therefore sketch for ourselves a much more complete idea of the scheme of <term>monadic activity</term> in the <term>mind-body relation</term> if instead of two clocks made by a skilled artisan we take as illustration an institution or corporation such, for example, as a bank. In such a business organization the governing body will stand for the mind and the various officials for the body. The banker, we shall say, in Leibniz’s words “acts in accordance with the laws of final causes” <note>The banker acts based on intentions and goals.</note>; the various officials, the cashiers, the book-keepers, the discounters, the checkers of securities, act collectively

This gives us a fairly complete example of the <term>mind-body relation</term> <note>The philosophical challenge of explaining how a non-physical mind or soul can be connected to and interact with a physical, material body.</note>, in illustrating how independent individual activities, in, and through, and by means of, a dominant directing activity, take a form which, without destroying or even invading the free composite individualities, issue in a common function. 

Here however we may imagine someone to object that it is not the staff of officials but a certain material structure which stands for the body of the organism. The bank, it will be said, with all its complete staff of officials cannot effectively work without an office for the transaction of its business, a strong room for the safe custody of securities, with all the accompanying accessories. It is true enough, we admit in reply, that the bank involves these material accessories but so far as the analogy goes they no more correspond to the body of the living animal or are essential to the bank’s business, than the clothes a man wears or the tools he employs are identical with his living body. The relation of mind and body is a quite distinct problem however it may seem to involve the general problem of the status of the material world and the physical law of the **equivalence of action and reaction** <note>A fundamental principle of physics (later formalized by Newton) stating that for every action there is an equal and opposite reaction.</note>.

...thought however that the soul could change the direction of bodies, for in his time the law of nature that the direction of the movement of bodies is conserved was unknown. Had <term>Descartes</term> <note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the French philosopher who argued that mind and body are two completely different substances that meet in the pineal gland of the brain.</note> been aware of this he might have chanced on my system of the **pre-established harmony**.

<margin>Descartes held that the body is an automatic machine and the principle of a machine is the conservation of force. Force, he held, consists wholly of movement which accordingly must be constant in quantity. To suppose therefore that the soul imparts force to the body, increasing or decreasing the amount of movement, is directly to contradict the principle on which the physical world is unified. Descartes supposed however that the soul might influence the body by directing or guiding the force already possessed by the body. Had he seen the significance of the first law of movement, that a moving body can only be changed in direction by force, he would have had no alternative but to regard the inclinations of the soul and the adaptations of the body as independent of one another, and their correlation as the result of a **pre-established harmony**.</margin>

81. The **pre-established harmony** is the system according to which bodies act as though <note>original: "per impossibile"</note> *even if it were impossible* there were no souls, and souls act as though there were no bodies; and together they act as though they influenced one another.

<summary>This page explains Leibniz's "pre-established harmony" by contrasting it with Descartes' view, arguing that because physical direction is conserved, the soul cannot physically "steer" the body.</summary>

# THE PRINCIPLE OF CONTINUITY

It is impossible there should be no souls because souls are the **simple substances** of which the universe consists and therefore were there no souls there would be no bodies. Bodies are composites, and the simples of which composites consist are the **monads** <note>From the Greek *monas*, meaning unit. Leibniz uses this term to describe the fundamental, indivisible building blocks of reality; they are "spiritual atoms" rather than physical ones.</note>. All the monads are not souls, but souls only differ from simple monads by their position in the **hierarchy** <note>Leibniz believed in a "Great Chain of Being," where everything from a speck of dust to God exists on a single continuous scale of complexity and consciousness.</note>. The reason why there can be no interaction between mind and body is not because they are substantially different but because the monads which constitute the reality of the body do not give it the status of the body. It is only for the **dominant monad** <note>The central "soul" or "mind" that organizes a cluster of simpler monads into what we perceive as a single living body.</note> or mind that the body is body; the mind therefore could only act on the body by acting on the monads which constitute the body, and there is no interaction between monads.

Leibniz is preparing to present his doctrine of the **principle of continuity** <note>The law that "Nature never makes leaps" (*Natura non facit saltus*). Everything in the universe changes by degrees rather than sudden jumps.</note> in the universe in his concept of the supreme monad. The only real bond between the monads is God on whom they depend. This dependence on God is not merely the efficiency implied in their creation but the finality in realizing God's end. God's relation to the created monads is that of the dominant monad or mind to the body. God realizes his purposes not by coercing or violating the natures of his creatures but by **overruling** <note>In this context, it means "harmonizing" or "guiding" rather than suppressing.</note> their independent activities to the fulfilment of an end of his own.

82. There is a peculiarity in regard to <term>minds</term> <note>Also called "rational souls," these are monads capable of self-reflection and understanding eternal truths, distinguishing humans from animals.</note> or rational souls. While it is fundamentally true of animals and all living things that their beginning is with the beginning of the world and that they last as long as the world, yet rational animals have this character

...that the **rational soul** <note>In Leibniz’s philosophy, this is the highest level of soul, capable of reason, self-reflection, and understanding eternal truths, distinguishing humans from animals.</note> is not present in them from the very beginning. The **infinitesimal animals** <note>Original: *animalcules*. This refers to the microscopic organisms discovered by early scientists like Leeuwenhoek; Leibniz believed these contained the "seeds" of all future generations.</note> which are generated in the sperm, while they are merely "spermatic" animals, possess only simple, ordinary, or sensitive souls. However, when the "elect" among them—as we might say—attain their full human nature through the act of conception, their sensitive souls are raised in dignity to the status of minds and become **rational souls**.

This curious idea is a logical consequence of the **preformation theory** <term>preformationism</term> <note>The now-obsolete biological theory that an embryo does not "grow" but rather "unfolds" from a tiny, fully-formed version of the organism that was already present in the parent's seed since the beginning of creation.</note>. This concept has its own version of the problems found in modern biology. Myriads of individuals, each possessing the potential to become a rational soul, exist throughout the universe. In Leibniz’s theory, not only was the entire future lineage of **Adam** <note>The biblical first man. Leibniz uses this to suggest that all humans who will ever live were physically "pre-packed" inside the first human.</note> preformed and already existing within Adam when he was first created, but there exists an even more immensely vast number of **monads**, each with the full possibility of maturing into an individual. Furthermore, we must assume that all these microscopic bodies exist alongside their animal souls, and from among them, a select few become rational monads in the specific sense of the word. Leibniz undoubtedly saw in this **Principle of Nature** an analogy with the **Principle of Grace** <note>A theological comparison: just as nature "chooses" very few seeds to become adults, God's grace "chooses" the elect for salvation.</note>. Many are called and few are chosen, and the overwhelming majority are not even called at all.

83. Among all the differences I have been noticing between ordinary souls and rational minds, the chief distinction is this: while souls in general are **living mirrors** <note>Leibniz’s famous metaphor suggesting that every soul reflects the entire universe from its own unique perspective.</note> or images of the universe of created things, rational minds are images of the Creator himself, the divine...

<summary>This page explains Leibniz’s theory on the origin of the human soul, arguing that rational souls develop from simpler "animal" souls through a process of divine selection and biological unfolding. It links 17th-century microscopy and the "preformation" theory to theological concepts of grace and the hierarchy of being.</summary>

# NATURE AND GRACE

->K<-

author of nature. **Rational minds** are capable of knowing the system of the universe and to some extent imitating it by **architectonic patterns** <note>This refers to the way humans can design and build complex systems—like architecture, laws, or science—that mirror the logical and organized structure of God’s own creation.</note>. Every rational mind is as it were a **little divinity** <note>Original: *petit divinité*. Leibniz suggests that because humans possess reason, we share a spark of the divine creative power, allowing us to govern our own small "worlds" of influence.</note> in its own department.

We now come to the completion of the **metaphysical scheme** <note>The overarching philosophical system Leibniz constructed to explain the nature of reality, substance, and God.</note>. The full implication of the argument is that what we may learn by studying our own relation to the universe shows us also the relation in which we must ourselves stand to the **supreme monad** <term>supreme monad</term> <note>In Leibniz’s hierarchy, this is God—the ultimate, simplest, and most powerful unit of existence who coordinates the harmony of all other monads.</note>. The ideal bond which unites the free active constituents of the universe into a harmonious system not only embraces our human relations of mind and body, spirit and matter, agent and sphere of activity, but also shows us our own free spirits dominated and overruled by the supreme spirit, in such way that God's ends are fulfilled in our self-originated, self-directed actions.

The relation of mind to body, the relation of the **dominant monad** achieving its ends without interfering with the subservient activities which provide the efficiency, typifies the relation in which we stand to one another as members of the human society, and the relation in which we may stand to God who fulfils his ends by means of free spirits fulfilling their ends.

### 84.
This is what makes **rational minds** capable of entering into a kind of **society with God** <note>A key Leibnizian concept where humans are not just subjects of nature, but moral partners with the divine in a "Universal Monarchy" or the "City of God."</note>, so that to them God is not as an inventor to his machine (this being his relation to his other creatures) but as a **Prince** to his subjects, and even as a **Father** to his children.

<meta>This page continues the discussion of the relationship between rational minds and the divine. # THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

Throughout the **monadology** <term>monadology</term> <note>Leibniz’s philosophical system which proposes that the universe is made of "monads"—indestructible, soul-like units that do not physically interact but are coordinated by God.</note> Leibniz has taken the **discursive nature of reason** <note>This refers to "discursive" reasoning, where the mind arrives at truths through a step-by-step logical process rather than through direct, instant intuition.</note> for granted, and presents no special theory of it. Intercourse he seems to regard as fact and its possibility is not for him a problem. The modern interpreter however meets with a difficulty at once when he tries to reconcile the insistence on the **non-interaction of the monads** (which have **neither doors nor windows**) <note>One of Leibniz's most famous metaphors. He argues that monads are entirely self-contained; nothing "flows" in or out of them. Instead, they appear to interact because God pre-synchronized them like two perfectly timed clocks.</note> with the assumption of an interchange of ideas by means of impressions on the organs of sense.

The passage before us clearly means that as reason is in its nature discursive, and as rational minds are capable of intercommunication, so there is for them the possibility and evidence of the actuality of intercourse with God. Our relation to the **Supreme Being** is not merely that of rational monads to one another. We belong to a society over which an invisible Prince presides, or to a family of which a Father stands at the head. It is our rational nature which enables us to communicate with God and to receive from him the direct revelation of his will.

**85.** We are led to conclude that the assemblage of all rational minds is the **City of God**. It is the most perfect state possible under the most perfect monarch.

Observe that Leibniz conceives the **City of God** as a true universal. It is not as it was for **St. Augustine** <note>A reference to the 5th-century philosopher's work *The City of God*. While Augustine separated the spiritual "City of God" from the earthly world, Leibniz argues they are integrated into one metaphysical reality.</note> the church in separation from the world; it is not a kingdom of heaven confronted by an opposing kingdom of darkness. The idea is essentially **meta-physical** <note>Dealing with the fundamental nature of reality and existence itself, rather than just physical or religious observation.</note>, whatever theological significance it may have.

<summary>This page explores how Leibniz uses the concept of "discursive reason" to justify a moral and social relationship between humans and God, culminating in the idea of the "City of God." It highlights the shift from monads as isolated units to members of a universal community governed by a divine monarch.</summary>
<keywords>Monadology, Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, City of God, discursive reason, non-interaction of monads, Supreme Being, St. Augustine, metaphysics, rational minds</keywords>

<page-num>137</page-num>
# THE CITY OF GOD

The fundamental character of the system of the **pre-established harmony** <term>pre-established harmony</term> <note>A central idea in Leibniz's philosophy: God synchronized all the "clocks" of the universe at the beginning of time so they appear to interact, even though every soul or "monad" is actually independent.</note> is that everything in the world, every constituent **monad** <term>monad</term> <note>From the Greek word "monas" meaning unit. These are the basic, indivisible building blocks of Leibniz's universe—more like tiny souls or points of force than physical atoms.</note>, is an essential and necessary part of the whole. The **City of God** is thus in a way what in contemporary philosophy is termed the **Absolute** <note>A term often used in 19th-century philosophy to describe the ultimate, all-inclusive reality that contains everything within itself.</note>, the reality transcending individual experience in which all contradictions are reconciled. It involves therefore the idea that **evil**—physical and moral—is **negative** <note>Leibniz argues for "privative" evil: the idea that evil isn't a "thing" created by God, but rather a lack of perfection or a "shadow" that exists only where goodness is limited.</note>. Evil does not exist in its own right. It has not to be cast out that good may triumph, and it does not set up for itself an opposition.

86. <note>This refers to Section 86 of Leibniz's "Monadology," which this text is analyzing.</note> This **City of God**, this really universal monarchy, is a **moral world** in the **natural world**. It is what is noblest and divinest in the works of God. In it God’s glory really consists, since there would be none were his greatness and goodness not known and admired by rational minds. It is also relatively to this divine city that there is **goodness** in the strict meaning, that is, something other than **wisdom** and **power** which manifest themselves everywhere.

The **natural world** <note>The universe seen as a machine governed by physical laws and cause-and-effect.</note> and the **moral world** <note>The universe seen as a community of spirits governed by purpose and justice.</note> are not numerically two nor are they in the relation of part to whole. Just as the attributes **wisdom**, **power** and **goodness**, do not exist apart in the divine essence, so the moral world does not exist apart from the natural world.

<summary>This page explains Leibniz’s "City of God" as a moral community existing within the natural universe, where evil is viewed as a mere lack of perfection rather than a competing force. It argues that God’s true glory is only realized when rational minds recognize his goodness within this moral order.</summary>
<keywords>pre-established harmony, monads, City of God, the Absolute, privation of evil, moral world vs natural world, divine attributes, Leibnizian ethics</keywords>

# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

87. We have already seen that a perfect harmony exists between the two natural realms, the realm of **efficient causes** <term>efficient causes</term> <note>The mechanical "how" of the world—the physical chain of events where one thing pushes another, like gears in a clock.</note> and the realm of **final causes** <term>final causes</term> <note>The "why" or purpose of things—the goals and intentions that guide the actions of rational beings.</note>. We have now to show another harmony between the physical realm of nature and the moral realm of **grace** <term>grace</term> <note>While usually a religious term, Leibniz uses "grace" here to describe the moral laws that govern spirits, as opposed to the physical laws that govern bodies.</note>, that is to say, between God considered as **architect of the machine of the universe** <note>Leibniz often compares the physical universe to a vast, perfectly designed machine created by a master engineer.</note> and God considered as **Monarch of the divine city of rational minds**.

The term “grace” is not used here in the strict theological sense although it is from the theological doctrine it derives its meaning. Leibniz does not confine it to the immediate work of God in the individual soul, but identifies it with the general moral realm. It is not the concept of a scheme of salvation by which some are **elect** <note>Those chosen by God for salvation.</note> and some **reprobate** <note>Those rejected by God or destined for damnation.</note>, it is the general scheme of a world in which final causes are the correlative and counterpart of efficient causes.

88. This harmony makes things lead to grace by the ways of nature, and shows us, for example, that the earth must be destroyed and renewed by natural ways when the government of minds requires it, for **chastisement** <note>Punishment meant to correct or discipline.</note> in the one case and reward in the other.

The thought here is profound, and reveals to us the key idea in Leibniz’s mind. The natural world is not the stage on which the moral drama is played, and the
