mechanism of that world is not interfered with as occasion requires to inculcate moral lessons. On the contrary, what we from our finite standpoint see in the moral world is the fuller and higher expression of the final end of the author of nature, while what we see in the natural world is the **efficient cause** <term>efficient cause</term> <note>The physical 'how'—the chain of events or mechanical forces that make something happen.</note> by which the **final cause** <term>final cause</term> <note>The 'why'—the ultimate goal or purpose behind an action.</note> is actualized. Yet the two worlds are not different aspects of one and the same world, rather are they partial views. Let us take for example the illustration which Leibniz has followed out in the *Theodicy* <note>original: "Théodicée," Leibniz’s famous work defending the idea that we live in the "best of all possible worlds."</note> (405-417). It is the story of **Tarquin the Proud** <note>original: "Tarquinius Superbus," the legendary seventh and final king of ancient Rome, whose tyrannical rule and eventual overthrow led to the birth of the Roman Republic.</note>. We see the havoc and waste in the natural world, the sin and presumption in the moral world, and both, it seems to us, could have been left out of the creative scheme to the advantage of a world which in each aspect, the natural and the moral, would then be better than it is. To the extent however to which we can rise to a complete view we see the two sets of conditions mutually righting one another, even to the degree that we are able to say, it would be a worse world and not a better from which Tarquin and the consequences of his usurpation should have been excluded. The master theme in Leibniz’s speculation is that God works, not mechanically and piecemeal, but with the whole scheme present from the beginning so that the harmony of the created universe is complete in its inception and not waiting for its far off end.

-># THE BEST POSSIBLE WORLD<-
<page-num>141</page-num>

and yet to be content with what God brings to pass effectively by his **secret, consequent and decisive will** <note>Leibniz distinguishes between God's "antecedent" will (the desire for every individual good) and his "consequent" will (the final decision made after weighing all possibilities to create the best overall result).</note>. It leads us to recognize that were we able to understand sufficiently the order of the universe we should find it surpass our wisest wishes, and see that a better world than the world which is could not be. Not only is the world the best possible for all in general, it is the best possible for us in particular, if we are attached as we should be to the author of all, not only as architect and <term>efficient cause</term> <note>The physical agent or mechanical force that brings something into being; the "how" of existence.</note> of our being but also as our Lord, the <term>final cause</term> <note>The purpose, goal, or "end" toward which something is directed; the "why" of existence.</note> who should be the only goal of our will and who alone can make our happiness.

The *Monadology* is now seen to be a theory of **metaphysical optimism** <note>In a philosophical context, "optimism" does not mean a sunny disposition, but rather the technical belief that this is the "optimum" or best possible version of reality that could exist.</note>. The world is the best of all possible worlds, not in the meaning that it is the best under the circumstances, or that it possesses a clear balance of good over evil, or that the choice is between necessary evil and no world at all. It is best in the absolute meaning. We may see in it the expression of perfect wisdom, perfect power, and perfect goodness. Evil is not explained away, either in the physical meaning of pain or in the moral meaning of sin, nor do pain and sin lose their character, nor are the free individual agents relieved of responsibility for evil. constituent parts, so the universe is valued for its individuality and not for its constituent individuals.

Thus the <term>Monadology</term> <note>Leibniz’s 1714 work, which summarizes his entire philosophy in 90 short paragraphs.</note> which begins with the concept of the <term>monad</term> <note>From the Greek *monas* (unit); Leibniz’s term for the fundamental, indivisible "spiritual atoms" that make up all of reality.</note> as a simple substance and constituent unit of the universe closes with the concept of the <term>City of God</term> <note>A moral community of all rational minds (spirits) under God; a concept Leibniz adapted from St. Augustine to describe the universe as a perfect state.</note> as the embodiment of the principle of the <term>pre-established harmony</term>. A study of Leibniz’s early writings shews that the order in which he conceived his ideas was the reverse of this. The idea of the system led him to the definition of the <term>monad</term> and the concept of its nature. The <term>pre-established harmony</term> <note>The theory that God perfectly synchronized all things in the universe at the moment of creation, so that they appear to interact even though they only follow their own internal programming.</note> seems to have occurred to him early in his philosophical reflexion and to have been excogitated by him as a possible way of interpreting the relation of God to the universe.

The problem in its first form was theological and doubtless had reference to the controversy then troubling the peace of the church, the problem of <term>free-will</term> versus <term>grace</term>, whether it was for all men or only for the elect that Christ died <note>A reference to the fierce 17th-century debates between Jansenists, Jesuits, and Protestants regarding predestination and how much "choice" a person has in their salvation.</note>. It raised however a more fundamental problem for the philosopher, that of the creation of free spirits with its consequence in the existence of evil and sin. The very idea of harmony presupposes the clash of private ends. Take any work of art—a symphony, a landscape, a building—and break it into its constituent parts, you have only opposition and conflict, inconsistency, stresses and strains. The harmony is not an outcome of the assemblage of the constituent units. It is <term>pre-established</term>. It represents the <term>final cause</term> <note>The purpose or "why" behind something; the goal toward which a process is moving.</note> for which the constituent units are the <term>efficient cause</term> <note>The mechanical force or "how" of a process; the physical movement that produces an effect.</note>, and is related to them as the end to the means.

It argues that the universe’s harmony is a pre-planned design by God that justifies the existence of individual struggles and "free spirits."</summary>

# THE ORIGIN
## OF THE THEORY OF MONADS

*Leibniz’s account of his philosophical development in a letter to <note>Nicolas Remond (1676–1725) was a high-ranking French official and a devoted correspondent of Leibniz during his final years.</note> Nicolas Remond, July 1714.*

My chief concern has always been edification <note>In this context, "edification" refers to the moral and spiritual improvement of his readers.</note>, but I have sought continually to disinter the truth which lies buried beneath the opinions of the different sects and also to reunite the knowledge dissipated by them, and in doing this I think I have been successful in contributing something of my own towards a new advance. The occasions of my studies from my childhood onwards have provided the means. I learnt my Aristotle when a child. I was not repulsed by the <term>Scholastics</term> <note>Scholasticism was the medieval school of philosophy that used logic to defend Christian dogma, largely based on Aristotle.</note> even then, and I certainly do not regret it now. Also while still quite young I took delight in Plato and Plotinus, not to mention other ancient philosophers whom I read somewhat later. When I came to know the moderns I soon emancipated myself from the trivialities of the <term>Schoolmen</term> <note>Another term for the Scholastic philosophers.</note>. I can well remember walking by myself in a wood near Leipzig, named <note>The Rosental (Valley of Roses) remains a well-known park in Leipzig today.</note> Rosendal, when only fifteen, and deliberating whether I should give up <term>Substantial Forms</term> <note>The Aristotelian idea that objects have an internal essence or "soul" that gives them their properties.</note>. It was then that mechanism <note>The 17th-century view that all natural phenomena can be explained through the motion and impact of physical parts, like a clock.</note> took hold of me and led me to apply myself to mathematics. It was not till much later, not in fact till after I had met

<note>Leibniz lived in Paris from 1672 to 1676, a period of immense growth where he met the greatest minds of the age.</note> Huyghens at Paris, that I devoted myself deeply to that study. When, however, searching for the basis of mechanism I came to study the laws of movement, I discovered to my surprise that it was impossible to derive these laws from mathematics and that I must go beneath mathematics to metaphysics. This brought me to <term>Entelechies</term> <note>Derived from the Greek word for "having a goal within," this refers to a soul-like force that gives life and direction to matter.</note>, and led me from the material to the formal, and made me understand at last after several corrections and progress in my notions, that the <term>Monads</term> <note>Leibniz’s term for the fundamental, indivisible "atoms" of existence. Unlike physical atoms, monads are spiritual or mental centers of force.</note> or simple substances are the only real substances, and that material things are phenomena, however well-founded and interconnected they may be. <note>Leibniz argued that the physical world we see is a "well-founded phenomenon"—it is not an illusion, but it is a mental representation of the underlying reality of Monads.</note> Plato and also the later Academicians and the Sceptics had glimpsed this truth, but the successors of Plato did not make such good use of it as he had.

I have found generally that the Sects <note>By "sects," Leibniz refers to the various competing schools of philosophy, such as the Aristotelians, Cartesians, and Platonists.</note> are for the most part right in what they affirm and wrong in what they deny. The Formalists, both Platonists and Aristotelians, are right in seeking the source of things in <term>final and formal causes</term> <note>In traditional philosophy, "final causes" are the purposes or goals of a thing, while "formal causes" are the designs or essences that make a thing what it is.</note>. They are wrong in neglecting <term>efficient and material causes</term> <note>"Efficient causes" are the direct physical triggers of an event, and "material causes" are the physical substances involved.</note>, and inferring, as the English philosopher Henry More <note>Henry More (1614–1687) was a leader of the Cambridge Platonists who believed that mechanical laws alone could not explain the natural world.</note> and other Platonists do, that there are some phenomena which cannot be mechanistically explained. Yet on the other hand the materialists who regard philosophy as wholly mechanistic, are wrong in rejecting metaphysical

<summary>Leibniz recounts how his study of physics in Paris led him to realize that mechanical laws require a metaphysical foundation, resulting in his theory of Monads. He argues for a synthesis of philosophical schools, suggesting that truth is found by combining physical "mechanistic" explanations with metaphysical "purposive" ones.</summary>

considerations and attempting to explain everything by what depends on the imagination. <note>By "imagination," Leibniz refers to things we can visualize or picture, like the physical shape and movement of objects, rather than abstract rational principles.</note>

I flatter myself that I have succeeded in penetrating to the harmony of these different realms. In my view both sides are right so long as they do not clash. All the phenomena of nature proceed mechanistically and metaphysically, but the source of mechanism is in metaphysics. <note>The OCR reads "is not metaphysics," but in the original French (*la source du mechanisme est dans la metaphysique*), Leibniz argues that the laws of physics are grounded in deeper spiritual or logical principles.</note> To unravel the mystery was no easy matter since so few will take the trouble to unite the two kinds of study. Descartes did so but fell short. In most of his dogmas he proceeds too rapidly. His philosophy may be called the antechamber of truth. The greatest hindrance to his progress was his ignorance of the true laws of mechanics and movement.

Huygens <note>Christiaan Huygens (1629–1695) was a leading Dutch mathematician and physicist who made major breakthroughs in the study of light and motion.</note> was the first to perceive these though even he fell short through his distaste for metaphysics, in common with so many other clever men. I have remarked in my book *Theodicy* <note>original: "Théodicée"; Leibniz’s 1710 work attempting to reconcile the existence of evil with a good God.</note> that had Descartes perceived that the laws of movement require the conservation not only of force but also of the whole direction of force, he would have been led to attribute to the soul the power to change the direction of a force without the power to exercise it, and he would have been led straightway to my system of <term>Pre-established Harmony</term> <note>Leibniz’s theory that the soul and body do not interact physically; instead, God synchronized them at the beginning of time like two perfect clocks.</note>, for that is the necessary consequence of the conservation of both force and direction.

<summary>Leibniz explains that while the universe operates through physical laws (mechanism), those laws are actually rooted in spiritual principles (metaphysics). He argues that Descartes and Huygens failed to realize this because they either lacked the correct physics or ignored the underlying philosophy.</summary>

## CONCERNING THE MONADS

*This elucidation appears to have been written by Leibniz to be enclosed in the foregoing letter to Nicolas Remond <note>Nicolas Remond (d. 1725) was a high-ranking French official and a long-time correspondent of Leibniz who sought to understand his philosophy.</note>, containing the account of the origin of the Theory of the Monads, but withheld. In this letter, dated Vienna July 1714, he says he had hoped to enclose an Elucidation concerning the Monads <note>original: "Éclaircissement sur les Monades"</note> but the matter had grown in his hands and so many distractions had interfered with its completion that he must hold it over. Writing again from Vienna a month later (August 26th 1714) he refers to the former letter and says that he is now sending a short discourse which he had prepared for Prince Eugene <note>Prince Eugene of Savoy (1663–1736) was a celebrated military commander and a patron of the arts and sciences; this "short discourse" became the famous *Monadology*.</note>. It seems therefore that a copy of the Monadology was sent and this Elucidation laid aside.*

I have heard from Monsieur Hugony <note>A friend and intermediary who moved in the same social circles as Remond and Leibniz.</note> that you are finding some difficulty in regard to my Unities or Monads <note>From the Greek *monas*, meaning "unity" or "one." For Leibniz, these are the fundamental, indivisible points of existence that make up the universe.</note>. I would like to know what your difficulty is but meanwhile I will try to explain myself. I believe that the whole universe of creatures consists of simple substances or Monads and their assemblages alone. These simple substan-

<summary>In this preface and opening fragment, Leibniz introduces a clarification of his "Monad" theory intended for Nicolas Remond, explaining that the entire universe is composed of these simple, indivisible substances.</summary>

In Leibniz's time, this referred to rational beings or intelligences, ranging from humans to angels.</note> we call mind, and in the animals, soul. All have *perception* (which is nothing more than the representation of the multitude in the unity) and *appetite* (which is nothing more than the tendency of one perception towards another) and this appetite is called *passion* in animals and *will* wherever perception is understanding. We cannot even conceive anything else in the simple substances and consequently in the whole of nature. The assemblages are what we call bodies. What appears passive and uniform and resisting in these bodies, in the mass, we call matter; but the primitive active force is what we call <term>Entelechy</term> <note>From the Greek *entelecheia*, meaning to have a goal or completion within itself. This is a key Leibnizian concept: while physical objects aren't "real" in the way indivisible Monads are, they are not mere illusions because they are consistently grounded in the reality of those Monads.</note>, the ground of appearances, different for different observers. These appearances are related, inasmuch as they have the same ground, like the different appearances of one and the same city viewed from different standpoints. Space, far from being a substance, is not even an entity. It is an order, like time, an order of coexistences just as time is an order between existences which are not compresent <note>Existing at the same time.</note>. [situa]tions into which it can be divided and which can be arbitrarily assigned to it, the parts being only potential; but in real continuity the simple is anterior to the assemblages <note>In Leibniz’s philosophy, "assemblages" are complex things made of simpler parts. He argues that the simple units (Monads) must exist before the whole can exist.</note>, the parts are actual, they are before the whole. These considerations raise difficulties in regard to continuity, when it is assumed that the continuous is real, and has parts previous to any division, and that matter is a substance. Extension <note>The physical quality of taking up space.</note> is not to be conceived as a real continuous space with points scattered over it. Such conceptions are fictions, which may satisfy the imagination, but of which reason cannot take account.

All the more must we not conceive the monads as points in a real space, jostling, pushing or touching one another. Phenomena do indeed make them so appear and this appearance has truth so far as phenomena have a ground, that is, insofar as they admit this appearance. Movements and concurrences are only appearance, but an appearance well founded <note>original: "bien fondé." This refers to his theory that while our senses deceive us about the nature of matter, our perceptions are not random; they are consistent because they are based on the underlying reality of the Monads.</note> which never belies itself, like a dream which is precise and persistent.

Movement is the phenomenon of change according to place and time, the body is the phenomenon which changes. The laws of movement, being founded in the perception of the simple substances, come from final causes <note>A philosophical term for the purpose or "goal" of an action, rather than just the physical mechanics.</note> or from convention and these are immaterial and private to each monad. If matter were substance these laws would be derived from natural reason or from geometrical necessity <note>Leibniz argues that physics follows laws chosen for their "fitness" or "goodness" by God, rather than being strictly required by the rules of geometry alone.</note>

<summary>Leibniz argues that space and movement are not fundamental realities, but "well-founded phenomena" resulting from the internal states and purposes of Monads.</summary>

and then would be quite different from what they are. There is no action of substances other than <term>perceptions</term> <note>The internal state of a "monad" (simple substance) that reflects the world around it.</note> and <term>appetitions</term> <note>The internal drive or tendency that moves a substance from one perception to the next; essentially, the "will" of the monad.</note>, all other actions are <term>phenomena</term> as are all other actings. Plato seems to have held a similar view, for he considered that material things had little reality, and the **Academicians** <note>Followers of the school of philosophy founded by Plato; in later years, they were known for doubting that we can truly know the external world through our senses.</note> challenged the whole idea of external reality. Material things can be rationally explained consistently with the view that there is nothing outside our perceptions and that they therefore have their reality in the agreement of the perceptions of the apperceiving substances.

This agreement is due to the <term>pre-established harmony</term> <note>Leibniz's famous theory that God synchronized all substances in the universe at the moment of creation, so they appear to interact with one another like two perfectly timed clocks, even though they do not actually influence each other physically.</note> in these substances, for each <term>simple substance</term> <note>An indivisible, non-physical unit of reality, which Leibniz calls a "monad."</note> is a **mirror of the same universe**, as durable and as ample as it, although perceptions of creatures can only be distinct in regard to a few at a time. Also they are diversified by their relations or rather we may say by the different viewpoints of the mirrors. This brings it about that one and the same universe is multiplied in infinite ways, each living mirror representing the universe in its own way. 

We may say, then, that each simple substance is an image of the universe, and over and above this that each **mind** is an **image of God**. For minds are not like souls, without reason, **empirics** <note>Those who rely strictly on sensory experience and memory of past events without understanding the underlying laws of logic or reason.</note>, having knowledge only of facts and relations; minds have knowledge of the necessity of the **eternal truths**, are able to understand the reason of facts,

<summary>Leibniz argues that the physical world is a phenomenon arising from the internal perceptions of "monads," which are synchronized by a pre-established harmony. He distinguishes between simple souls, which are purely experiential, and minds, which can grasp eternal truths and reflect the nature of God.</summary>

## ELUCIDATIONS
## OF
## THE REFORM OF THE ONTOLOGICAL ARGUMENT;

*In the years 1702-1706 Leibniz was corresponding with Isaac Jacquelot <note>Isaac Jacquelot (1647–1708) was a prominent French Protestant theologian and philosopher who had taken refuge in Berlin. He was a frequent correspondent of both Leibniz and the skeptic Pierre Bayle.</note>, Pastor of the Reformed Church of the French Colony in Berlin. These elucidations are direct replies to questions submitted by Jacquelot. It will be seen by the date that they antecede the Monadology, 1714, and also The Theodicy, 1710 <note>This chronological note is important because it shows Leibniz’s ideas in development before they were synthesized into his most famous final works.</note>.*

### I

### REFORM OF THE ONTOLOGICAL ARGUMENT

<term>Ontological Argument</term> <note>A philosophical argument that tries to prove God exists by examining the very definition or "idea" of God, rather than by looking at the physical evidence of the world.</note> Descartes's Argument for the Existence of God assumes that the all-perfect being, or the necessary being, is possible <note>Leibniz’s main "reform" or critique was that Descartes simply assumed such a being could exist. Leibniz argued one must first prove that the concept of an "all-perfect being" is not self-contradictory.</note>. The argument comes to this : the all-perfect being includes in its idea among other perfections that of existing necessarily ; or, the all-perfect being is a **necessary being** <term>necessary being</term> <note>In metaphysics, a being whose nature is such that it must exist; it is impossible for it not to exist.</note>. Now the necessary being exists ; therefore, the all-perfect being exists. It is as though one

<summary>Leibniz introduces a series of clarifications sent to Isaac Jacquelot, beginning with a critique and "reform" of Descartes’s Ontological Argument for the existence of God.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, Isaac Jacquelot, Ontological Argument, René Descartes, necessary being, existence of God, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 168 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Elucidations" addressed to Isaac Jacquelot, specifically focusing on the first section regarding the Reform of the Ontological Argument. Leibniz is mid-argument, demonstrating that one cannot simply define something into existence without first proving its logical possibility.</meta>

-> THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ <-
-> 154 <-

...should reason thus: The necessary body is a necessary being; necessary being exists; therefore the necessary body exists. To this my only reply would be that this argument assumes that the necessary body is possible, just as the other assumes that the being which includes all perfections in its essence and especially existence, is possible. It would be exactly the same if for necessary body we substituted all-perfect body.

We can see at once that a conclusion so important as that of the existence of God cannot be so hastily reached and that something must be added. This confirms me in what I have said elsewhere concerning the abuse of the doctrine of ideas. (Definitions are not arbitrary as Hobbes <note>Thomas Hobbes (1588–1679) was an English philosopher who famously argued that truth is a result of how we name things, suggesting that definitions are merely human conventions or "arbitrary" labels.</note> maintained and we cannot form ideas at will, though the Cartesians <note>Followers of René Descartes. Leibniz often critiqued them for relying on "clear and distinct" intuitions of God without first providing a formal logical proof that the concept of an all-perfect being does not contain hidden contradictions.</note> seem to hold that we can. The ideas we claim to form must be real, that is to say they must be possible, and the ingredients we put into our ideas must be mutually compatible). It is only by the abuse of ideas that we can deduce, for example, from the definition or idea of infinite <note>original: "extrême"</note> velocity that the part is as great as the whole. The only way of rejecting this conclusion is to deny that infinite velocity is possible, or that it is a real idea. All our reasonings imply the reality of the ideas or, what is the same thing, their possibility. Geometricians <note>In the 17th century, "geometricians" referred generally to mathematicians who used rigorous deductive logic to prove their points, much like Leibniz tries to do in philosophy.</note> have always understood this, for they have made it a...

<summary>Leibniz critiques the Ontological Argument by insisting that existence cannot be deduced from a definition unless the concept—such as an "all-perfect being"—is first proven to be logically possible. He warns against "arbitrary" definitions, using mathematical paradoxes to show that inconsistent ideas cannot serve as the basis for truth.</summary>

<keywords>Ontological Argument, existence of God, logical possibility, Thomas Hobbes, Cartesians, infinite velocity, geometric proof, doctrine of ideas</keywords>

# REFORM OF THE ONTOLOGICAL ARGUMENT

155

postulate. Thus Euclid <note>Euclid (c. 300 BC) was a Greek mathematician. Leibniz cites him because Euclid’s geometry begins with "postulates"—basic assumptions that certain constructions, like drawing a line, are possible.</note> postulates at the beginning that a straight line is possible: *that a straight line may be drawn from one point to another.* <note>original: "a puncto ad punctum duci posse rectam"</note>

Yet Descartes’s <note>René Descartes (1596–1650) argued that the idea of a supremely perfect being is innate, and because existence is a "perfection," such a being must necessarily exist.</note> argument, and St. Anselm’s <note>St. Anselm of Canterbury (1033–1109) was the first to formulate the "Ontological Argument," which claims that God, defined as "that than which nothing greater can be conceived," must exist in reality as well as in the mind.</note> before him, is not the sophism <note>A "sophism" is a clever but false argument or a fallacy.</note> which so many think it. In my view it is a good argument though imperfect and what it lacks can be supplied. But even as it stands, imperfect and with nothing added, it has considerable value insofar as it proves that the divine nature enjoys at least this privilege that it needs only possibility or essence in order to exist. What is more, the argument gives at least presumptively the existence of God, for all existence must be thought possible in the absence of proof to the contrary, until, that is to say, we are made to see that it is impossible. This presumption, as we may call it, is incomparably higher than a simple supposition, since most suppositions must be proved before they can be admitted, while whatever is presumed must pass as true until it is refuted. The existence of God therefore has the presumption in its favour, since it only need be possible. Now possibility is always presumed and must be held true until impossibility is proved. This argument accordingly has the force that it lays the **burden of proof** <note>original: "onus probandi"</note> on him who denies it, and since no one can prove its impossibility we must hold God’s existence to be real. Should we wish, however,

<summary>Leibniz defends the Ontological Argument of Anselm and Descartes, arguing that it holds "presumptive" weight: since the existence of God only needs to be possible to be true, the burden of proof lies with the skeptic to prove that God is impossible.</summary>

I have tried to contribute such a proof by making it evident that if the necessary being <note>In philosophy, a "necessary being" is one that must exist and cannot *not* exist; for Leibniz, this is God.</note> were impossible, all the contingent beings <note>"Contingent beings" are things that happen to exist but could logically not have existed (like humans, trees, or planets).</note> would be impossible also, and so nothing would be possible. For since contingent beings have not in themselves the sufficient reason <note>The "Principle of Sufficient Reason" is a core tenet of Leibniz’s philosophy, stating that nothing happens without a reason why it is so and not otherwise.</note> of their existence, they must have recourse to the necessary being, which is the **final ground of things** <note>original: "ultima ratio rerum"</note>.

## II
### THE DEFINITION OF MATTER

1. Matter (by which I mean not *primary matter* <note>Leibniz distinguishes "primary matter" (the passive power of resistance in a substance) from "secondary matter" (the physical mass we see).</note> but *secondary matter* or mass) is not a substance but substances, such as a flock of sheep and a pond full of fish. Corporeal substances, in my view, are only nature’s machines with souls or something analogous, without which they would have no real unity.

2. Every created substance acts and suffers. There is nothing contradictory in this. <note>Isaac Jacquelot (1647–1708), a French theologian and philosopher, had argued that it is a direct contradiction for a substance to both act and be acted upon.</note> I hold that nothing whatever exists apart from matter. The ancient philosophers gave bodies to genii <note>In this context, "genii" refers to spirits or minor deities in classical mythology.</note> and the Fathers <note>The "Church Fathers" were influential Christian theologians and teachers in the early centuries of the Church.</note> give bodies to angels. God alone, who is **pure act** <note>original: "pure act" (Latin: *actus purus*), a theological term meaning a being with no unfulfilled potential or passive parts.</note>, is exempt from matter.

<summary>Leibniz concludes his refinement of the Ontological Argument by linking the existence of contingent things to a necessary "final ground," and defines matter as an aggregate of substances rather than a single substance.</summary>
<keywords>Ontological Argument, Necessary Being, Sufficient Reason, Primary Matter, Secondary Matter, Corporeal Substance, Jacquelot, Pure Act</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion on the nature of matter and substance from page 156. While the previous page established that matter (as mass) is a collection of substances rather than a single substance, this page delves into the internal forces—the "entelechy"—that give these substances their reality and distinguishes between force and movement.</meta>

->### THE DEFINITION OF MATTER<-

157

3 There are two kinds of force in the body, a primitive force which is essential to it (<term>first entelechy<note>original Greek: "ἐντελέχεια ἡ πρώτη". A term Leibniz borrowed from Aristotle to describe the internal "soul-like" principle of activity that makes a substance a complete, living entity.</note></term>) and derived forces which depend on other bodies. The derivative or accidental force, which we have to attribute to bodies in movement, is a modification of the primitive force, just as the shape of the body is a modification of <term>extension<note>In 17th-century philosophy, "extension" refers to the property of taking up space in three dimensions (length, breadth, and depth).</note></term>. Accidental forces could not exist in a substance without essential force, for accidents <note>In philosophy, an "accident" is a property that a thing has but could lose without ceasing to be what it is (like the color of a car or the speed of a ball).</note> are only modifications or limitations and cannot include more perfection or reality than the substance.

4. Movement is not the cause but the effect or the consequence of force. Movement, moreover, is not a being <note>Leibniz argues that movement is not a "thing" in itself because it never exists all at once; it is merely a sequence of states over time.</note> any more than is time, it has no coexistent parts and consequently does not exist. Force, on the other hand, is subsistent and can endure. Moreover it is the same quantity of the force from which movement is derived, and not the same quantity of movement, which is conserved in nature. Force, being instantaneously available, makes the momentary difference between a body in movement and a body at rest or in least movement.

5. You claim to prove that movement is not essential to matter and you assume that you are in agreement with me when you say that a body... <note>This sentence appears to be part of a dialogue or a response to a specific critic, possibly arguing against the Cartesian view that the essence of matter is simply extension.</note>

<summary>This page defines the distinction between "primitive force" (the internal essence of a substance) and "derived force" (the movement resulting from physical interaction). Leibniz argues that force is a real, enduring entity, whereas movement is merely a transitory effect that does not possess true existence.</summary>
<keywords>primitive force, derived force, entelechy, extension, accidental force, conservation of force, metaphysics of movement, Leibnizian substance</keywords>

...may be absolutely at rest. <note>Leibniz is concluding a point from the previous page: while we can imagine an object standing still, he argues that in the physical universe, everything is in constant, subtle motion.</note> [Jacquelot <note>Isaac Jacquelot (1647–1708) was a French Protestant theologian and philosopher who corresponded with Leibniz about the nature of the soul and matter.</note> had written: If movement were part of the essence <note>In philosophy, "essence" refers to the core nature of a thing—the qualities that make it what it is.</note> of a given body A, it would be impossible for me to think of A at rest. Now I can conceive of A at rest, and so clearly that when I suppose A to be the point at which body B begins to move, I necessarily suppose A to be at rest in order to calculate B’s movement. This is so true that even if A is actually in motion, I must pay no attention to its movement—I must even suppress the idea of it—and this I could not do if movement were part of its essence.] But I hold that a body without action and movement is as unacceptable in nature as a space without matter. Every body is affected by surrounding bodies, and this could not happen without receiving movement. I agree, however, that a body by itself could not act, for it could not come to any determination; but a body could not exist alone. I agree also that in pure matter without primitive <term>entelechy<note>A term Leibniz adopted from Aristotle to describe a "vital spark" or internal power that drives a substance to act. Without this, he argues, matter would be dead and motionless.</note></term> there could be no action.

6. You ask me whether the degree of movement a body has is essential to it. I reply that the essential force of the body goes to the indefinite <note>original: "à l'indéfini". This suggests a force that is not limited by its own nature, but only by its environment.</note>, but it is limited by the collision of other bodies, which produces a definite movement.

<summary>Leibniz argues against Isaac Jacquelot, asserting that matter is never truly at rest and that all bodies possess an internal "entelechy" or active force. He maintains that while we can imagine an object at rest for the sake of physics, in reality, all substance is inherently active.</summary>

# THE DEFINITION OF MATTER

<page-num>159.</page-num>

7. When mathematicians assume a state of rest, they do so in the same way they assume the existence of perfect circles—which, in reality, are never found. Rest is an ideal concept that, like the circle, serves as a tool for reasoning. In practical terms, we say a body is at rest when no movement is perceived. Just as rest can be understood relatively when a movement is very small compared to others, so too bodies that keep the same distance between one another are "relatively" at rest, even if they are moving together. Likewise, a whole object might appear to stay in its place while there are internal movements among its parts. It is unnecessary to refer to the many other ways we use the idea of rest.

8. I believe I am in fundamental agreement with you when you say that it is the mind which acts and matter which <note>In this context, "suffers" (from the Latin *pati*) does not mean feeling pain, but rather "undergoing" or being the recipient of an action—the opposite of acting.</note> suffers. In every corporeal <note>Physical or bodily.</note> substance, I conceive of two primitive powers: first, <term>entelechy<note>Derived from the Greek *entelecheia*, meaning "having a goal within." Leibniz uses it to describe the internal "battery" or soul-like force that gives an object the power to act.</note></term> (or the primitive active power), which in animals is the soul and in man is the mind, and which the ancients generally called <term>substantial form<note>A concept from medieval philosophy (Scholasticism) referring to the essential nature that makes a thing what it is and gives it its specific qualities.</note></term>; and second, <term>primary matter<note>For Leibniz, this is not "stuff" we can touch, but the inherent quality of "passive power" or resistance that keeps objects from moving infinitely fast and makes them distinct from one another.</note></term> (or the primitive passive power), which provides resistance. Therefore, strictly speaking, it is the entelechy which acts and matter which suffers; yet, neither one can form a complete substance without the other.

9. I also agree that, in a certain sense, the mind draws everything from its own source, or rather...

### III

## THE PRE-ESTABLISHED HARMONY
<note>This is Leibniz’s famous solution to the "mind-body problem." If the mind is spiritual and the body is physical, how do they talk to each other? Leibniz argues they don't; instead, God synchronized them at the beginning of time like two perfectly timed clocks.</note>

1. I hold that the soul has received from God the power of producing its own thoughts. The <term>Cartesians<note>Followers of René Descartes, the philosopher famous for the phrase "I think, therefore I am."</note></term> themselves, I think, admit this in regard to some thoughts. I think so in regard to all. And though indeed in my system the nature of each substance consists in <term>active force<note>Leibniz’s term for the internal energy or "drive" that makes a substance change and develop from within.</note></term>, that is in what makes it produce changes according to the laws of its being, yet it is true that God concurs in everything by the <term>continual conservation<note>A theological concept: the idea that the universe doesn't just keep running on its own, but requires God to actively "sustain" or "re-create" it at every single moment.</note></term> of beings. (And it is because God is the common cause that all substances harmonize. My system is a new proof of God’s existence, a proof which at the same time demonstrates the wisdom of God beyond any concept of it expressed hitherto. <note>Pierre</note> **Bayle** has recognized in his *Dictionary* <note>The *Dictionnaire Historique et Critique*, a massive and influential encyclopedia of the 17th century that often critiqued religious and philosophical theories.</note> that my system easily gives a higher idea of this attribute <note>The attribute of God's wisdom.</note>.)

2. The soul produces its thoughts in the presence of corporeal <note>Physical or bodily.</note> ideas and not by their aid or influence. I should be glad to find the system of <term>occasional causes<note>A rival theory (popularized by Nicolas Malebranche) which argued that when you want to move your arm, your mind doesn't cause the movement; rather, your "will" is just the *occasion* for God to step in and move the arm for you.</note></term> explained in a way which would make it agree with mine.

<summary>Leibniz introduces the "Pre-established Harmony," arguing that the soul generates all its own thoughts internally rather than being "pushed" by the physical body, though both remain synchronized by God.</summary>
<keywords>Pre-established Harmony, Cartesians, active force, Pierre Bayle, occasional causes, divine conservation, mind-body problem</keywords>

3. The soul is prompted to form new thoughts by its own internal object—that is, by the thoughts that came before it. Just as one moment in time follows another in a continuous chain, there is a natural succession or connection between our thoughts. The marvelous thing—the "miracle" of existence—is that every individual <term>substance<note>In Leibniz’s philosophy, a "substance" (or Monad) is a basic building block of reality, like a soul, that cannot be broken down further.</note></term> is a representation of the entire universe from its own unique point of view. This design grants to created beings and the work of their Creator the greatest possible richness and the highest possible perfection. In these countless "mirrors" of substance, the world is duplicated over and over, and through this, the universe is varied to infinity. All simple substances are, in a sense, **small divinities** <note>original: "petites divinités." Leibniz means that every soul has a spark of the divine because it can perceive and understand the order of the cosmos.</note> in relation to one another; they have existed from the very beginning, and as for their end, they have none.

4. The reason children do not think the same thoughts as adults is because their thoughts develop in parallel with the physical changes <note>original: "external phenomena"</note> occurring in their bodies. This is a direct consequence of the <term>harmony<note>The "Pre-established Harmony": the idea that the mind and body are perfectly synced by God, so that what happens in the soul always matches what happens in the body, even though they don't actually "touch" each other.</note></term>. Souls, however, are never truly inactive; they are never without thoughts, no matter how confused or obscure those thoughts might be when the physical senses do not provide anything clearly distinct or prominent. <note>Leibniz argues here against the idea of a "blank slate" or dreamless sleep; he believes the mind is always "on," even if we aren't consciously aware of it.</note>

5. This natural harmony between the thoughts in the mind and the physical processes in the body's organs is also the cause of the apparent derangement of

A <term>Hottentot<note>A historical term for the Khoikhoi people of Southern Africa. 7. When sounds are not clearly distinguished the reason is that other objects which are more distinct occupy our attention and block them. Whatever is morbid in us and marks some imperfection—as, for example, the slowness of our progress in knowledge—has its reason in the organs. Insofar as we are passive and imperfect, God, in regulating the universal harmony, has fitted us to other things; and insofar as we are active and have some perfection, God has fitted other things to us. We are not to suppose, however, that the small unnoticed things <note>Leibniz refers here to his theory of "tiny perceptions" (petites perceptions)—sensations that are too small or frequent for us to consciously notice, like the sound of a single drop of water in the ocean, yet which still affect the soul.</note> which strike us are bodies lost. Nothing is lost and everything which happens to us enters into what will happen.

### IV

## THE NATURE OF FREE-WILL

My hypothesis is not inconsistent with freedom. On the contrary, more than any other system, it affirms true freedom—that is to say, <term>spontaneity<note>In this context, spontaneity refers to the soul's ability to produce its own states and actions from within itself, rather than being driven by external physical forces.</note></term> with choice and the soul's independence of everything save God alone. There is in things a greater inclination towards what is going to happen than to any other event, but this imposes no necessity. For although this prevailing inclination serves to predetermine the future—and in fact everything is in this meaning predetermined—yet is it always subject to the <term>contingency<note>Contingency refers to things that happen but could have been otherwise. Leibniz distinguishes between "necessity" (like 2+2=4, which must be true) and "contingent" truths (like a person choosing to go for a walk), which are certain but not logically mandatory.</note></term> in things and to the freedom in minds. The <term>pre-established harmony<note>Leibniz's theory that God perfectly synchronized the "clocks" of the soul and the body at creation so they appear to interact, though they actually follow their own internal laws.</note></term> requires no more. To come now to your actual objections:—

1. If the ideas which the soul must have in the course of time are all enveloped within it at the first moment of its creation, what freedom can there be in a thing done in the doing of which the soul had no concurrence in the ordinary meaning? It behaved as a mere subject and purely passively. <note>original: "Habuit se ut merum subjectum et mere passivè." The objector is suggesting that if the soul's future is already "pre-programmed" inside it, the soul is just a passive stage where events happen, rather than an active participant.</note> I reply: Firstly, that although the

future is enveloped in the soul, the soul is none the less free, because the future is in God’s understanding and God’s understanding does not deprive the soul of freedom. Secondly, that future things are enveloped in the soul but not in the full meaning in which they are in God, because they are in the soul only in a confused fashion which produces <term>inclination<note>In Leibniz's philosophy, "inclination" is a leaning or tendency toward a choice that makes the choice certain to happen, but not logically necessary—leaving the soul free to have chosen otherwise.</note></term> and not in the express and perfect fashion of the divine ideas. And thirdly, that though doubtless the soul has no <term>concurrence<note>In this context, concurrence refers to the way the soul works alongside God to bring about its own actions. While God creates the soul, the soul is the "active" cause of its own behaviors.</note></term> in its original constitution yet it will concur in the actions which will arise in the course of time. *It acted as a mere subject* <note>original Latin: "Habuit se merum ut subjectum"</note> when receiving its being, *but it will not act as a mere subject; rather, it will actively participate* <note>original Latin: "sed non habebit se mere ut subjectum, imo concurret active"</note>, it will not be merely subject but will actively concur from within in its actions when they happen.

2. I have never alleged the **absolute decrees** <note>Absolute decrees refer to the theological idea that God decides what will happen based purely on His will, without needing a reason. Leibniz argues against this, believing God always has a reason for His choices.</note> of the theologians, for I hold that there are no divine decrees which have not their grounds of choice although those grounds may be unknown to us. The majority of modern theologians may found the decrees on God’s foreknowledge, but since the objects of that foreknowledge still depend on him, he must in the end have recourse to the **choice of the best**, making this choice from among all the **possibles**. This is independent of his foreknowledge of what will actually happen.

3.  Even if I were to suppose that ideas or perceptions arise in the soul at the moment they are produced—as those who believe in <term>occasional causes<note>A philosophical theory, famously held by Nicolas Malebranche, suggesting that created things have no real power of their own and that God is the only true cause, acting on the "occasion" of a mental or physical event.</note></term> hold—I would not thereby destroy freedom. Why should this destroy freedom, seeing that what is voluntary and free in our actions always depends on choice, although the *grounds* for this choice may have been prepared from all time? For these *grounds* are <term>inclinations<note>Leibniz argues that internal motives "incline without necessitating," meaning they provide a reason for a choice without making it logically or mathematically impossible to choose otherwise.</note></term> and not <term>necessitations<note>A state where only one outcome is possible, such as 2+2 necessarily equaling 4, or a physical object falling due to gravity.</note></term>.

4.  My bodily movements, those of my arm for example, are in my view necessary without it being in my power to change them. Where then is the freedom? I reply: freedom is in me, not in the movements of my body. Yet however free I may be, God always knows what I shall choose and can make the body accord in advance with my <term>volition<note>The act of willing or choosing.</note></term>. Tell me then wherein the impediment lies, whatever be your idea of freedom. You can, if you will, put down your pen. Who doubts it? But God knows whether you will or not.

5.  You say you are determined by the <term>animal spirits<note>In early modern science, these were thought to be very subtle, vapor-like fluids that flowed through the nerves to trigger muscle movement. They were the "mechanical" explanation for how the body moved.</note></term> in a way which you cannot avoid, so that were you able to see them working, they would appear to you as the bullet from a cannon in whose path you might be. I reply: The **animal spirits** do not determine you; to see them do so would be to see the impossible. Your choice and judgment

6. You ask me: Was this idea which I am now writing to you, yesterday enveloped in my soul without my knowing it? If yes, then there can be no freedom in a thing done. I reply: It is not a thing done but a thing to do. The future exists in the past no otherwise than by an **inclination** which is in the past to the production of the future.

7. You ask me finally, whether those animal spirits which are today the means of your writing were yesterday determined to do what they are doing today. # I

## WHAT IS LIVING AND WHAT IS DEAD IN LEIBNIZ’S <term>MONADOLOGY<note>Leibniz’s "Monadology" (1714) is his most famous work, proposing that the universe is composed of "monads"—simple, indivisible, soul-like substances that do not interact physically but mirror the entire universe from their own perspective.</note></term>

### I. LEIBNIZ’S <term>METAPHYSICS<note>The branch of philosophy that examines the fundamental nature of reality, including the relationship between mind and matter, substance and attribute.</note></term> AND NEWTON’S PHYSICS

Throughout the whole course of modern philosophy we find two divergent directions in which the attempt is made to interpret our knowledge of the physical universe. Broadly speaking, we may designate the first as the way of <term>realism</term>, the second as the way of <term>idealism</term>. They lead to alternative and antithetical <note>Directly opposed or mutually exclusive.</note> conceptions of the nature of the existence of the physical world.

According to the way of <term>realism<note>In this context, the belief that the physical world exists independently of any mind perceiving it.</note></term>, there is assumed or postulated an existential distinction between mind and world. Knowledge is the relation between them, and in this relation the only thing distinctively mental is the act of knowing, the relation being between act of knowing and thing known. The activity in the act of knowing is affirmed to be purely an activity of direction. The attention is directed on an object and controlled, but the mind is purely passive in receiving revelation.

The correlation of act and thing is affirmed to be complete. To the knowing act the independently existing reality is revealed. Realists insist on the simplicity and ultimate character of the knowing relation. It is sometimes

<summary>This page introduces a comparison between Leibniz's metaphysics and Newtonian physics, framing the history of modern philosophy as a conflict between realism and idealism. It specifically defines the realist perspective, where the mind acts as a passive observer of an independent reality.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, Monadology, metaphysics, Isaac Newton, physics, realism, idealism, theory of knowledge, mind-world distinction</keywords>

--- Page 186 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of the "realist" approach to the physical world—finishing the sentence from the previous page—and introduces the "idealist" alternative, which aligns more closely with Leibniz’s own metaphysical framework.</meta>

->THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ<-

represented as the simple relation of togetherness.
The fatal defect of this attractive theory, and the method of direct investigation to which it invites the scientific researcher, is that it involves an initial assumption. It cannot start from existence—its own or another’s; it cannot validate the truth of the relation (knowledge) which it affirms.

The second way of approaching the problem of the physical world is the way of <term>idealism<note>In philosophy, idealism is the view that reality is fundamentally mental or spiritual rather than material. In Leibniz’s case, it suggests that the physical world is a "well-founded phenomenon" resulting from the perceptions of internal substances.</note></term>, using that term in its widest comprehension. The physical world is not assumed to be self-existent and self-revealing. The idealist starts with the active subject of experience in its immediate self-affirmation. To every <term>apperceptive<note>From "apperception," a term used by Leibniz to distinguish between simple "perception" (the internal state of a monad representing external things) and "apperception" (consciousness, or the reflective knowledge of that internal state).</note></term> or self-conscious subject existence is given immediately in the exercise of its activity. Existence is not identical with consciousness, but it is identical with conscious activity. It is the <term>I think, therefore I am<note>original: "cogito ergo sum"</note></term>. Every such active subject already possesses its external world in its own perceptions of its range of activity. This external world of its own perceptions becomes for each subject of experience a common world of general perception whenever there is intercourse between one subject and another, and the concept of a common world is the natural and necessary consequence of such intercourse. The idealist theory, therefore, is that the idea of a physical world independent

<summary>This page contrasts the "realist" view of a self-existent physical world with the "idealist" view, which begins with the conscious activity of the subject. It defines existence as conscious activity, famously captured by the phrase "I think, therefore I am."</summary>

# METAPHYSICS AND PHYSICS
### 173

of each subject of experience and common to all is a function of <term>intercourse<note>In this historical context, "intercourse" refers to social, intellectual, and verbal communication between people, rather than physical contact.</note></term> and originates in the intercommunication of minds, and not *vice versa*.

I will now state the <term>monadological theory<note>A system of philosophy (associated with Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz) which suggests that the universe is made of "monads"—indivisible, mental units that each mirror the universe from a specific point of view.</note></term> in the form in which I am myself prepared to defend it. If the <term>reals<note>The fundamental, truly existing entities or substances of the universe.</note></term> of the universe are monads, then there is no imaginable way in which two monads can perceive the same perceptions. By this I mean not only that the perceptions of an identical object are different for every individual observer of that object by reason of perspective, but that there is no imaginable way in which the different perspectives of individual observers can be identified by reference to an identical object.

Every one of us may easily satisfy himself on this point by simple reflexion on experience. We only perceive our own perceptions and yet in our intercourse with one another we always refer our perceptions to a common world. This common world is not an independent universe acting on its own account. On the other hand, it is not illusion. The common world which is the subject matter of intercourse is an **ideal construction**, and it arises in a **metaphysical necessity**. We can test that it is so. The same argument applies to it as that which is accepted by everyone in regard to the real world of physics. Physics does not deal with sense qualities, but with mathematical coordinations. The object of

<summary>The author explains that our shared "common world" is not a physical reality existing outside of us, but a mental construction necessitated by the way individual "monads" communicate their private perceptions to one another. He argues that this shared world is built through mathematical coordination, much like the laws of physics, rather than through direct shared experience of the same physical objects.</summary>

The real world of physics is entirely ideal—not in the sense that it is a random or imaginary invention, like the mythical and artistic creations of a poet, but rather that it is the necessary result of our need to bring order to our experiences. According to the <term>monadological theory<note>The philosophy that the universe is made of "monads," or individual points of consciousness that do not physically interact but are synchronized.</note></term>, the shared world of our conversation is created in exactly this same way.

I am not claiming that Leibniz explicitly stated this in his *Monadology*, but the principle is certainly present, and in my view, there is no other philosophical alternative. There is, however, a possible alternative <term>modus operandi<note>Latin: "way of operating." Here, it refers to a practical method for doing science.</note></term> that has always been very attractive to the general public. This *way of operating* is to follow Newton’s warning that physics should "beware of metaphysics." This approach assumes that the information from our basic common sense is true and sufficient, and it accepts everyday objects and their surroundings as the actual subject matter of physics. Great scientific progress has followed this path, achieving extraordinary success up to a certain point. It is only when we realize that trying to get rid of <term>metaphysics<note>The branch of philosophy that examines the fundamental nature of reality, including the relationship between mind and matter.</note></term> actually requires creating a new metaphysics—and when the principle <term>hypotheses non fingo<note>Latin: "I frame no hypotheses." A famous statement by Isaac Newton, meaning he refused to speculate on the hidden causes of physical laws like gravity, sticking only to what could be measured.</note></term> is revealed to be an unconscious hypothesis itself—that the trouble begins. Newton’s theory is just as much a "metaphysics" as Leibniz’s theory is.

He contrasts Leibniz’s philosophical approach with Newton’s attempt to avoid philosophy, concluding that even Newton's "common sense" science relies on hidden philosophical assumptions.</summary>

WHAT IS VITAL
175

### 2. WHAT IS VITAL IN THE MONADOLOGY

The fundamental principle of the <term>monadology<note>The philosophical system developed by Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716), which proposes that the universe is made of an infinite number of simple, indivisible substances.</note></term> is that the real is essentially active, that the active is the living, and therefore that only living activities are true units.

The monadology sets forth a logically consistent theory of the composition or constitution of a universe in which every constituent is an active subject of experience living its own life and, in living its own life, subserving the universal life—the life of the universe.

The monadology rejects as self-contradiction and inconsistency the idea that dead, inert, material atoms or mass-units are or can be constituents of a universe. There is nothing dead, nothing purely inert in the universe. To be absolutely dead is not to be. The <term>Democritean<note>Refers to Democritus, the ancient Greek "atomist" philosopher who believed the world was made of tiny, physical, indivisible particles moving through empty space.</note></term> concept of the atoms and the void is rejected because mathematically the atom is not a unit, but divisible to infinity, and physically it is not simple. The reals are <term>monads<note>From the Greek word 'monas,' meaning 'unit.' These are the "souls" or "atoms of nature" that make up reality; they are not physical, but centers of force and consciousness.</note></term>, simple substances, self-centred subjects of experience, whose activity consists in <term>perception<note>For Leibniz, 'perception' is the internal state of a monad as it represents or mirrors everything happening in the outside world.</note></term>, and whose perceptions are the apprehension of the whole universe, limited in the degree of clearness or obscurity, because the monads are created and therefore finite, and qualified by their individual standpoints.

The idea of such a universe of individual reals is illustrated by Leibniz in the relation of the

<summary>This page outlines the core of Leibniz's philosophy: that the universe is composed of living, active units called monads rather than dead, physical atoms. These monads are individual subjects of experience that reflect the entire universe from their own unique points of view.</summary>
<keywords>monadology, Leibniz, activity, living units, Democritus, atoms, perception, substance, metaphysics</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues the exploration of Leibniz’s Monadology, moving from the definition of the monad as a living unit to the implications for physics and the external universe. It concludes by identifying the flaws Leibniz encountered in deducing universal harmony from his starting principles.</meta>

->THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ<-

mind to the body. Had he known the <term>cell theory<note>The biological principle that all living organisms are composed of cells, which are the basic units of life. This was developed in the mid-19th century, long after Leibniz's time.</note></term> in its modern development, his illustration would have fitted the theme even more exactly. The mind in Leibniz’s view is a <term>simple substance<note>In Leibniz's philosophy, this is a basic building block that cannot be broken down further because it has no physical parts.</note></term>, an indivisible unity which enters into compounds. The body is composite. Yet every part of the body lives its own individual life, and the whole mind-body organization is a <term>harmony<note>Leibniz’s "pre-established harmony" suggests that the mind and body do not physically influence each other but are perfectly synchronized like two identical clocks.</note></term> of individual living active subjects.

The profound significance of this theory when we apply it to physics is that the external universe of physical reality can only be represented as a <term>phenomenon of intercourse<note>The idea that what we perceive as "matter" or "objects" is actually the result of how these individual living units (monads) relate to and reflect one another.</note></term>. There can be no interaction of the monads in the mode in which the atoms of the <term>materialist philosophy<note>A school of thought claiming that everything in the universe is made of physical matter and that all phenomena, including consciousness, are results of material interactions.</note></term> had been conceived to interact, because in its very conception the monad is self-centred, a subject not an object, viewing the universe only as it is mirrored to it in its perception. That is to say the activities of other monads are perceived by each monad, not as they are in themselves, but by each monad as the limitations of its own activity.

Where Leibniz fails entirely is in any attempt to deduce the harmony of his system from the living principle, which is illustrated so excellently in the concept of the monad. There are monads

# WHAT IS DEFECTIVE

—this to Leibniz is immediate self-evident fact—but there is no way of conceiving their genesis, no way of understanding how they could have come into existence, except by creation. The harmony therefore, he concluded, could only be ascribed to the wisdom of the creator. It is true, indeed, that this did not present itself to him as a difficulty. It caused him no embarrassment. On the contrary, he regarded the appeal to the omniscience of God as the special merit of his system and probably it was the origin and basis of his whole conception. He reverts to it on every occasion and always refers to it as “my new system of the <term>pre-established harmony<note>The theory that God programmed every substance in the universe from the beginning so that they all stay in perfect synchronization without actually influencing one another.</note>” .

From the modern standpoint, and generally for us, this doctrine is not only entirely unconvincing, but it stamps the whole conception with the mark of artificiality. Even Leibniz himself had difficulty, for he hesitates between two quite irreconcilable concepts of God. At one time he conceives God as <term>first cause<note>The philosophical argument that there must be an initial, uncaused being that started the chain of cause-and-effect in the universe.</note></term> and omniscient artificer, exercising his infinite wisdom in choosing the best possible universe from among all the possibilities present to his mind before creation, following out in anticipatory thought the consequences of the <term>monadic activities<note>The internal changes and perceptions occurring within each "monad" (individual unit of reality).</note></term> to their infinitesimal limits ; at another time he conceives God as the **Monad of monads** <note>original: "Monas monadum"</note>, ruler and upholder of a world of free spirits.

### 4. THE SCIENTIFIC SIGNIFICANCE OF THE CONCEPTION OF THE MONADS

The atom of modern physics is neither the material "space-occupier" of old philosophy, nor the indivisible, unit-without-size, or simple substance described by Leibniz; rather, it is an intellectual and purely mathematical construction. It cannot be shown to our physical senses, even though we can only imagine it using sensory shapes. This is because, by its very definition, the atom is the *condition* for our sensory experiences to exist, rather than something created *by* those experiences. It is mathematically calculated and philosophically constructed to explain—like a working model—the chain of cause and effect seen in <term>spectroscopically revealed phenomena<note>Patterns of light that appear when scientists split light into its component colors to analyze the chemical makeup of an object.</note>. Moreover, the atoms of modern physics are not seen as the absolute final pieces or building blocks of the physical world. They are not "simple," but "compound"—meaning they are made of multiple parts. They are systems of opposite forces held in a more or less stable balance. To find the "ultimate" foundation of reality, physics goes behind and beyond the atom to the <term>field of force<note>A region in space where a force, such as gravity or electricity, exerts influence on objects.</note></term> where the atom is created, and to the <term>unit electric charges<note>The fundamental particles, like electrons or protons, that carry electricity and into which an atom can be broken down.</note></term> into which it is finally dissolved.

There are, however, two principles in modern science—principles that are not yet universally accepted, but which gain constant support from scientific research—which are in complete accord with...

The <term>principle of relativity<note>Referring to Albert Einstein’s theories of relativity, which revolutionized physics by showing that measurements of space and time depend on the observer's motion.</note></term> enables us to go behind this activity of perception, to rationalize it and see its ultimate ground. According to the principle of relativity, every subject of experience co-ordinates the universe—that is, the aggregate of events which constitutes nature—from an individual view-point. 

This view-point is that of an observer attached to a <term>system of reference<note>A "frame" or coordinate system (like a laboratory or a planet) used by an observer to measure the position and motion of objects.</note></term>. This system of reference is, for the observer attached to it, at rest absolutely in regard to all other systems moving uniformly or non-uniformly in relation to it. The <term>axes of co-ordination<note>The mathematical dimensions used to locate an event: three for physical space (height, width, depth) and one for time.</note></term>, three for space and one for time, which the observer in a system employs, are, therefore, always equal and invariable in whatever direction they are turned, although to observers in systems moving relatively to it they will vary to the exact extent necessary to compensate the relative movement. 

According to this principle therefore, the activity of the <term>monads<note>The fundamental, individual units of reality that make up the universe.</note></term> is not "perception," if by perception we imply something absolute in the reality perceived. The primordial activity of the monads is not perceiving, but co-ordinating the universe. This co-ordinating the universe is a fundamental necessity of living activity. Action of any kind is clearly impossible in an uncoordinated universe. The

### 5. IS THERE AN IMPASSE IN THE INITIAL CONCEPTION OF A MONADIC UNIVERSE?

Most philosophers who have given their attention to the theory of a monadic universe are deterred by a certain <term>impossibility<note>original: "non possumus" — a Latin phrase meaning "we cannot," used here to describe a logical dead-end or an insurmountable objection.</note></term> which seems to confront them in the initial presentation of the doctrine. On what conceivable principle, they ask, can “windowless”, non-interacting, <term>monads<note>The fundamental, indivisible building blocks of reality in Leibniz's thought. They are "windowless" because they are entirely self-contained and do not receive any external physical influence.</note></term> be compounded? Have you not, they say, in characterizing your unit as a <term>simple substance<note>An elementary entity that has no parts and therefore cannot be divided or destroyed by physical means.</note></term> negated the inter-relatedness of the monads? Is not the monad by its definition a <term>solipsistic activity<note>Refers to the idea that the monad functions as if it were the only thing in existence, with all its experiences arising from within itself rather than from external objects.</note></term>; how then, can it enter into and become a part of a community? If the argument I have advanced is accepted, the solution of the problem is anticipated. I admit the solipsistic character of the monad and I make no attempt to qualify it or to explain it away. What I insist on is that the interrelatedness of the monads is ideal; that

II

# THE IDEALISM OF THE MONADOLOGY

The Theory of <term>Monads<note>From the Greek word 'monas,' meaning 'unit.' In Leibniz's philosophy, these are the fundamental, indivisible building blocks of reality—spiritual rather than physical atoms.</note></term> rests on the clear acceptance of two facts. The first is that the knowledge possessed by any individual is <term>solipsistic<note>The philosophical idea that only one's own mind is sure to exist, making personal experience the only certain reality.</note></term>. The second is that the relationship between one mind and another is "ideal," meaning there is no physical contact between them. Leibniz expressed the first fact with his famous maxim: "The monads have no windows through which anything can enter or leave" (<note>original: "Les monades n'ont point de fenêtres, par lesquelles quelque chose y puisse entrer ou sortir."</note> *Monadology*, 7). He expressed the second by saying that monads exercise only an "ideal" influence on one another (*Monadology*, 51). It follows, therefore, that monads cannot interact or communicate directly. The central problem in this system is this: how can there be a shared exchange of ideas between monads if knowledge is entirely private and there is no physical interaction?

## 1. IS THE THEORY SELF-CONTRADICTORY?

The theory of monads, despite its appeal, is usually dismissed quickly because people believe its two fundamental points are a self-contradiction—and a two-fold one at that. First, if knowledge is entirely private to the individual, it seems impossible that

<page-num>185</page-num>

<keywords>Leibniz, Monadology, monads, solipsism, idealism, windowless monads, interaction, self-contradiction</keywords>

a monad can know there are other monads. Secondly, if interaction between monads is denied it appears a plain contradiction in terms to affirm that one monad can exercise an influence such as <term>intercourse<note>In this historical context, "intercourse" refers to social, intellectual, or physical communication and exchange between entities, rather than its modern sexual connotation.</note></term> presupposes. The logical dilemma in each argument appears complete and yet as a matter of fact both arguments are and have always proved to be strengthless.

### 2. THE SENSE IN WHICH THE UNKNOWABILITY OF A THING MAY BE AFFIRMED

The first argument is practically identical with the objection which is continually raised against Kant’s doctrine of the existence of an unknowable ***thing-in-itself*** <note>original: "Ding an sich." This is a fundamental concept in Immanuel Kant's philosophy, referring to reality as it exists independently of human observation and sensory perception.</note>. We cannot, it is said, affirm that a thing exists and also that it is unknowable, for if it is unknowable not even its existence is known; nay, further, it is only possible to affirm unknowability of anything if we are already in possession of some knowledge about it, and therefore to affirm unknowability is to deny what we are affirming. Obvious as this argument is generally held to be, it is not effective. We feel that the contradiction can only be verbal for Kant’s doctrine is intelligible, and it may be true. The answer to the objection is that self-knowledge, even in the extreme <term>solipsistic<note>The philosophical view that the self is the only thing that can be known to exist.</note></term> meaning, contains within itself the implicit distinction between knowing a thing as it exists for the knower and

<page-num>186</page-num>

<summary>This page refutes common logical objections to Leibniz’s theory by comparing them to Immanuel Kant’s concept of the "thing-in-itself," arguing that claiming something is "unknowable" is not a self-defeating statement. It suggests that the distinction between how a thing appears to us and how it exists independently is a necessary part of self-knowledge.</summary>
