# INTERACTION

...knowing a thing as it exists for itself. Even in ordinary common-sense experience, we make this distinction. We recognize that to know there are other minds is not to know those other minds as they are in themselves, and we have no difficulty in reconciling our knowledge of the existence of others with our conviction that their inner nature is completely unknowable to us. The argument is an empty <term>logomachy<note>A dispute about words or a purely verbal controversy, rather than a disagreement over actual facts or reality.</note></term>.

### 3. IS EVERY RELATION A FORM OF INTERACTION OR DEPENDENT UPON AN INTERACTION?

The second objection is equally ineffective. It seems <term>at first glance<note>original: "prima facie"</note></term> absurd to affirm that <term>monads<note>In the philosophy of Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, monads are the fundamental, indivisible building blocks of reality. They are "simple substances" that do not have physical parts and, according to Leibniz, do not physically influence one another.</note></term>—which by definition are "reals" (that is, things-in-themselves and not just ideas dependent on a mind)—do not interact and yet nevertheless can enter into the relations with one another which <term>intercourse<note>In this context, the term refers to the communication, connection, or social exchange between entities.</note></term> implies. The reason for this confusion simply is that we have come to regard all relatedness as being essentially a form of physical interaction. 

Interaction, however, has a quite unambiguous meaning. In physics, it is the fundamental conception. It is defined by Newton's third law of motion <note>The physical law stating that for every action, there is an equal and opposite reaction.</note>. It is the concept which underlies the principle of conservation wherever there is physical action and reaction. It affirms a perfect equivalence or quantitative equality between every action and its consequent reaction. The principle was originally applied by <term>Descartes<note>René Descartes (1596–1650), the French philosopher and mathematician who proposed that the total quantity of motion in the universe is conserved.</note></term> to movement, which he held to be

<header>THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ</header>
->190<-

...rejection of the <term>scientific postulate<note>The fundamental assumption in physical science that a material world exists independently of our thoughts or observations.</note></term>. The theory of <term>monads<note>In the philosophy of Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716), monads are the simple, indivisible substances that make up the universe. They are more like "souls" or "points of force" than physical atoms.</note></term> is that the conception of a common universe is a consequence of <term>monadic intercourse<note>The intellectual or spiritual communication and relationship between these fundamental units of existence.</note></term>, and not its condition. It is from intercourse that the concept arises, out of intercourse that it takes shape, and not *vice versa*. The monads are not, as they are so often misrepresented, merely private perspectives. It is the monad which is private, not its outlook. The monad has no outlook <note>This refers to Leibniz's famous claim that monads "have no windows" through which anything could come in or go out; everything they experience comes from within themselves.</note>. The monads are the reals and there is no universe except the universe which the monads themselves constitute. 

It is essential that the full significance of this aspect of monadology should be comprehended. It means that there is an alternative to the common-sense and scientific view that there must be a common universe, self-existent, as the pre-condition of mental <margin>?</margin> activity. It is that the external world is an illusion which necessarily arises as a direct consequence of minds communicating. In the monadic theory, for example, <term>space<note>Leibniz argued that space is not a physical "thing," but merely the way we perceive the arrangement of things existing at the same time.</note></term> is for each monad the order of co-existences in its active perception, and <term>time<note>Similarly, Leibniz viewed time as the order of things following one after another, rather than a self-existing timeline.</note></term> the order of successions. There is no need to assume, nor reason to believe in, a space in itself or a time in itself or matter in itself, but if and when intercourse takes place between the monads the idea will naturally arise that the different orders of each monad to which each in its intercourse refers are one order common to all.

<header>THE ALTERNATIVE TO MECHANISM</header>

If the <term>metaphysic<note>A branch of philosophy that deals with the first principles of things, including abstract concepts such as being, substance, cause, and identity.</note></term> of <term>monadism<note>The belief that the universe is made of "monads"—simple, indivisible, soul-like entities—rather than physical matter.</note></term> is consistent and well-founded, then the postulate of an external world is <term>otiose<note>Meaning useless or serving no practical purpose; redundant.</note></term>.

### 4. MONADOLOGY AS AN ALTERNATIVE TO <term>MECHANISM<note>The philosophical view that all natural phenomena can be explained by physical causes and the laws of matter in motion, similar to the workings of a machine.</note></term>

Monadology then is a metaphysic of reality. In challenging the common-sense view it claims to be itself consistent and self-contained in its **integrality**. It does not pretend to harmonize contradictions, what it does is to point to the contradictions of the common sense view as evidence that common sense is concerned with partial aspects of reality. The principle of individuality requires us to conceive the whole in its unity and this unity as indivisible. We may take an organism and disintegrate it, dissecting out what we may choose to consider its constituent parts, and represent these constituents as independent elements of which the whole is the aggregate, but in dividing the unity we lose the reality.

The alternative principle of monadology in regard to the genesis of the idea of an external universe is no longer a principle of philosophical idealism opposed to a principle of positive science. According to the principle of <term>relativity<note>A reference to the then-revolutionary physics of Albert Einstein, which suggested that space and time are not absolute containers but depend on the observer's frame of reference.</note></term> the external world to which we refer in physics is not a discovery and is not a postulate and no scientific advantage is to be gained by assuming its...

### 6. THE IN-ITSELF NATURE OF THE MONAD

The monad then is the concept of the individual philosophically defined in logically precise terms as an indivisible unit centre of activity, self-contained in its actions, expressing in its actions its own nature. To take an explicit example let us consider the individuality of a human being. A human being enters on its separate existence in the form which, as presented to our external perception, we term a fertilized ovum. Internally and in itself it is a heritage of instinctive tendencies and developing potencies. It will unfold and expand its inner force in response to and subject to favourable conditions of environment, yet its whole energy and its directing power are innate and self-contained. How then in fact does such a **solipsistic** <note>From the Latin "solus" (alone) and "ipse" (self); referring to the philosophical challenge that because the monad is "windowless" and self-contained, it must find a way to recognize others outside itself.</note> individual come to recognize other individuals and inter-communicate with them? The usual answer is that it must arrive at this recognition by reasoning on its sense-experience. This is transparent nonsense. Recognition is the outcome of the individual's own power of self-expression and of the sympathetic or antipathetic

The monad, being conceived as activity, must express itself in actions. Actions are innately determined but externally limited. The external limitations can only be perceived from within as self-limitations, but if the limitations are beyond control they will be conceived as alien forces. The limitations to the activity of a monad and the range they allow to its expression in actions give form and outline to the world. The world for the monad is the range of its activity.

If now we conceive this activity as consisting in perception alone, the objects perceived will be the limitations or determinations of its actions reflected back to the perceiving centre. In the **simple monad** there is nothing more. Its activity is pure perception and it is self-contained and self-sufficient.

### 7. THE IMPLICATIONS OF APPERCEPTION OR SELF-CONSCIOUSNESS

The **apperception** <note>The mind's reflexive ability to perceive its own internal states; self-awareness.</note> or self-consciousness of a monad opens to it the possibility of knowing that other monads exist. Let us suppose the possibility realized and the knowledge attained. The crucial problem still remains. How is it possible for a monad to have **intersubjective intercourse** <note>Philosophical "commerce" or communication between two distinct subjects or "selves."</note> with another? The best approach to the problem is to consider how human individuals do actually hold intercourse. We communicate by speech. Speech is an acquirement conditioned by the development in the human organism of a complex neuro-muscular system of articulation, correlated with a sensory organ receptive of sound. Speech or any device for intercourse presupposes therefore that the individuals who would communicate by means of it have already found expression for their **intuitions** <note>In this context, direct, internal perceptions or insights that have not yet been put into words.</note>. When a man makes use of a plastic material <note>Malleable substances like clay, stone, or even sounds that can be shaped into a form.</note> in order to express his artistic intuition, as when he fashions a stone into what for him is the **semblance** <note>The outward appearance or likeness of something.</note> of a man there is nothing absolute in the material object or in the form impressed on it, the stone is simply employed **for this specific purpose** <note>original Latin: "ad hoc"</note> for an expression and a conveyance of meaning. The meaning it expresses to the artist who has fashioned it is conveyed to the man who beholds it only to the extent that the beholder has the creative artistic power to make it express his own intuition. It conveys no meaning to a bird or to a dog, nor has it even the power to deceive them by a semblance. The bird finds it a convenient resting-place, quite undisturbed by the human form into which the artist has fashioned it. Human beings, in short, are able to be responsive to the expressions of one another because they are organized on the same type, confined to the same range of action and identical in their life interests.

The reception of a communication, then, supposes in the receiver a process of artistic creation which originates within him, and the conditions of intercourse <note>In this context, "intercourse" refers to the social and intellectual exchange of ideas between people.</note> are, first that the inter-communicating minds are by their inner nature already attuned to one another, and second that each communicating mind acts creatively for itself. If there is a shared experience between subjects <note>original: "intersubjective intercourse"</note>, nothing more is necessary to account for the origin of the idea of a common world, for such an idea must be its immediate consequence. The existence of a common world as the *cause* of this interaction is impossible to understand, just because knowledge of such existence could only arise as an *effect* of that interaction. The idea of an external world could not arise naturally and spontaneously in a mind cut off from shared communication with others.

**Monadology** is a rational doctrine from beginning to end just because it works without needing assumptions, and is able to reject unproven premises. There is no **gap** <note>original: "lacuna"</note> in its scheme, no stage in its theory which requires a "leap in the dark," an appeal to faith, or an urge toward religious or natural piety. The sympathetic or antipathetic responses we receive from these monads enable intersubjective intercourse <note>Interaction and communication between different individual minds or subjects.</note> to be effective to the extent to which the responding activities are attuned to our own. From intersubjective intercourse arises the concept of the physical world of our daily routine experience.

### 9. PHILOSOPHY AND COMMON SENSE

Is then the external world of common-sense an illusion and are we called upon to disown it and abandon it? It is an illusion in the same sense in which we now believe the stationary earth and moving firmament are illusions. The monadic theory in giving us insight does not change our nature. We may reckon the common sense world as part of our humanity and we need not do violence to our common sense view. We are not expected to stand on our heads because optical science can prove that visual images are reversed on the retina. "The Copernicans," <note>Followers of Nicolaus Copernicus (1473–1543), who proved the Earth rotates and orbits the Sun, contrary to everyday appearances.</note> said Leibniz, "speak with other men of the movement of the" <note>original French: "Les Coperniciens parlent avec les autres hommes du mouvement du..."</note>

# MONADOLOGY VERSUS MATERIALISM

The **materialism** <term>materialism: The philosophical belief that nothing exists except matter and its physical movements.</term> of Epicurus and Lucretius, the **Scholasticism** <term>Scholasticism: A medieval school of philosophy that used logic to reconcile Christian theology with the classical philosophy of Aristotle.</term> of St. Thomas Aquinas, and the scientific **realism** <term>realism: In this context, the belief that the physical world exists exactly as we perceive it, independent of our minds.</term> of Newton and Locke were sufficient when science meant little more than astronomy and mechanics. However, materialism fails completely in the fields of biology and psychology. We are coming to see that the reason for this failure is that the sciences of life and mind introduce us to a realm of reality that is more profound and more fundamental than the one we study in physics.

### 10. MONADOLOGY: A REALISM WHICH IS THE ANTITHESIS OF MATERIALISM

We are bound to admit, however, that from a metaphysical standpoint, **monadology** <term>monadology: The theory developed by Leibniz that the universe is composed of 'monads,' or basic, soul-like units of force that do not have physical parts.</term> labors under a peculiar difficulty—one which has presented itself many times and in diverse forms throughout the history of philosophy. Our only view of the universe is from within. We can never attain the position we are always striving for as a scientific ideal: the vantage point from which we can survey reality with a **disinterested** <note>In a philosophical sense, 'disinterested' does not mean 'bored,' but rather 'objective'—observing without personal bias or a private stake in the outcome.</note> and dissociated contemplation. What we distinguish as external to us is not external in any but a purely relative meaning. We have absolutely nothing but our own **consciousness** <term>consciousness: The state of being aware of and able to think about one's own existence and surroundings.</term> of our own activity, self-centered and self-contained, from which and—

<meta>This page continues from the previous discussion regarding the limitations of common-sense perception (such as our daily observation of the "sun"). It picks up mid-sentence to explain how we use our internal experiences to model the vast, external universe.</meta>

# THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ

...with which to construct our representation of the universe. It seems to us indeed that the reality, the ocean of being which surrounds us, must transcend our finite experience, and yet all we can do is to frame our idea of infinity on this **solipsistic** <term>solipsistic: Relating to the theory that only the self can be known to exist; here, it refers to the challenge of building a view of the vast universe when we are limited to our own internal thoughts.</term> foundation. **Monadology** <term>monadology: The system of philosophy created by Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz, proposing that the universe is made of fundamental, soul-like units called "monads."</term> faces this difficulty, frankly and fearlessly, and instead of recoiling appalled by it, or devising expedients to circumvent it, accepts it. 

The theory of monads is a **realism** <term>realism: The belief that the world exists independently of our senses and is not just a dream or a mental construction.</term> in the true meaning of the term. The monads are reals in the same meaning in which the atoms of **Democritus** <note>Democritus was an Ancient Greek philosopher who was among the first to suggest that the world is composed of tiny, indivisible physical particles called atoms.</note> were reals. The monads are not **ideal** <note>In philosophy, "ideal" refers to things that exist only as ideas in a mind; Leibniz argues his monads are more than just thoughts—they are the actual "stuff" of reality.</note> in the sense that their existence is mind-dependent. They are **things-in-the-selves** <term>things-in-the-selves: A philosophical term for objects as they exist in their own right, regardless of how we perceive them.</term>. At the same time, monadology is the antithesis of **materialism** <term>materialism: The belief that only physical matter exists and that there is no spiritual or mental reality behind it.</term>. The universe of monadology is a living thing and its constituent elements are living things. There is nothing dead, no substratum of lifeless, mindless stuff. 

The monads though self-contained enter into compounds. The **Cartesians** <note>Followers of René Descartes, who famously compared the universe to a vast, clockwork machine governed by the laws of physics.</note> conceived the world as a vast machine which had been set in motion, its large wheels interlinked with and receiving movement from its small wheels, the whole being self-contained. Leibniz conceived the world as a living individual every part of which was also an individual, living its own life and **subserving** <note>Meaning "serving as a subordinate part" or contributing to a larger purpose.</note> by its activity the organic life of the whole.

**Vocabulary:** Monadology, Leibniz, monads, realism, Democritus, things-in-themselves, materialism, Cartesians, organic life.

It contrasts this "living" universe with the clockwork physicalism of the Cartesians and the ancient atomic theory of Democritus.</summary>

## III

# LEIBNIZ AND SPINOZA

**Spinoza** <note>Baruch Spinoza (1632–1677) was a Dutch philosopher of Portuguese-Jewish origin, known for his radical views on God and nature.</note> died in 1677, **Leibniz** <note>Gottfried Wilhelm Leibniz (1646–1716) was a German polymath and philosopher who independently developed calculus and the theory of monads.</note> in 1716, a difference of thirty-nine years. The dates are important, for Leibniz's philosophical writings, those in which his system appears complete and not as a tentative sketch, were composed in the last fifteen years of his life, that is, more than twenty years after Spinoza's death and the **posthumous** <term>posthumous: Occurring or published after the death of the originator.</term> publication of the *Ethics* <note>The *Ethics* is Spinoza's most famous work, written in a style resembling a geometric proof, where he argues that God and Nature are one and the same.</note>.

In 1676, the year before Spinoza's death, Leibniz travelling home to **Hanover** <note>A city in Germany where Leibniz served as a librarian and advisor to the House of Brunswick.</note> from Paris by way of London and Amsterdam, paid a visit to Spinoza, was shewn by him the unpublished manuscript of the *Ethics* and carried away with him some extracts from it. Out of this simple and quite ordinary incident a monstrous fabrication of **calumny** <term>calumny: The making of false and defamatory statements about someone to damage their reputation; slander.</term> has been constructed. The detractors of Leibniz suggest that the main **metaphysical** <term>metaphysical: Relating to the branch of philosophy that deals with the first principles of things, including abstract concepts such as being, knowing, substance, and cause.</term> ideas of his philosophy were borrowed from Spinoza without acknowledgment and that the injustice was concealed by Leibniz's feigned indignation at the blasphemous conclusions laid to Spinoza's charge, a charge from which Leibniz was honourably bound to exonerate him. It is unlikely that any significance in the visit whatever would have been suggested if we had

->### LEIBNIZ AND SPINOZA<-

This doctrine of **causality** <term>causality: The relationship between cause and effect. Spinoza views this as "immanent"—occurring within the nature of a thing—rather than "transient"—an outside force acting upon it.</term> as immanent and not
transient was implicit in **Descartes’s** fundamental

stratum in which the qualities inhere, although
neither **Descartes** himself nor his followers with

**Spinoza** alone realized it in its full comprehension

He draws out its full consequences, which amount

denunciation of final causes as mere sense
deception.

The genius of **Leibniz**, confronted with the same
position, follows an entirely different direction.
**Freedom**, **purpose**, **final end**, **responsibility**,
**individuality**, **personality**, for him are facts. If
they are inconsistent with the concept of
substance and contradict its definition, then it is
the concept of substance which calls for re-form-
ation and new definition. He realizes with all the
force of **Spinoza** that the static concept of substance
makes creation impossible, negates the idea of it.
Accordingly he rejects the static concept of sub-
stance. He reforms the whole notion. It is not

<meta>This page continues the comparison between Spinoza and Leibniz, moving from the rejection of final causes to Leibniz’s redefinition of substance as dynamic force. It establishes the core conflict between Spinoza's single-substance determinism and Leibniz's pluralistic system of individual monads.</meta>

<header>THE MONADOLOGY OF LEIBNIZ</header>
<page-num>210</page-num>

movement but **force** <term>force: For Leibniz, this is the inherent capacity for action—a dynamic power that makes reality more than just static matter.</term> which we are to regard as indestructible. This was a new idea. **Substance** is activity. It is not an inert substratum in which attributes inhere, to which qualities are attached. The idea of **substance** is replaced by the idea of **subject**. A thing is what it does. **Substance** is not necessarily one, it is many, for every subject of experience expressing its nature in outward action is a substance. It is in this new concept that the contrast appears between the **modes** <note>modes: In Spinoza’s system, these are individual things (like people or objects) which are merely temporary expressions or "ripples" in the one single substance of God or Nature.</note> of **Spinoza** and the **monads** <term>monads: The fundamental, indivisible units of reality in Leibniz’s philosophy, acting as individual centers of force or consciousness.</term> of **Leibniz**. So far from expressing the same thought in different ways, it is the same fact interpreted in directly opposite ways.

**Spinoza's** **determinism** <term>determinism: The philosophical view that all events, including human actions, are determined by preceding causes and the laws of nature.</term> is not **mechanism** in the modern scientific meaning and **Leibniz's** **libertarianism** <term>libertarianism: The belief in human free will and the capacity for individuals to be the true source of their own actions.</term> is not **finalism** in the ordinary **teleological** <term>teleological: Explaining phenomena by the purpose or "end" they serve, rather than just their physical causes.</term> meaning. Both philosophers interpret **causality** as the expression in outward action or in movement of an inherent nature. Yet the difference is profound and the opposition complete. It turns on the principle of **individuality**. We are far even today from a final and satisfactory solution. So far as ordinary opinion is concerned, common sense, always shallow and superficial, contents itself with a compromise. Rigid theoretical necessity in the laboratory; purpose, responsibility and freedom in the practical sphere; seem easily reconcilable, and hence the profound

<summary>This page explains Leibniz’s shift from viewing substance as a static base to viewing it as active "force," contrasting his individual monads with Spinoza's modes. It highlights the fundamental philosophical tension between a determined universe and the preservation of individuality and freedom.</summary>
<keywords>Leibniz, Monadology, substance, force, Spinoza, monads, determinism, libertarianism, causality, individuality</keywords>

### LEIBNIZ AND SPINOZA
211

...difference between the two principles, **Spinoza’s** and **Leibniz’s**, usually passes unnoticed.

In many respects **Spinoza’s** principle appears the more satisfactory. It follows the general trend of rational speculation and it seems to accord with the intellectual bias of the human mind. **Individuality** appears to **Spinoza** as the time aspect of an eternal being. The **modes** <term>modes: In Spinoza’s system, these are individual things—like a specific person, a tree, or a thought—which are not independent entities but temporary "ripples" or expressions of the one single Substance (God or Nature).</term> are the transient forms which the unchanging reality assumes to the onlooker. Like the waves of the sea they present an aspect of ceaseless change and yet they are no more than the momentary forms of the changeless ocean. There can be no real individuality if individuality means dividing the **substance** <note>substance: The fundamental reality that exists in itself. For Spinoza, there is only one; for Leibniz, reality is made of many individual substances.</note>. **Leibniz** on the other hand approaches the problem from a different angle. Suppose, he puts the question to himself, that **God**, infinite in knowledge and power, wills to create free spirits, what are the conditions of the possibility of such a creation? The answer is the system of the **pre-established harmony** <term>pre-established harmony: Leibniz’s theory that God programmed all substances (monads) at the moment of creation to be perfectly synchronized with one another, like two clocks kept in perfect time, creating the appearance of interaction without actual physical influence.</term>. From it follows a new concept of **God**, a new concept of man, and a new concept of man’s relation to **God**. **God** may now be conceived under the category of **personality** <note>personality: This suggests that God is a conscious, moral being with a will, rather than just an abstract or mechanical force of nature.</note>. He becomes the ruler of free spirits. We have a philosophy of freedom, of creation, of invention.

In the history of philosophy **Spinoza** and **Leibniz** appear together. They differ pro-

<vocab>**Spinoza**, **Leibniz**, **Individuality**, **modes**, **substance**, **pre-established harmony**, **God**, **free spirits**, **philosophy of freedom**</vocab>

<keywords>Spinoza, Leibniz, individuality, substance, pre-established harmony, free will, divine personality, rationalism</keywords>
